# Topic catalogue

All 557 topics, grouped by strand and domain. Full records, including mastery evidence, assessment prompts and teaching guidance, are in `data/`.

## Academic Language

### Academic Vocabulary

Academic Vocabulary covers the lexical platform for university study: the vocabulary size and text coverage needed to read and listen independently; the general academic, sub-technical and technical layers of university lexis; the word-level knowledge (morphology, word grammar, countability, spelling) needed for accurate production; and the strategies learners use to keep expanding vocabulary beyond the classroom. Academic word lists (AWL, AVL) are treated as useful but contested starting points, because the 'academic' status, meaning and frequency of items vary by discipline, and coverage thresholds are treated as approximate planning guides rather than cut-offs.

<a id="vochigh-frequency-base"></a>
#### High-frequency vocabulary base

`voc.high-frequency-base` · LINGUISTIC · A2–B1+ · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Know, receptively and productively, the most frequent 2,000–3,000 word families of English, with fast, accurate recognition in reading and listening. Because these words make up the large majority of running words in any text, academic texts included, gaps at this level undermine comprehension more than gaps in specialised vocabulary.

<a id="voclexical-coverage"></a>
#### Lexical coverage and vocabulary size

`voc.lexical-coverage` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Understand that comprehension depends strongly on the proportion of running words in a text that the reader or listener knows — commonly cited as around 95% for minimal and 98% for comfortable unassisted reading — and use this relationship, with a vocabulary size estimate, to set realistic goals and choose texts. Coverage figures are approximate: comprehension rises gradually with coverage rather than switching on at a threshold, and background knowledge also matters.

Prerequisites: [High-frequency vocabulary base](#vochigh-frequency-base) (soft)

<a id="vocword-families-morphology"></a>
#### Word families and derivational morphology

`voc.word-families-morphology` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Recognise and produce the word-class members of a word family (analyse, analysis, analyst, analytical, analytically) and choose the form a sentence slot requires. Control of derivational morphology multiplies the value of each word learned and prevents a frequent class of errors in academic writing, where nominal and adjectival forms predominate.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft)

<a id="vocaffixes-roots"></a>
#### Academic affixes and Greco-Latin roots

`voc.affixes-roots` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use common academic prefixes (inter-, multi-, sub-, pre-, re-, non-), suffixes (-tion, -ity, -ise/-ize, -ology, -al) and Greco-Latin roots (bio-, geo-, -graph-, -cracy) to infer and remember the meanings of unfamiliar words, while checking inferences, because word parts can mislead (e.g. 'inflammable', 'discursive').

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology)

<a id="voccore-academic-vocabulary"></a>
#### Core academic vocabulary

`voc.core-academic-vocabulary` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise and use general academic vocabulary — words such as 'analyse', 'concept', 'significant', 'factor', 'establish' — that recurs across many disciplines and is more frequent in academic than general texts, using lists such as the Academic Word List (AWL) or Academic Vocabulary List (AVL) as starting points while recognising that the frequency, meaning and collocation of listed items vary considerably by discipline.

Prerequisites: [High-frequency vocabulary base](#vochigh-frequency-base), [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology) (soft)

<a id="vocacademic-verbs"></a>
#### Verbs for academic and research processes

`voc.academic-verbs` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Control verbs that describe research and reasoning processes — 'investigate', 'examine', 'demonstrate', 'indicate', 'suggest', 'constitute', 'derive', 'attribute' — including their meaning, the strength of claim they convey and their complementation patterns ('attribute X to Y', 'indicate that', 'derive from', 'account for'). These verbs carry much of the propositional and evaluative work of academic writing.

Prerequisites: [Core academic vocabulary](#voccore-academic-vocabulary), [Word grammar and complementation patterns](#vocword-grammar-patterns)

<a id="vocabstract-shell-nouns"></a>
#### Abstract and shell nouns

`voc.abstract-shell-nouns` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use abstract nouns ('approach', 'issue', 'factor', 'notion') and shell nouns ('this problem', 'the fact that', 'the claim that', 'the aim is to') that label and encapsulate stretches of text, so that writers can refer back to and characterise ideas, build cohesion and signal stance through the label chosen (a 'finding' versus an 'assumption').

Prerequisites: [Core academic vocabulary](#voccore-academic-vocabulary), [Word grammar and complementation patterns](#vocword-grammar-patterns) (soft)

<a id="voctechnical-terminology"></a>
#### Technical and discipline-specific terminology

`voc.technical-terminology` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Learn field-specific terms as labels for concepts within a disciplinary knowledge structure, understand their precise definitions and relations to other terms, define them on first use where readers may need it, and use them consistently. Technical vocabulary can make up a substantial share of the words in specialised texts, and learning it is inseparable from learning disciplinary content.

Prerequisites: [Academic affixes and Greco-Latin roots](#vocaffixes-roots) (soft), [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge) (soft), [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities) (soft)

<a id="vocsubtechnical-polysemy"></a>
#### Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy

`voc.subtechnical-polysemy` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Recognise that many general and academic words take specialised meanings in particular disciplines ('significant' in statistics, 'culture' in biology, 'power' in physics and politics, 'tissue', 'consistent', 'discourse'), and interpret and use the intended sense. Misreading such words is a hidden source of comprehension failure because learners believe they already know them.

Prerequisites: [Core academic vocabulary](#voccore-academic-vocabulary), [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Inferring word meaning from context](#vocinferring-from-context) (soft)

<a id="vocprecision-near-synonyms"></a>
#### Lexical precision and near-synonyms

`voc.precision-near-synonyms` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose between near-synonyms (affect/effect/impact/influence; show/prove/suggest; method/methodology; increase/rise/growth) by meaning, strength, grammar, collocation and register, so that the chosen word states exactly what the writer intends. Imprecise word choice is a frequent reason markers judge writing unclear or overclaimed.

Prerequisites: [Core academic vocabulary](#voccore-academic-vocabulary), [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness) (soft)

<a id="vocevaluative-lexis"></a>
#### Evaluative vocabulary

`voc.evaluative-lexis` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use adjectives, adverbs, nouns and verbs that evaluate ideas, evidence and work ('robust', 'compelling', 'limited', 'flawed', 'a shortcoming', 'a strength', 'overlooks', 'convincingly') with appropriate strength and polarity, so that evaluation is precise, justified and suitably mitigated. Evaluative lexis is central to critical writing, where markers expect judgement rather than description.

Prerequisites: [Lexical precision and near-synonyms](#vocprecision-near-synonyms), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation) (soft)

<a id="vocmultiword-verbs-register"></a>
#### Multi-word verbs and register

`voc.multiword-verbs-register` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Understand phrasal and prepositional verbs ('carry out', 'point out', 'set up', 'come up with', 'look into') in lectures, seminars and texts, and judge in writing when a single-word alternative ('conduct', 'note', 'establish') is more conventional and when the multi-word verb is the normal academic choice ('carry out', 'consist of', 'rule out'). The aim is register judgement, not avoidance: blanket replacement produces stilted prose.

Prerequisites: [Word grammar and complementation patterns](#vocword-grammar-patterns) (soft), [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features)

<a id="vocinferring-from-context"></a>
#### Inferring word meaning from context

`voc.inferring-from-context` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Infer the meaning of unknown words while reading or listening by using co-text (definitions, examples, contrasts, appositives), word parts and background knowledge, and check the inference against later context or a dictionary when the word matters. Successful inferencing depends on knowing most of the surrounding words, so it complements rather than replaces vocabulary learning.

Prerequisites: [Lexical coverage and vocabulary size](#voclexical-coverage) (soft), [Academic affixes and Greco-Latin roots](#vocaffixes-roots) (soft), [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="vocdictionary-use"></a>
#### Using learner and specialist dictionaries

`voc.dictionary-use` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use monolingual learner dictionaries, collocation dictionaries, specialist glossaries and bilingual or bilingualised dictionaries effectively — finding the right entry and sense, and using grammar codes, examples, frequency and register labels — while recognising the risks of taking the first bilingual equivalent or treating machine-translation output as a dictionary.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology) (soft)

<a id="vocdeliberate-learning-systems"></a>
#### Deliberate vocabulary learning systems

`voc.deliberate-learning-systems` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Set up and maintain a system for deliberately learning selected words — paper word cards or spaced-retrieval software recording form, meaning, collocates and an example — and review by retrieval at expanding intervals. Deliberate learning efficiently establishes form–meaning links for priority vocabulary and complements the large amount of exposure needed for depth.

Prerequisites: [Lexical coverage and vocabulary size](#voclexical-coverage) (soft), [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques) (soft)

<a id="vocreceptive-to-productive"></a>
#### Moving vocabulary into productive use

`voc.receptive-to-productive` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Develop depth of knowledge of selected words — form, meaning, grammar, collocation, register and constraints on use — so that words recognised in reading and listening can be used accurately in speech and writing, and trial new words in low-stakes production with feedback before relying on them in assessed work.

Prerequisites: [Deliberate vocabulary learning systems](#vocdeliberate-learning-systems) (soft), [Word grammar and complementation patterns](#vocword-grammar-patterns), [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness) (soft)

<a id="vocword-grammar-patterns"></a>
#### Word grammar and complementation patterns

`voc.word-grammar-patterns` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Know the grammatical patterns that individual verbs, nouns and adjectives take — dependent prepositions ('focus on', 'contribute to', 'an increase in', 'consistent with'), clause complements ('emphasise that', 'aim to', 'an attempt to') and object patterns — and use them accurately. Many errors perceived as 'grammar' in academic writing are word-specific pattern errors.

Prerequisites: [High-frequency vocabulary base](#vochigh-frequency-base) (soft), [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements)

<a id="voccountability-academic-nouns"></a>
#### Countability of academic nouns

`voc.countability-academic-nouns` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Handle the countability of nouns frequent in academic writing: uncountable nouns whose equivalents are countable in many other languages ('research', 'evidence', 'information', 'knowledge', 'equipment', 'feedback'), nouns with countable and uncountable senses ('theory', 'experience', 'work'), irregular plurals ('criterion/criteria', 'phenomenon/phenomena', 'hypothesis/hypotheses') and the variable treatment of 'data'. Countability errors propagate to articles, quantifiers and agreement.

Prerequisites: [Core academic vocabulary](#voccore-academic-vocabulary) (soft), [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft)

<a id="vocspelling-conventions"></a>
#### Spelling conventions and consistency

`voc.spelling-conventions` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Spell academic vocabulary accurately and consistently: follow one variety (e.g. British or American) or whatever a style guide requires within a text, manage variable spellings such as -ise/-ize, and avoid frequently confused forms (principle/principal, affect/effect, dependent/dependant, licence/license, its/it's). Inconsistent or inaccurate spelling distracts readers and is penalised in many marking criteria.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology) (soft)

<a id="vocuniversity-terminology"></a>
#### Vocabulary of university study

`voc.university-terminology` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand and use the vocabulary of university study and administration — module, credit, seminar, tutorial, assessment brief, learning outcome, formative and summative assessment, extension, extenuating (mitigating) circumstances, personal tutor, VLE — including institution-specific variants. Misunderstanding these terms can lead to missed deadlines, misdirected requests and lost marks.

Prerequisites: [Teaching and learning formats](#acuteaching-formats) (soft)

<a id="voccommand-words"></a>
#### Assignment command words

`voc.command-words` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Interpret assignment and examination command words — analyse, evaluate, discuss, critically assess, compare, outline, examine, explain, to what extent — in terms of the cognitive operations and text structures they typically require, while recognising that tutors use them inconsistently and that the whole task, criteria and context must be read together.

Prerequisites: [Verbs for academic and research processes](#vocacademic-verbs) (soft), [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft)

<a id="vocabbreviations-latin"></a>
#### Academic abbreviations and Latin expressions

`voc.abbreviations-latin` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Understand and use common academic abbreviations and Latin expressions (e.g., i.e., et al., cf., etc., vs., viz., ibid., n.d., ca.) correctly in reading and writing, following the conventions of the referencing or style guide in use, and introduce acronyms and initialisms in full on first use.

Prerequisites: [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft)

### Phraseology & Formulaic Language

Phraseology & Formulaic Language treats multi-word units — collocations, lexical bundles, spoken formulas and fixed expressions — as core carriers of academic meaning rather than decorative extras. Corpus research shows that expert academic writing and speech rely heavily on recurrent combinations whose functions differ (research-, text- and stance-oriented) and vary by discipline, and that L2 and novice writers tend to underuse, misuse or overuse them. The domain also develops strategies (corpus consultation, critical use of phrase resources) so that learners can verify phraseology independently, while guarding against formulaic, template-driven text.

<a id="phrcollocation-awareness"></a>
#### Collocation awareness

`phr.collocation-awareness` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Understand that words habitually co-occur with particular other words (collocation) and that many meanings are carried by multi-word units rather than single words, and notice such units when reading and listening. This awareness is the basis for learning vocabulary in chunks, for natural-sounding production and for using collocation resources.

Prerequisites: [High-frequency vocabulary base](#vochigh-frequency-base) (soft)

<a id="phracademic-collocations"></a>
#### Academic collocations

`phr.academic-collocations` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise and use frequent academic collocations — verb–noun ('conduct research', 'draw a conclusion', 'raise a question'), adjective–noun ('a significant difference', 'empirical evidence') and noun–preposition combinations ('an increase in', 'the effect on') — so that writing and speech express academic meanings in conventional, readily processed forms.

Prerequisites: [Core academic vocabulary](#voccore-academic-vocabulary), [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness)

<a id="phrverb-noun-collocation-accuracy"></a>
#### Verb–noun collocation accuracy

`phr.verb-noun-collocation-accuracy` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Avoid and repair verb–noun miscollocations that arise when learners transfer combinations from another language or overextend general delexical verbs (e.g. 'make research', 'do a mistake', 'rise a question'), by identifying their own recurrent errors and checking combinations with collocation dictionaries or corpora. Verb–noun errors are among the most persistent lexical errors even in advanced learner writing.

Prerequisites: [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Academic collocations](#phracademic-collocations)

<a id="phrbundles-research-oriented"></a>
#### Research-oriented lexical bundles

`phr.bundles-research-oriented` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise and use research-oriented lexical bundles — recurrent word sequences that structure content about the research world, such as location, procedure, quantification and description ('the extent to which', 'in the case of', 'the nature of the', 'at the same time', 'on the basis of') — together with their typical grammatical continuations. These bundles are frequent in expert writing, vary by discipline and help writers express relations between concepts economically.

Prerequisites: [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness), [Noun-phrase postmodification](#grmnp-postmodification) (soft)

<a id="phrbundles-text-oriented"></a>
#### Text-oriented lexical bundles

`phr.bundles-text-oriented` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use text-oriented bundles that organise discourse and signal relations between ideas — transition ('on the other hand', 'in addition to the'), result ('as a result of'), structuring ('in the next section') and framing ('in terms of the', 'with respect to the') — with accurate meaning and grammar and without overuse. These bundles support cohesion and help readers navigate a text.

Prerequisites: [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft)

<a id="phrbundles-stance"></a>
#### Stance and engagement bundles

`phr.bundles-stance` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use stance and engagement bundles — largely impersonal frames expressing likelihood, importance, necessity or evidentiality ('it is possible that', 'it is important to', 'it should be noted that', 'as can be seen') — to position claims and guide readers, matching the frame's strength to the evidence and avoiding formulaic overuse.

Prerequisites: [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness), [Anticipatory 'it' and extraposition](#grmanticipatory-it)

<a id="phrspoken-academic-formulas"></a>
#### Spoken academic formulas

`phr.spoken-academic-formulas` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise and use formulas typical of lectures, seminars and presentations ('what I want to do is', 'if you look at', 'the point is', 'I'd like to come back to') that organise talk, focus attention and manage interaction. Recognising them supports lecture comprehension; producing them supports fluent, well-structured speaking.

Prerequisites: [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness), [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft)

<a id="phrfixed-expressions"></a>
#### Fixed expressions, idioms and binomials

`phr.fixed-expressions` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise idioms, binomials and other relatively fixed expressions ('pros and cons', 'by and large', 'trial and error', 'to a greater or lesser extent', 'in the long run') in academic texts and lectures and judge their register, using them in writing only where they are conventional and avoiding conversational idioms in formal prose.

Prerequisites: [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness), [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft)

<a id="phrcorpus-consultation"></a>
#### Consulting corpora for usage

`phr.corpus-consultation` · STRATEGIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use concordancers and academic corpora, including small self-built corpora, to check collocation, frequency, grammatical patterns and register while writing — a data-driven learning approach that lets learners answer their own usage questions and interpret the evidence critically.

Prerequisites: [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness)

<a id="phrphrasebank-use"></a>
#### Using phrasebanks critically

`phr.phrasebank-use` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use function-organised phrase resources (academic phrasebanks, formula lists) to find conventional language for rhetorical moves — introducing a topic, reporting findings, acknowledging limitations — while adapting phrases to one's own content and avoiding formulaic, template-driven text in which phrases substitute for ideas.

Prerequisites: [Text-oriented lexical bundles](#phrbundles-text-oriented) (soft), [Stance and engagement bundles](#phrbundles-stance) (soft), [Genre and discourse community](#gesgenre-concept) (soft)

### Register & Academic Style

Register & Academic Style develops learners' ability to read a communicative situation and choose language accordingly: register as a configuration of field, tenor and mode; the systematic differences in how speech and writing package meaning; and the features conventionally associated with formal academic prose. The domain deliberately treats style 'rules' as probabilistic tendencies to be tested against evidence of disciplinary and historical variation, frames impersonality as a choice about agency rather than a route to objectivity, and extends to inclusive language and to writing for non-specialist readers.

<a id="regregister-awareness"></a>
#### Register and situational variation

`reg.register-awareness` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Recognise that language varies systematically with the situation of use — the activity and subject matter (field), the relationship between participants (tenor) and the channel and role of language (mode) — and that formality is a continuum rather than a binary. This lets learners explain why a seminar contribution, a lab report and an email to a tutor are worded differently, and adjust their own language accordingly.

Prerequisites: [Teaching and learning formats](#acuteaching-formats) (soft)

<a id="regspoken-vs-written"></a>
#### Spoken versus written academic language

`reg.spoken-vs-written` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand how academic writing and academic speech package meaning differently: writing tends towards high lexical density and compressed noun phrases, whereas speech distributes meaning across chains of clauses, with repetition, discourse markers and direct address. Learners use this to unpack dense prose, follow lectures, and avoid carrying conversational clause-chaining into essays or essay-style density into presentations.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness), [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft)

<a id="regformality-features"></a>
#### Features of formal academic style

`reg.formality-features` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Control the features conventionally associated with formal academic writing — avoiding contractions, colloquial vocabulary and conversational intensifiers, using direct questions and second-person address sparingly, and choosing pronouns deliberately — while understanding these as probabilistic tendencies that vary by genre and discipline rather than absolute rules. Control of these features lets writers meet reader expectations without adopting a stilted, over-formal voice.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness)

<a id="regstyle-myths-variation"></a>
#### Myths and change in academic style

`reg.style-myths-variation` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Evaluate prescriptive style 'rules' — never use I, never start a sentence with And or But, never split an infinitive, always prefer the passive — against evidence of what expert writers in a field actually do and how practice is changing. This replaces rule-following with informed choice and helps learners read assignment and journal guidance in the light of disciplinary norms.

Prerequisites: [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features)

<a id="regimpersonality-presence"></a>
#### Impersonality and authorial presence

`reg.impersonality-presence` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose between impersonal constructions (agentless passive, anticipatory 'it', inanimate subjects such as 'this study') and visible authorial presence (first-person pronouns, explicit evaluation) according to discipline, genre and rhetorical purpose. Understanding that impersonal forms background agency rather than make claims objective lets writers decide where to foreground their own role, for example in methodological decisions or claims of contribution.

Prerequisites: [Myths and change in academic style](#regstyle-myths-variation) (soft), [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice)

<a id="reginformation-density"></a>
#### Information density and readability

`reg.information-density` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Balance the compressed phrasing typical of academic writing — nominalisation, long noun phrases, noun + noun sequences — against the intended reader's need to process the text, unpacking where density hides agency, causality or reference. Writers who control density can condense content for expert readers and loosen it for novices or mixed audiences.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written), [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation)

<a id="regvague-overgeneralised"></a>
#### Avoiding vague and overgeneralised language

`reg.vague-overgeneralised` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Replace vague nouns and quantifiers ('things', 'stuff', 'a lot of', 'many aspects'), unbounded time references ('nowadays', 'since the dawn of time') and sweeping claims ('everyone knows', 'always', 'in all countries') with precise reference and bounded claims that could be supported. Vagueness and overgeneralisation weaken credibility because readers cannot tell exactly what is claimed or check it against evidence.

Prerequisites: [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft), [Determiners and quantifiers](#grmdeterminers-quantifiers) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="reginclusive-language"></a>
#### Inclusive and bias-free language

`reg.inclusive-language` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Refer to people accurately and respectfully: use gender-neutral wording (including singular 'they'), specific and current terms for groups, and person-first or identity-first language according to the conventions of the field and the preferences of the communities concerned. Inclusive language is now expected by most style guides, journals and ethics processes, and it usually improves precision as well as respect.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Pronoun and demonstrative reference](#grmreference-pronouns) (soft)

<a id="regnon-specialist-audiences"></a>
#### Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences

`reg.non-specialist-audiences` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Adapt specialist content for readers or listeners outside the field — lay summaries, participant information, public talks, blog posts — by selecting what matters to that audience, replacing or glossing technical terms, reducing density, using concrete examples, and keeping the expression of certainty faithful to the evidence. Students and researchers are increasingly required to communicate this way for ethics, funding, impact and engagement.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Information density and readability](#reginformation-density), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft)

<a id="regdisciplinary-style"></a>
#### Disciplinary variation in style

`reg.disciplinary-style` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Understand how disciplines differ in style — frequency of first person, passive voice, hedging and boosting, citation forms and the way arguments are built — and relate these differences to how fields create and validate knowledge (e.g. hard/soft and pure/applied tendencies). This lets learners interpret conflicting advice from different modules and align their writing with their target discipline.

Prerequisites: [Impersonality and authorial presence](#regimpersonality-presence), [Metadiscourse](#stametadiscourse) (soft), [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities) (soft)

### Academic Grammar

This domain covers the grammatical resources that carry meaning in academic registers, prioritised by what corpus research shows academic writing actually does rather than by a general-English syllabus order. Its centre of gravity is the noun phrase: academic prose compresses information into premodified and postmodified noun phrases and nominalisations far more than conversation does, so learners need both to unpack such phrases when reading and to build them when writing. Clause-level resources (tense, voice, modality, subordination, complement and reporting clauses) are treated in terms of the rhetorical choices they serve, such as currency in citation, agency in methods and degrees of commitment in claims. Accuracy topics are framed as monitoring and editing problems as much as knowledge problems.

<a id="grmclause-elements"></a>
#### Clause elements and sentence analysis

`grm.clause-elements` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Identify the subject, verb, object, complement and adverbial of a clause, including when each is realised by a long phrase or an embedded clause, and use this analysis to find the core of dense academic sentences and to build complete ones. Clause analysis supplies the working metalanguage on which later grammar, punctuation, reading and self-editing work depends.

<a id="grmsubject-verb-agreement"></a>
#### Subject–verb agreement with complex subjects

`grm.subject-verb-agreement` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Make finite verbs agree with the head of the subject when modifiers separate the two, and handle academic cases where number is not transparent: 'the number of' versus 'a number of', proportion and quantity expressions, coordinated subjects, collective nouns and nouns such as data, criteria and research. Agreement slips are highly visible to markers and editors even when meaning is clear.

Prerequisites: [Countability of academic nouns](#voccountability-academic-nouns) (soft), [Noun-phrase postmodification](#grmnp-postmodification)

<a id="grmnp-premodification"></a>
#### Noun-phrase premodification

`grm.np-premodification` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Build and interpret noun phrases with adjectives, participles and nouns before the head (a carefully designed longitudinal study; carbon emission reduction targets), including the order of premodifiers and the implicit meaning relations in noun + noun sequences. Premodification is a major and growing resource for compression in academic writing, and it is a frequent source of both comprehension difficulty and ambiguity.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology)

<a id="grmnp-postmodification"></a>
#### Noun-phrase postmodification

`grm.np-postmodification` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use and parse the structures that follow a head noun: prepositional phrases (by far the most frequent postmodifier in academic prose), relative clauses, non-finite clauses and appositive noun phrases or clauses. Postmodified noun phrases let writers specify and qualify referents precisely, and they produce the long subjects that L2 readers and writers find hardest to process.

Prerequisites: [Defining and non-defining relative clauses](#grmrelative-clauses) (soft), [Prepositions and complex prepositions](#grmprepositions) (soft)

<a id="grmnominalisation"></a>
#### Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor

`grm.nominalisation` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Express processes and qualities as nouns (X increased sharply → the sharp increase in X) so that they can be classified, evaluated, made the subject of further clauses and used to carry given information forward, and unpack nominalisations when reading. Writers also need to recognise the costs: nominal style can hide agency and causal relations and can make prose harder to process than necessary.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology), [Noun-phrase postmodification](#grmnp-postmodification)

<a id="grmphrasal-complexity"></a>
#### Phrasal versus clausal complexity

`grm.phrasal-complexity` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand that academic writing is complex mainly through compressed phrases (noun phrases with premodifiers and prepositional postmodifiers, nominalisations), whereas conversation elaborates through dependent clauses, and use this understanding to read dense prose and to set realistic development goals. The insight corrects the widespread belief that 'academic' means long sentences with many subordinate clauses.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Noun-phrase premodification](#grmnp-premodification), [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation)

<a id="grmarticles"></a>
#### Articles in academic writing

`grm.articles` · LINGUISTIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose between a/an, the and zero article according to countability, specific versus generic reference, first versus subsequent or otherwise identifiable mention, and postmodification (the role of X; zero article with uncountable and plural generic nouns). Article choices encode whether a reader can identify a referent and whether a claim is general or specific, so they matter for meaning as well as accuracy.

Prerequisites: [Countability of academic nouns](#voccountability-academic-nouns), [Noun-phrase postmodification](#grmnp-postmodification) (soft)

<a id="grmdeterminers-quantifiers"></a>
#### Determiners and quantifiers

`grm.determiners-quantifiers` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Select determiners and quantifiers that match the countability of the noun and the precision the claim needs: few versus a few, much versus many, less versus fewer, each versus every, a number of, the majority of, a proportion of. Quantifiers carry evaluative and hedging meaning (few studies versus a few studies), so choices affect the claim as well as accuracy.

Prerequisites: [Countability of academic nouns](#voccountability-academic-nouns)

<a id="grmtense-aspect"></a>
#### Tense and aspect in academic writing

`grm.tense-aspect` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use present simple for generalisations, definitions and the content of texts and figures; past simple for completed procedures and specific past studies or events; and present perfect for developments whose relevance continues into the present state of a field. Academic prose relies overwhelmingly on the simple present, with perfect and progressive aspects comparatively rare, so the skill is choosing the marked forms purposefully and keeping tense consistent within a section.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft)

<a id="grmtense-in-reporting"></a>
#### Tense when reporting research

`grm.tense-in-reporting` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose tense in citing and reporting sentences to signal how the writer positions the cited work: past for a specific study's actions or findings, present perfect for an area of continuing activity, and present for claims the writer treats as currently valid or for what a source argues. Tense correlates with citation form (integral versus non-integral) and reporting-verb choice, and it varies by discipline.

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect), [Integral and non-integral citation](#srccitation-forms) (soft)

<a id="grmpassive-voice"></a>
#### Passive voice and agency

`grm.passive-voice` · LINGUISTIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Form passives across tenses and with modals, and choose between active and passive on functional grounds: keeping given information or the topic in subject position, omitting an agent that is unknown, obvious or irrelevant, and foregrounding processes in methods. The passive is common in academic prose, but it is not inherently 'objective'; writers also need to know when an active verb with a human or 'we' subject is clearer or expected, and how passives interact with dangling modifiers.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements), [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect)

<a id="grmepistemic-modality"></a>
#### Epistemic modal verbs

`grm.epistemic-modality` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use and interpret may, might, could, would, should and must to express degrees of likelihood and inference, including modal + perfect for past inferences (may have contributed) and modal + passive (could be explained by). Modal verbs are a core grammatical resource for hedging and inference, and learners need to separate their epistemic meanings from obligation and ability meanings.

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect) (soft)

<a id="grmconditionals"></a>
#### Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning

`grm.conditionals` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use real and unreal conditionals and conditional subordinators (if, unless, provided that, assuming that, as long as) to state the conditions under which a claim holds, to reason about hypothetical cases and counterfactuals, and to describe dependencies in methods and models. In academic writing, conditionals do much of the work of limiting and qualifying claims rather than describing imaginary situations.

Prerequisites: [Epistemic modal verbs](#grmepistemic-modality), [Subordination and adverbial clauses](#grmsubordination)

<a id="grmrelative-clauses"></a>
#### Defining and non-defining relative clauses

`grm.relative-clauses` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Form and punctuate defining and non-defining relative clauses with which, that, who, whom, whose and preposition + which/whom (the extent to which, the conditions under which), and recognise reduced relatives. The defining/non-defining distinction changes what a sentence claims about a set of referents, so commas here carry meaning, not just style.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements)

<a id="grmnon-finite-clauses"></a>
#### Non-finite clauses

`grm.non-finite-clauses` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use -ing, -ed and to-infinitive clauses as postmodifiers (the factors affecting uptake; the data collected in 2020), adverbials of purpose, means and result (To test this, …; thereby reducing …) and complements, so that information can be added without a full finite clause. Because non-finite clauses have no expressed subject, writers must ensure the implied subject is recoverable and avoid dangling participles.

Prerequisites: [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice) (soft), [Defining and non-defining relative clauses](#grmrelative-clauses) (soft), [Subordination and adverbial clauses](#grmsubordination) (soft)

<a id="grmsubordination"></a>
#### Subordination and adverbial clauses

`grm.subordination` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Combine clauses with subordinators that express reason, concession, contrast, condition, purpose and time (because, since, although, whereas, while, so that, once), placing the subordinate clause before or after the main clause according to information flow. Subordinators make logical relations explicit within the sentence and are central to concession and explanation, even though academic prose relies less on finite adverbial clauses than conversation does.

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect) (soft), [Simple, compound and complex sentences](#sensentence-types)

<a id="grmcoordination-parallelism"></a>
#### Coordination and parallel structure

`grm.coordination-parallelism` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Coordinate words, phrases and clauses with and, but, or, nor and correlative pairs (both … and, either … or, not only … but also) so that coordinated elements share the same grammatical form, especially in lists, comparisons and aims or research questions. Faulty parallelism makes relations between ideas harder to follow, and phrasal coordination is itself a resource for compression in academic writing.

Prerequisites: [Simple, compound and complex sentences](#sensentence-types)

<a id="grmcomplement-clauses"></a>
#### That-clauses and wh-clauses

`grm.complement-clauses` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use that-clauses and wh-/whether-clauses as noun clauses after verbs (suggest that, examine whether), adjectives (clear that, unclear how) and nouns (the claim that, the question of whether), and as subjects. Complement clauses are the main grammatical frame for reporting and evaluating propositions, and the noun + that-clause pattern is a characteristic way of labelling claims in academic prose.

Prerequisites: [Defining and non-defining relative clauses](#grmrelative-clauses) (soft), [Simple, compound and complex sentences](#sensentence-types)

<a id="grmanticipatory-it"></a>
#### Anticipatory 'it' and extraposition

`grm.anticipatory-it` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use anticipatory it with an extraposed that-, to- or wh-clause (it is important to note that; it has been argued that; it remains unclear whether) to place a heavy clause at the end of the sentence and to express stance without self-mention. These frames are frequent in academic writing, but they can also add empty words and obscure who holds a view.

Prerequisites: [Non-finite clauses](#grmnon-finite-clauses) (soft), [That-clauses and wh-clauses](#grmcomplement-clauses)

<a id="grmexistential-there"></a>
#### Existential 'there' constructions

`grm.existential-there` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use existential there + be to introduce new entities, evidence or developments (there is evidence that; there has been a shift towards; there are three reasons for), with agreement controlled by the following noun phrase. The structure presents new information at the end of the clause, and a frequent learner error is to follow it directly with a finite verb (*There are many people think…).

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect) (soft), [Defining and non-defining relative clauses](#grmrelative-clauses) (soft)

<a id="grmcomparison-structures"></a>
#### Comparative structures

`grm.comparison-structures` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Compare quantities, qualities and cases precisely with comparative and superlative forms, degree modifiers (considerably, slightly, markedly higher than), multiplicative comparisons (twice as likely as; three times the rate of), proportional comparisons (the more…, the more…) and prepositional frames (compared with/to, in comparison with, relative to). Comparison is central to data commentary, compare–contrast writing and evaluation, and imprecise or illogical comparison is a common source of unclear claims.

Prerequisites: [Determiners and quantifiers](#grmdeterminers-quantifiers) (soft), [Coordination and parallel structure](#grmcoordination-parallelism) (soft)

<a id="grmprepositions"></a>
#### Prepositions and complex prepositions

`grm.prepositions` · LINGUISTIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use dependent prepositions after verbs, nouns and adjectives (focus on, impact on, consistent with) and complex prepositions (in terms of, with respect to, in contrast to, due to, owing to, as a result of) accurately, recognising both their grammatical attachment and their meaning. Prepositions are high-frequency, largely lexically governed and hard to infer, so learners need to treat many of them as parts of larger units.

Prerequisites: [Collocation awareness](#phrcollocation-awareness) (soft), [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft)

<a id="grmclefts-emphasis"></a>
#### Cleft and pseudo-cleft sentences

`grm.clefts-emphasis` · LINGUISTIC · B2+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use it-clefts (It is X that…) and wh- or pseudo-clefts (What this suggests is…) to place one element in focus, typically for contrast or correction, and recognise what clefts presuppose. Clefts are a marked information-structure resource: used sparingly they direct the reader to the key point; overused they sound emphatic or speech-like.

Prerequisites: [Anticipatory 'it' and extraposition](#grmanticipatory-it) (soft), [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new)

<a id="grmfronting-inversion"></a>
#### Fronting and inversion

`grm.fronting-inversion` · LINGUISTIC · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise and use marked word orders in formal writing: fronted complements with subject–verb inversion (Of particular concern is…; Central to this argument is…), inversion after fronted negative or restrictive elements (Not only does X…, Only when… does…, Rarely have…), and fronted adverbials. These structures link to preceding text or create emphasis, but they are infrequent and easily overused, so the target is controlled, occasional use and accurate comprehension.

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect), [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new)

<a id="grmlinking-adverbial-placement"></a>
#### Placement and punctuation of linking adverbials

`grm.linking-adverbial-placement` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Place linking adverbials (however, therefore, thus, moreover, consequently, nevertheless) in initial, medial or final position and punctuate them correctly, recognising that they link sentences rather than join clauses. Medial placement is comparatively common in academic prose and can put focus on the element before the adverbial; the main accuracy problem is the comma splice created by using however or therefore as if it were a conjunction.

Prerequisites: [Semicolons and colons](#sensemicolons-colons), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials)

<a id="grmreporting-structures"></a>
#### Reporting structures

`grm.reporting-structures` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Control the grammatical patterns used to report sources: reporting verb + that-clause (argues that), + noun phrase (questions this assumption), + noun + as (describes X as Y), + wh-clause (examines how), passive and raising patterns (X is thought to…; It has been suggested that…), and adjunct frames (according to X; as X argues; in X's view). Accurate patterns let writers attribute content precisely and signal the extent of attributed material.

Prerequisites: [Word grammar and complementation patterns](#vocword-grammar-patterns) (soft), [That-clauses and wh-clauses](#grmcomplement-clauses)

<a id="grmreference-pronouns"></a>
#### Pronoun and demonstrative reference

`grm.reference-pronouns` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use it, they, this, these, that, those and the former/the latter so that each has a clear antecedent, and choose 'this + noun' (this approach, these findings) when a pronoun alone could refer to several things or to a whole preceding clause. Clear reference is a basic condition of cohesion, and ambiguous 'this' or 'it' is one of the most frequent clarity problems tutors identify.

Prerequisites: [Determiners and quantifiers](#grmdeterminers-quantifiers) (soft), [Simple, compound and complex sentences](#sensentence-types) (soft)

<a id="grmellipsis-substitution"></a>
#### Ellipsis and substitution

`grm.ellipsis-substitution` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Avoid unnecessary repetition with substitute forms (do so, one/ones, that of, those of, the same) and with ellipsis in coordinated and comparative structures, while ensuring the omitted or substituted element is recoverable. These resources contribute to cohesion and concision, and 'that of/those of' is essential for logically valid comparisons.

Prerequisites: [Comparative structures](#grmcomparison-structures) (soft), [Pronoun and demonstrative reference](#grmreference-pronouns)

<a id="grmerror-monitoring"></a>
#### Monitoring recurrent grammatical errors

`grm.error-monitoring` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify one's own persistent error patterns from feedback and self-analysis, prioritise those that most affect meaning and reader judgement, and build targeted editing routines (error logs, single-focus editing passes, checklists) that reduce them over time. Because many errors persist as performance slips rather than gaps in knowledge, self-monitoring is the main route to accuracy beyond the EAP classroom.

Prerequisites: [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading) (soft), [Using written corrective feedback](#fbkcorrective-feedback) (soft)

### Sentence Construction & Punctuation

This domain covers how clauses are combined into well-formed sentences, how punctuation marks grammatical structure and meaning, and how sentence-level choices (length, concision, end-weight) affect readability. Punctuation is treated as a consequence of clause and phrase analysis rather than as a list of rules, and conventions that vary by style guide (serial commas, quotation marks, capitalisation, numbers) are taught as choices to be applied consistently. Sentence-level style is grounded in what corpus research shows about academic prose, in particular that its difficulty comes mainly from dense noun phrases rather than from long chains of clauses.

<a id="sensentence-types"></a>
#### Simple, compound and complex sentences

`sen.sentence-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Recognise and build simple, compound, complex and compound-complex sentences by identifying independent and dependent clauses and the devices that join them (coordinators, subordinators, relative pronouns, punctuation). The classification is a working tool for punctuation and clause combining; it does not measure sophistication, since a long simple sentence with dense noun phrases is typical of academic prose.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements)

<a id="sensentence-boundaries"></a>
#### Sentence boundaries

`sen.sentence-boundaries` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Mark sentence boundaries so that every written sentence contains an independent clause and independent clauses are joined only by a coordinator, a semicolon or a colon, avoiding comma splices, fused (run-on) sentences and unintended fragments. Boundary errors are highly visible to markers and can obscure the logical relation between ideas.

Prerequisites: [Simple, compound and complex sentences](#sensentence-types)

<a id="sencommas"></a>
#### Commas in academic prose

`sen.commas` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use commas after introductory adverbials and subordinate clauses, around non-defining relative clauses, appositives and interrupting elements, and between list items (with a consistent decision on the serial comma), and avoid commas between subject and verb, between a verb and its object or that-clause, and around defining clauses. In academic prose most comma decisions follow from clause and phrase structure, and some (non-defining clauses, serial commas) change or clarify meaning.

Prerequisites: [Defining and non-defining relative clauses](#grmrelative-clauses) (soft), [Subordination and adverbial clauses](#grmsubordination) (soft), [Sentence boundaries](#sensentence-boundaries)

<a id="sensemicolons-colons"></a>
#### Semicolons and colons

`sen.semicolons-colons` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use semicolons to join closely related independent clauses of comparable weight (including before a linking adverbial) and to separate list items that contain internal commas; use colons after a grammatically complete clause to introduce an explanation, elaboration, example, list or quotation. Both marks signal logical relations inside the sentence and are valuable in dense prose, but overuse makes text harder to process.

Prerequisites: [Commas in academic prose](#sencommas)

<a id="senapostrophes-hyphens-dashes"></a>
#### Apostrophes, hyphens and dashes

`sen.apostrophes-hyphens-dashes` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use apostrophes for possession (the author's, the authors', Smith and Jones's study) but not for plurals (1990s, PhDs, NGOs); hyphenate compound modifiers before a noun (evidence-based practice, a well-designed study) but not after it or after -ly adverbs; and use en dashes for ranges and relationships (pp. 12–18; a cost–benefit analysis) and dashes for parenthetical asides according to the target style. These marks distinguish meanings (a small-business owner versus a small business owner) and are routinely checked by editors.

Prerequisites: [Noun-phrase premodification](#grmnp-premodification), [Commas in academic prose](#sencommas) (soft)

<a id="sencapitalisation"></a>
#### Capitalisation conventions

`sen.capitalisation` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Capitalise proper nouns and named entities, and use lower case for general concepts, disciplines and theories unless they derive from a name (psychology, constructivism, Marxism, Piaget's theory); apply the target style's rules for titles, headings and reference-list entries (title case versus sentence case), for the first word after a colon and for acronyms. Inconsistent capitalisation signals unedited text and can imply false proper-noun status for general concepts.

Prerequisites: [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems) (soft)

<a id="senquotation-punctuation"></a>
#### Punctuating quotations and brackets

`sen.quotation-punctuation` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Punctuate quotations accurately: single or double quotation marks and nested quotations according to style, placement of commas, full stops and citations relative to closing marks, block quotations, ellipsis for omissions, square brackets for insertions and changes, [sic] for errors in the original, and parentheses for citations and asides. Accurate quotation punctuation is part of representing sources faithfully as well as a formatting requirement.

Prerequisites: [Commas in academic prose](#sencommas) (soft), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft)

<a id="sensentence-length-variety"></a>
#### Sentence length and variety

`sen.sentence-length-variety` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Control sentence length for readability and emphasis: split or restructure sentences that overload the reader, combine strings of short sentences whose logical relations need to be explicit, and use a short sentence to state a key claim or turning point. Sentence length is a weak index of academic quality, so the aim is length chosen for purpose rather than long sentences as a marker of sophistication.

Prerequisites: [Non-finite clauses](#grmnon-finite-clauses) (soft), [Coordination and parallel structure](#grmcoordination-parallelism) (soft), [Sentence boundaries](#sensentence-boundaries)

<a id="senconcision"></a>
#### Concision

`sen.concision` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Remove redundancy, empty phrases and wordiness (due to the fact that → because; in order to → to; each and every → each), replace light verb + nominalisation with a verb where no compression benefit results (carry out an analysis of → analyse), and cut unnecessary repetition, without stripping the hedges, qualifications and signposting the reader needs. Concision serves word limits and readability, but it is not the same as brevity.

Prerequisites: [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation) (soft), [Non-finite clauses](#grmnon-finite-clauses) (soft)

<a id="senclarity-end-weight"></a>
#### Clarity and end-weight

`sen.clarity-end-weight` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Structure sentences so that subject and main verb come early and close together, long or new elements come late (the principle of end-weight), and long pre-verbal subjects or noun stacks do not delay the verb or create ambiguity; use extraposition, the passive, postmodification or splitting to achieve this. These choices reduce the processing load of dense academic prose and align sentence structure with given–new information flow.

Prerequisites: [Phrasal versus clausal complexity](#grmphrasal-complexity), [Anticipatory 'it' and extraposition](#grmanticipatory-it) (soft), [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new)

<a id="sennumbers-units-style"></a>
#### Writing numbers, units and symbols in text

`sen.numbers-units-style` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Present numbers, units, percentages, ranges and symbols in running text according to a style guide: numerals versus words (thresholds differ between, for example, APA and Chicago), not opening a sentence with a numeral, spacing between number and unit symbol, consistent decimal and thousands separators, ranges with an en dash or 'from … to', and consistent use of % or 'per cent'. Consistency in these conventions is checked by markers and editors and affects readability of data-heavy texts.

Prerequisites: [Apostrophes, hyphens and dashes](#senapostrophes-hyphens-dashes) (soft), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft)

<a id="senlists-bullets"></a>
#### Lists and bullet points

`sen.lists-bullets` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Decide when vertical lists are appropriate (common in reports, proposals, methods, slides and posters; usually discouraged in essays where connected prose is expected) and format both run-in and vertical lists with a grammatical lead-in, parallel items, and consistent punctuation and capitalisation according to the target style. Converting a list into connected prose that makes the relations between items explicit, and vice versa, is a key skill for moving between genres.

Prerequisites: [Coordination and parallel structure](#grmcoordination-parallelism), [Semicolons and colons](#sensemicolons-colons) (soft), [Genre and discourse community](#gesgenre-concept) (soft)

## Academic Discourse

### Cohesion & Coherence

This domain covers the resources that make a text hang together for its reader: cohesive ties (reference, conjunction, lexical cohesion) and the information-structuring choices (given–new order, thematic progression, encapsulation, text patterns) that make a sequence of sentences read as a purposeful whole. It treats cohesion as a means to coherence rather than a proxy for it, countering the connector-heavy, checklist approach that learner-corpus findings and test preparation tend to reinforce. Topics move from sentence-to-sentence linking towards text-level signalling and the reader-oriented expectations of English-medium academic writing, including spoken signposting in lectures and presentations.

<a id="cohcohesion-vs-coherence"></a>
#### Cohesion versus coherence

`coh.cohesion-vs-coherence` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Distinguish cohesion — the explicit lexical and grammatical ties between sentences (reference, conjunction, substitution, ellipsis, lexical relations) — from coherence, the reader's sense that a text has a consistent topic, purpose and logical development. The distinction lets learners see that linking devices cannot rescue a text whose ideas are not ordered or connected, and that coherent texts often leave some relations implicit.

<a id="cohlexical-cohesion"></a>
#### Lexical cohesion and reference chains

`coh.lexical-cohesion` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Keep a topic in view across sentences through repetition, synonymy, hyponymy and superordinate or general nouns, combined with pronoun and article reference, so that the reader can track each participant through a text. Controlled reference chains are a major source of cohesion in academic prose, which repeats key nouns more than learners trained to 'avoid repetition' tend to expect.

Prerequisites: [Articles in academic writing](#grmarticles) (soft), [Pronoun and demonstrative reference](#grmreference-pronouns) (soft), [Cohesion versus coherence](#cohcohesion-vs-coherence) (soft)

<a id="cohlinking-adverbials"></a>
#### Linking adverbials and connectors

`coh.linking-adverbials` · LINGUISTIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use additive, adversative, causal, sequential and summative linking adverbials (in addition, however, therefore, first, overall) to make relations between sentences explicit where the reader needs them, with expert-like frequency, placement and punctuation. Learner corpora show both overuse of a small set of sentence-initial connectors and mismatches between connector and relation, so the aim is selective rather than maximal use.

Prerequisites: [Simple, compound and complex sentences](#sensentence-types) (soft), [Cohesion versus coherence](#cohcohesion-vs-coherence) (soft)

<a id="cohconnector-precision"></a>
#### Precise logical relations

`coh.connector-precision` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Choose between connectors with related but distinct meanings and grammar — however, whereas, although, in contrast, on the contrary, nevertheless; therefore, thus, hence, consequently — according to the exact relation (contrast, concession or correction; inference or consequence) and the clause structure each requires. Precision here distinguishes argument that is clear from text that merely sounds connected.

Prerequisites: [Subordination and adverbial clauses](#grmsubordination), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials)

<a id="cohgiven-new"></a>
#### Given–new information structure

`coh.given-new` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand and apply the tendency in English for clauses to begin with information the reader already has (given) and to end with new or newsworthy information, using choices such as voice, a different grammatical subject or a summarising noun phrase to achieve this. Respecting given–new order is a main reason expert prose reads smoothly with relatively few connectors.

Prerequisites: [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice) (soft)

<a id="cohthematic-progression"></a>
#### Thematic progression

`coh.thematic-progression` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Manage the sequence of clause themes across a paragraph using Daneš's patterns — constant theme, linear progression in which the rheme of one clause becomes the next theme, and themes derived from a hypertheme — to move an argument forward while keeping it anchored. The patterns give writers a practical diagnostic for paragraphs that feel either repetitive or disjointed.

Prerequisites: [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation) (soft), [Lexical cohesion and reference chains](#cohlexical-cohesion) (soft), [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new)

<a id="cohsignalling-nouns"></a>
#### Encapsulation with signalling nouns

`coh.signalling-nouns` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use signalling (shell) nouns with a demonstrative or definite determiner — this approach, these findings, such a view, the problem is that — to encapsulate a preceding stretch of text or set up a following one, labelling it as a claim, problem, finding or limitation. Encapsulation links sentences while also categorising, and often evaluating, what has just been said.

Prerequisites: [Abstract and shell nouns](#vocabstract-shell-nouns), [Pronoun and demonstrative reference](#grmreference-pronouns)

<a id="cohtext-patterns"></a>
#### Text structure patterns

`coh.text-patterns` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise and use recurrent text patterns — problem–solution (situation, problem, response, evaluation), general–specific, claim–counterclaim (hypothetical–real) and matching (comparison) — that organise stretches longer than a sentence and set up reader expectations. Knowing these patterns supports efficient reading, because readers can predict what comes next from lexical signals, and gives writers a frame for organising paragraphs and sections.

Prerequisites: [Cohesion versus coherence](#cohcohesion-vs-coherence)

<a id="cohframe-markers"></a>
#### Frame markers and advance organisers

`coh.frame-markers` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use frame markers — sequencing (first, finally), stage labels (to summarise, overall), goal announcements (this essay argues, the aim of this section is) and topic shifts (turning to, with regard to) — to make the organisation of an extended text visible at a density suited to its length and genre. Frame markers are an interactive metadiscourse resource that eases the reader's processing; they should not substitute for content.

Prerequisites: [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft), [Reader and writer responsibility](#cohwriter-responsibility) (soft)

<a id="cohparagraph-transitions"></a>
#### Transitions between paragraphs and sections

`coh.paragraph-transitions` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Link one paragraph or section to the next with bridging sentences or clauses that state how the coming point relates to the previous one — extends, contrasts with, qualifies or applies it — typically by encapsulating the prior point and announcing the new one. Effective transitions make the line of argument visible across units instead of leaving each paragraph as a free-standing block.

Prerequisites: [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new) (soft), [Encapsulation with signalling nouns](#cohsignalling-nouns) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences)

<a id="cohterminological-consistency"></a>
#### Terminological consistency

`coh.terminological-consistency` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Keep key technical and conceptual terms constant once introduced, varying wording only where the referent genuinely differs, so that readers do not infer distinctions the writer does not intend. In academic writing a change of term is often read as a change of concept, so the 'elegant variation' rewarded in some other kinds of writing can mislead.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Lexical precision and near-synonyms](#vocprecision-near-synonyms) (soft), [Lexical cohesion and reference chains](#cohlexical-cohesion)

<a id="cohwriter-responsibility"></a>
#### Reader and writer responsibility

`coh.writer-responsibility` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand the expectation, common in English-medium academic writing, that the writer rather than the reader is responsible for making purpose, structure and logical connections explicit, and recognise this as a convention that varies across disciplines, genres and writing traditions rather than a universal standard of good writing. The concept helps learners interpret feedback such as 'unclear' or 'make the link explicit' and decide how much to spell out for a given reader.

Prerequisites: [Cohesion versus coherence](#cohcohesion-vs-coherence), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft)

<a id="cohspoken-signposting"></a>
#### Signposting in spoken academic discourse

`coh.spoken-signposting` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use and recognise macro-markers (Let's move on to…, What I want to focus on today is…) and micro-markers (so, now, right) together with pausing and intonation to organise spoken academic discourse in lectures, presentations and seminars. Because listeners cannot scan back, explicit structural signalling carries more of the organisational load in speech than in writing.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft)

### Stance, Metadiscourse & Evaluation

This domain covers the interpersonal dimension of academic discourse: how writers and speakers signal certainty and commitment, evaluate ideas and sources, represent themselves, engage readers, and keep their own claims distinct from those they attribute to others. It draws on Hyland's models of metadiscourse and of stance and engagement and on Martin and White's appraisal framework, especially engagement, treating them as complementary lenses rather than a single taxonomy. Stance is approached as calibration and positioning grounded in evidence, not as lists of hedges to insert, and disciplinary variation is made explicit throughout.

<a id="stametadiscourse"></a>
#### Metadiscourse

`sta.metadiscourse` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand metadiscourse as the resources by which writers and speakers organise a text for its reader (interactive: transitions, frame markers, endophoric markers, evidentials, code glosses) and express attitudes and engage the reader (interactional: hedges, boosters, attitude markers, self-mention, engagement markers). The concept gives learners a unified way of seeing academic writing as interaction with a reader rather than as neutral presentation of content.

Prerequisites: [Frame markers and advance organisers](#cohframe-markers) (soft), [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft)

<a id="stahedging"></a>
#### Hedging devices

`sta.hedging` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use hedging devices — modal verbs (may, might, could), lexical verbs (suggest, appear, indicate), adverbs and adjectives (possibly, likely), approximators (approximately, to some extent) and impersonal frames (it seems that, it is possible that) — to present claims with appropriate tentativeness, mark the writer's precision about evidence and show deference to readers and the field. Hedging is central to how academic claims are negotiated and accepted.

Prerequisites: [Verbs for academic and research processes](#vocacademic-verbs) (soft), [Epistemic modal verbs](#grmepistemic-modality), [Anticipatory 'it' and extraposition](#grmanticipatory-it) (soft)

<a id="staclaim-calibration"></a>
#### Calibrating claims to evidence

`sta.claim-calibration` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Match the strength of each claim to the type, quality and quantity of evidence and to the scope of the data, choosing among assertion, boosting, hedging and limitation of scope so that claims are neither overstated nor hedged into vacuity. Calibration is a reasoning skill realised in language: the writer first judges what the evidence licenses and then chooses wording.

Prerequisites: [Hedging devices](#stahedging), [Boosters](#staboosters), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="staboosters"></a>
#### Boosters

`sta.boosters` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use boosters — adverbs (clearly, undoubtedly), verbs (show, demonstrate, establish), and impersonal frames (it is evident that, there is no doubt that) — to express justified certainty and solidarity with readers, and recognise when certainty is unwarranted or rhetorically risky. Together with hedges, boosters let writers mark which claims they are prepared to stand behind.

Prerequisites: [Verbs for academic and research processes](#vocacademic-verbs) (soft), [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft)

<a id="staattitude-markers"></a>
#### Attitude markers

`sta.attitude-markers` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use attitude markers — evaluative adverbs (surprisingly, importantly, unfortunately), adjectives (striking, notable, problematic) and frames (it is notable that, a key limitation is) — to signal the writer's affective and evaluative stance towards propositions, and judge when explicit evaluation strengthens or weakens an academic text. Such markers direct readers to what the writer considers significant, surprising or problematic.

Prerequisites: [Evaluative vocabulary](#vocevaluative-lexis), [Metadiscourse](#stametadiscourse) (soft)

<a id="staself-mention"></a>
#### Self-mention and authorial roles

`sta.self-mention` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use first-person pronouns and alternatives (I, we, the present study, this thesis) to construct authorial roles — from guide through the text and architect of its structure to recounter of procedures, opinion-holder and originator of ideas — in line with disciplinary and genre norms. Self-mention is a choice about presence and responsibility, not a matter of a blanket rule for or against 'I'.

Prerequisites: [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft), [Metadiscourse](#stametadiscourse) (soft)

<a id="staengagement-markers"></a>
#### Reader engagement

`sta.engagement-markers` · LINGUISTIC · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use reader-engagement resources — inclusive 'we' and other reader references, directives (note that, consider, see Table 2), questions, appeals to shared knowledge (as is well known) and personal asides — to bring readers into the argument, guide their interpretation and build solidarity, at a level suited to discipline and genre.

Prerequisites: [Boosters](#staboosters) (soft), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft)

<a id="stadialogic-positioning"></a>
#### Dialogic positioning (engagement)

`sta.dialogic-positioning` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP
