Position a text in relation to other voices by expanding the dialogue (entertaining possibilities, attributing views to others) or contracting it (denying, countering, concurring, pronouncing), as described in the engagement system of appraisal theory. Control of these options lets writers acknowledge alternatives while steering readers towards their own position, a pattern associated with highly rated academic argument.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution), [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language) (soft)

<a id="staaverral-attribution"></a>
#### Averral versus attribution

`sta.averral-attribution` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Distinguish propositions the writer avers (takes responsibility for) from those attributed to other sources, and track this distinction in reading and writing so that it is always clear whose claim is whose and how far the writer endorses it. Because unsignalled statements are read as the writer's own averral, failing to mark attribution or its boundary affects both the argument and academic integrity.

Prerequisites: [That-clauses and wh-clauses](#grmcomplement-clauses) (soft), [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite) (soft)

<a id="staevaluating-others-language"></a>
#### Language for evaluating others' work

`sta.evaluating-others-language` · LINGUISTIC · B2+–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use language that evaluates other scholars' work fairly and precisely — evaluative reporting verbs and adjectives, praise–criticism pairs and mitigated critique (While X offers…, it overlooks…; a limitation of this study is…) — so that critique is specific, justified and collegial. This language is central to literature reviews, critiques, peer review and positioning one's own research.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating) (soft), [Reporting verbs](#srcreporting-verbs)

<a id="staauthorial-voice"></a>
#### Authorial voice

`sta.authorial-voice` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand authorial voice as the identity and position a writer constructs through linguistic choices — stance markers, self-mention, the framing of sources and control of the argument's structure — and develop a voice that is distinct from, and in control of, the voices of sources. Voice is a textual achievement that can be analysed and taught, not an innate personal quality or simply the use of 'I'.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution)

<a id="staconcession-language"></a>
#### Language of concession and counter-argument

`sta.concession-language` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use the language of concession and counter-argument — subordinators (although, while, even though), adverbials (admittedly, nevertheless, nonetheless), impersonal frames (it could be argued that, it is true that…, but) and limiting moves (this does not mean that) — to acknowledge an opposing point and then limit or outweigh it. When followed by an effective counter, concession signals fairness and anticipates objections, strengthening rather than weakening an argument.

Prerequisites: [Precise logical relations](#cohconnector-precision), [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft)

<a id="stageneralisation-qualification"></a>
#### Generalising and qualifying

`sta.generalisation-qualification` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Make generalisations whose scope matches the evidence by using quantifiers and frequency expressions (most, many, a minority of, often, rarely), tendency expressions (tend to, are inclined to), approximators (broadly, largely, in general) and scope limiters (in most cases, with some exceptions, in this context). Bounded generalisation avoids both sweeping claims and lists of particulars that never reach a general point.

Prerequisites: [Determiners and quantifiers](#grmdeterminers-quantifiers), [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft)

<a id="stacode-glosses"></a>
#### Code glosses and exemplification

`sta.code-glosses` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use code glosses to reformulate (that is, in other words, namely, i.e.) and to exemplify (for example, such as, e.g.) so that readers recover the intended meaning of a term, claim or category, judging what the intended reader needs explained. Code glosses are an interactive metadiscourse resource that reflects the writer's model of the reader's knowledge.

Prerequisites: [Academic abbreviations and Latin expressions](#vocabbreviations-latin) (soft), [Exemplifying and illustrating](#funexemplifying) (soft)

<a id="staspoken-stance"></a>
#### Expressing stance in speech

`sta.spoken-stance` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Express and recognise stance in spoken academic interaction — opinion frames (I'd argue, it seems to me), mitigated disagreement (I'm not entirely convinced that), vague language and approximation (sort of, more or less) and boosting (I'm fairly sure) — together with prosodic cues, so that speakers can take positions in seminars, presentations and supervisions while maintaining collegial relations.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft)

### Rhetorical Functions

This domain covers recurrent rhetorical functions — defining, classifying, describing processes and structures, comparing, expressing cause and effect, exemplifying, explaining, hypothesising, recommending, summarising, narrating and evaluating — that cut across genres and disciplines. It follows the discourse approach to English for science and technology (Trimble), Mohan's knowledge framework and functional genre work (Martin and Rose; Nesi and Gardner's genre families), treating each function as a pairing of a communicative purpose with characteristic lexicogrammar and organisation. Functions are building blocks for paragraphs, sections and whole genres; teaching them through authentic disciplinary exemplars rather than decontextualised phrase lists keeps the focus on what each function achieves.

<a id="fundefining"></a>
#### Defining terms

`fun.defining` · LINGUISTIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Define terms using formal sentence definitions (term + class + distinguishing features), extended definitions that add examples, contrasts or history, operational definitions that state how a concept is measured or identified, and discussion of contested definitions that compares how different authors define a term. Defining establishes shared ground and shows readers how the writer is using key concepts.

Prerequisites: [Defining and non-defining relative clauses](#grmrelative-clauses)

<a id="funclassifying"></a>
#### Classifying and categorising

`fun.classifying` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Classify phenomena by stating the basis of classification, the categories and their members, using language such as can be divided into, falls into, comprises, is classified according to and consists of. Clear classification rests on a consistent criterion and categories that are mutually exclusive and, where intended, exhaustive.

Prerequisites: [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice) (soft), [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft)

<a id="fundescribing-process"></a>
#### Describing processes and procedures

`fun.describing-process` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Describe natural, experimental and technical processes and procedures in a clear sequence, using sequencing devices, passive voice where the agent is unimportant, purpose expressions (to + infinitive, in order to, so that) and time clauses. Process description underpins methods sections, laboratory and technical reports, and explanations of how phenomena occur.

Prerequisites: [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft)

<a id="fundescribing-structure"></a>
#### Describing structures and systems

`fun.describing-structure` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Describe the structure of physical objects, organisations and systems in terms of parts and wholes (consists of, comprises, is made up of), spatial relations (adjacent to, at the base of, connected to) and the functions of components (serves to, is responsible for). Structural description is essential in science, engineering, design, anatomy, geography and organisational studies.

Prerequisites: [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice) (soft), [Prepositions and complex prepositions](#grmprepositions)

<a id="funcomparing-contrasting"></a>
#### Comparing and contrasting

`fun.comparing-contrasting` · LINGUISTIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Compare and contrast entities, theories, methods or data against explicit criteria using similarity and difference language (similarly, likewise, whereas, in contrast, differ in, share, unlike) and comparative structures, and organise extended comparisons point by point or in blocks according to purpose. Academic comparison is led by criteria and arrives at a point, rather than listing similarities and differences.

Prerequisites: [Subordination and adverbial clauses](#grmsubordination) (soft), [Comparative structures](#grmcomparison-structures), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft)

<a id="funcause-effect"></a>
#### Expressing cause and effect

`fun.cause-effect` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Express causal relations through conjunctions (because, since, so), prepositions (due to, owing to, as a result of), verbs (lead to, result in, contribute to, stem from), nouns (a cause of, a consequence of, a factor in) and adjectives (attributable to, responsible for), and calibrate the strength of the causal claim (causes, is associated with, may contribute to). Much academic explanation packs causation into verbs and noun phrases rather than linking clauses with conjunctions.

Prerequisites: [Word grammar and complementation patterns](#vocword-grammar-patterns) (soft), [Subordination and adverbial clauses](#grmsubordination)

<a id="funexemplifying"></a>
#### Exemplifying and illustrating

`fun.exemplifying` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Illustrate general statements with examples and cases using exemplification language (for example, for instance, such as, a case in point is, as illustrated by) and choose examples that are representative and relevant, linking them explicitly back to the point they support. Examples make abstract claims concrete, but they carry argumentative weight only when their relevance is made clear.

Prerequisites: [Noun-phrase postmodification](#grmnp-postmodification) (soft), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft)

<a id="funproblem-solution"></a>
#### Problem, solution and evaluation

`fun.problem-solution` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Present situations, problems, responses and evaluations using problem vocabulary (challenge, constraint, drawback), response vocabulary (approach, measure, intervention) and evaluative language that judges whether responses work, including cycles in which a response creates a new problem. This function organises problem–solution essays, case studies, reports and the gap-identifying moves of research introductions.

Prerequisites: [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating) (soft)

<a id="funexplaining"></a>
#### Explaining and justifying

`fun.explaining` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Explain how and why phenomena occur and justify decisions or claims by giving reasons, mechanisms and rationales, using language such as because, since, which explains why, this is because, the reason for and the rationale for. Explanation goes beyond stating that something happens to show the mechanism or reasoning behind it, which is much of what markers mean by 'analysis'.

Prerequisites: [Defining and non-defining relative clauses](#grmrelative-clauses) (soft), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect)

<a id="funhypothesising"></a>
#### Predicting and hypothesising

`fun.hypothesising` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Predict outcomes and formulate hypotheses using predictive and speculative language — modal verbs (will, would, may, is likely to), conditionals, hypothesis frames (it is hypothesised that, would be expected to) and conditions on predictions (provided that, assuming that) — distinguishing confident predictions from tentative speculation. Hypothesising is central to research proposals, experimental reports, discussions of implications and thought experiments.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect) (soft)

<a id="funrecommending"></a>
#### Recommending

`fun.recommending` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Make recommendations that follow from analysis, using modal and lexical resources (should, it is recommended that, it is advisable to), the mandative subjunctive or should-alternative (It is essential that each participant be informed), and language of priority, feasibility and conditions (in the first instance, where resources allow). Effective recommendations are specific, justified by the preceding analysis and pitched to the reader's authority to act.

Prerequisites: [That-clauses and wh-clauses](#grmcomplement-clauses), [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft), [Problem, solution and evaluation](#funproblem-solution) (soft)

<a id="funsummarising-concluding"></a>
#### Summarising and concluding

`fun.summarising-concluding` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Summarise one's own preceding content and draw conclusions from it, using summative and inferential language (Overall, In sum, Taken together, These findings suggest, It can therefore be concluded that), so that a section or text ends by stating what has been established and what follows. This function differs from summarising a source: it consolidates the writer's own argument or results.

Prerequisites: [Pronoun and demonstrative reference](#grmreference-pronouns) (soft), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft), [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft)

<a id="funchronology-narration"></a>
#### Chronological narration

`fun.chronology-narration` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Narrate events and developments in chronological order using time expressions (in the early 1990s, over the following decade, by 2010), periodisation (the post-war period, the first phase), tense and aspect sequences (past simple, past perfect, present perfect for developments continuing to now) and time clauses. This function is needed in historical accounts, case histories, methodology recounts and literature reviews that trace the development of a field.

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft)

<a id="funevaluating"></a>
#### Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance

`fun.evaluating` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Evaluate ideas, evidence, methods, solutions or texts by stating explicit criteria, weighing strengths against weaknesses and reaching a judgement of value or significance, using evaluative lexis (robust, limited, compelling, a shortcoming), comparative evaluation and balanced structures (While X…, Y). Evaluation is a large part of what distinguishes critical from descriptive academic writing.

Prerequisites: [Comparing and contrasting](#funcomparing-contrasting) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation)

## Academic Reading

### Reading Processes & Fluency

This domain covers the cognitive and linguistic foundations of academic reading: automatic word recognition, parsing of compressed academic sentences, reading fluency and volume, and the way these lower-level processes interact with background knowledge, purpose and reading load. L2 reading research finds that L2 vocabulary and grammatical knowledge are the strongest correlates of L2 reading comprehension, so the domain prioritises building the linguistic base and reading volume on which strategies depend rather than treating strategies as a substitute for them. It also addresses the practical conditions of university reading: on-screen texts, long reading lists and the legitimate, checked use of a reader's other languages.

<a id="rdpinteractive-reading"></a>
#### Reading as an interactive process

`rdp.interactive-reading` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand comprehension as the interaction of lower-level processes (word recognition, syntactic parsing, forming propositions) with higher-level processes (building a model of what the text says and integrating it with background knowledge, purpose and inference). The concept explains why L2 readers who know many strategies can still fail when lexical and grammatical processing is slow or inaccurate, and gives learners and teachers a shared vocabulary for diagnosing reading problems.

<a id="rdpword-recognition"></a>
#### Automatic word recognition

`rdp.word-recognition` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Recognise frequent and core academic words quickly and accurately enough that recognition demands little attention, freeing working memory for building meaning. Recognition speed depends on the quality of stored form–meaning knowledge and is shaped by the reader's first-language writing system, so it develops mainly through large amounts of reading and timed practice with known words rather than through strategy instruction.

Prerequisites: [High-frequency vocabulary base](#vochigh-frequency-base)

<a id="rdpreading-fluency-rate"></a>
#### Reading fluency and rate

`rdp.reading-fluency-rate` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Read connected academic text at a rate appropriate to purpose while maintaining comprehension, and build that rate through timed reading of easy texts, repeated reading and extensive reading. Learners also develop realistic expectations: silent reading of expository text by skilled adult L1 readers averages roughly 200–300 words per minute, slower for dense or unfamiliar material, and claims that speed-reading techniques multiply rate without loss of comprehension are not supported by eye-movement and comprehension research.

Prerequisites: [Automatic word recognition](#rdpword-recognition), [Extensive reading](#rdpextensive-reading) (soft)

<a id="rdpextensive-reading"></a>
#### Extensive reading

`rdp.extensive-reading` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Read large quantities of self-selected, linguistically easy material, such as graded readers, accessible non-fiction and popular science, for meaning and enjoyment, with minimal dictionary use and no intensive exercises attached. Extensive reading supports reading rate, consolidation and incidental growth of vocabulary, and confidence, but its gains accumulate over months, so it must be planned into a programme and continued beyond it rather than recommended in passing.

Prerequisites: [Lexical coverage and vocabulary size](#voclexical-coverage) (soft)

<a id="rdpreading-purpose"></a>
#### Reading with a purpose

`rdp.reading-purpose` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Set an explicit purpose before reading (to locate information, grasp gist, learn content, follow an argument, extract a method, integrate sources, write or evaluate) and adjust speed, depth, sequence and note-making to that purpose. Purpose-setting is what makes selective reading principled rather than careless, and it sets the standard for what counts as having understood a text well enough.

Prerequisites: [Reading as an interactive process](#rdpinteractive-reading) (soft)

<a id="rdpparsing-complex-sentences"></a>
#### Parsing complex academic sentences

`rdp.parsing-complex-sentences` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Analyse long academic sentences by locating the finite verb of the main clause, its subject and the head nouns of heavily modified noun phrases, so that the core proposition can be separated from embedded and qualifying material. Academic prose packs information into phrases (nominalisations, prepositional chains, pre- and post-modification) rather than clauses, so readers who parse as they would conversation often misidentify what a sentence claims.

Prerequisites: [Noun-phrase premodification](#grmnp-premodification), [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation)

<a id="rdpbackground-knowledge"></a>
#### Background knowledge and schemata

`rdp.background-knowledge` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise that comprehension depends on topic, disciplinary and text-type knowledge as well as language, and deliberately activate and build that knowledge before and during reading through accessible overviews, glossaries, textbooks and lectures. This explains why the same reader can read fluently in a familiar field yet struggle in a new one, and why building knowledge is a legitimate reading strategy, especially for postgraduates changing discipline.

Prerequisites: [Reading as an interactive process](#rdpinteractive-reading) (soft)

<a id="rdpmanaging-reading-load"></a>
#### Managing reading load

`rdp.managing-reading-load` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Plan and triage the reading that university study generates: distinguish essential from further reading, decide what to read closely, partially or not at all in relation to teaching sessions and assessments, and schedule reading realistically given one's own reading rate in English. Effective triage is a judgement about relevance rather than a speed technique, and it involves accepting that not every item on a list is meant to be read in full.

Prerequisites: [Reading fluency and rate](#rdpreading-fluency-rate) (soft), [Reading with a purpose](#rdpreading-purpose), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft)

<a id="rdpdigital-reading"></a>
#### Reading on screen

`rdp.digital-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Read academic texts on screen, as PDFs, e-books and web pages, in ways that sustain comprehension: choosing screen or paper for the task, using search and digital annotation purposefully, managing hyperlinks, tabs and notifications, and checking unfamiliar web sources before trusting them. Meta-analytic evidence suggests a small comprehension disadvantage for screen reading of expository text, particularly under time pressure, which appears to reflect shallower processing and overconfidence rather than the screen as such.

Prerequisites: [Monitoring comprehension](#rdscomprehension-monitoring) (soft), [Purposeful annotation](#rdsannotation) (soft)

<a id="rdpplurilingual-reading"></a>
#### Strategic use of first language and translation in reading

`rdp.plurilingual-reading` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use the first language and other languages, bilingual dictionaries, glossaries and machine translation to support comprehension of difficult academic texts while continuing to process the English text itself, verifying key terms and disciplinary meanings, and staying within institutional rules. Reading ability and academic concepts developed in another language are a transferable resource; the risks lie in translation replacing engagement with English, mistranslated technical terms and the loss of hedging, negation and attribution.

Prerequisites: [Monitoring comprehension](#rdscomprehension-monitoring) (soft), [Machine translation](#digmachine-translation) (soft)

### Reading Strategies & Comprehension

This domain covers the strategies and comprehension skills that turn decoded text into usable understanding: previewing, selective reading, identifying main ideas and text organisation, tracking reference and stance, inferring, monitoring and repairing comprehension, annotating, and integrating prose with visuals. Strategy instruction has positive but variable average effects, and its benefits depend on learners having enough language knowledge to apply strategies to the text at hand. Skimming and scanning are often over-represented in materials shaped by test formats relative to the careful reading for learning and argument that dominates university study, so the domain gives priority to main ideas, structure, reference and stance, and treats speeded strategies as tools for specific purposes.

<a id="rdspreviewing"></a>
#### Previewing and surveying

`rds.previewing` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Survey a text before reading it in detail by examining its title, abstract or summary, headings, introduction and conclusion, figures and section structure, in order to predict content, judge relevance and plan how to read. Previewing builds a framework that makes later careful reading faster and more accurate, and is the quickest reliable way to decide whether a source deserves more time.

Prerequisites: [Reading with a purpose](#rdpreading-purpose), [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="rdsskimming"></a>
#### Skimming for gist

`rds.skimming` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Read rapidly and selectively, sampling headings, the opening and closing sentences of paragraphs and salient words, to grasp the gist and overall structure of a text. Skimming is useful for orientation and relevance decisions, but it inevitably reduces comprehension of what is skipped, so it complements rather than replaces careful reading of texts that matter for the task.

Prerequisites: [Automatic word recognition](#rdpword-recognition) (soft), [Previewing and surveying](#rdspreviewing) (soft), [Using paragraph structure when reading](#rdsparagraph-structure-reading) (soft)

<a id="rdsscanning"></a>
#### Scanning for specific information

`rds.scanning` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Locate specific information such as names, dates, figures, terms or a particular claim by searching for its likely form rather than reading continuously, then read the surrounding text carefully to confirm that it answers the question. In academic work scanning is used with indexes, tables, reference lists and long documents, increasingly through digital search, so anticipating wording and verifying context matter more than raw speed.

Prerequisites: [Automatic word recognition](#rdpword-recognition) (soft), [Reading with a purpose](#rdpreading-purpose)

<a id="rdsmain-ideas"></a>
#### Identifying main ideas and supporting detail

`rds.main-ideas` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Identify the central claims of a paragraph, section and whole text and distinguish them from elaboration, examples, evidence and background. Main-idea identification underpins summarising, note-making and critical reading, and in academic texts main ideas are often implicit, distributed across sentences or signalled only through evaluation.

Prerequisites: [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences) (soft), [Using paragraph structure when reading](#rdsparagraph-structure-reading)

<a id="rdsparagraph-structure-reading"></a>
#### Using paragraph structure when reading

`rds.paragraph-structure-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Use knowledge of how academic paragraphs are built (controlling sentences, development patterns such as definition, exemplification, cause–effect and comparison, and concluding or linking sentences) to predict and follow paragraph content. Paragraph structure is a probabilistic cue rather than a rule: many expert paragraphs open with background or a counter-view and have delayed, implicit or multiple points.

Prerequisites: [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft)

<a id="rdstext-organisation"></a>
#### Recognising text organisation

`rds.text-organisation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise the overall organisation of a text (problem–solution, claim–counterclaim, general–specific, comparison, cause–effect, chronological) and the signals that mark it (headings, frame markers, signalling nouns, connectors) in order to follow the writer's plan, anticipate what comes next and recall content in structured form. Explicit awareness of top-level structure has been shown to support L2 readers' recall.

Prerequisites: [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns), [Frame markers and advance organisers](#cohframe-markers) (soft)

<a id="rdsresearch-article-navigation"></a>
#### Navigating research articles

`rds.research-article-navigation` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Read empirical research articles non-linearly and efficiently by using their conventional structure (abstract; introduction establishing a gap and aims; methods; results; discussion; conclusion) and their figures and tables to locate what a given purpose requires. Experienced readers rarely read research articles from start to finish; knowing what each section typically does, and how this varies across disciplines and article types, lets novices do the same.

Prerequisites: [Previewing and surveying](#rdspreviewing), [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation) (soft), [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft)

<a id="rdsinference"></a>
#### Making inferences

`rds.inference` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Draw the inferences a text requires but does not state: bridging inferences that connect sentences (cause, contrast, reference), knowledge-based inferences that connect the text to what the reader knows, and inferences about implications and the writer's unstated position. Compressed academic phrasing leaves many logical relations implicit, so inferencing is essential, but inferences must be anchored in textual evidence and revised when the text contradicts them.

Prerequisites: [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge), [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft), [Tracking reference while reading](#rdsreference-tracking) (soft)

<a id="rdsreference-tracking"></a>
#### Tracking reference while reading

`rds.reference-tracking` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Resolve reference across sentences while reading: pronouns, demonstratives (this, these, such), signalling and shell nouns (this approach, the problem, these findings), comparative reference (the former, other studies) and substitution or ellipsis. Academic writers often use 'this' plus a summarising noun to encapsulate whole stretches of preceding text, and wrongly resolved reference is a frequent, often unnoticed source of misunderstanding.

Prerequisites: [Pronoun and demonstrative reference](#grmreference-pronouns)

<a id="rdsfact-opinion-claim"></a>
#### Distinguishing fact, opinion, claim and evidence

`rds.fact-opinion-claim` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Classify statements in academic texts by their epistemic status: background treated as established, the writer's own claim, a claim attributed to others, evidence offered in support, evaluation, and speculation. The school-level fact/opinion binary is inadequate for academic reading, because most academic statements are claims supported to different degrees and marked as such by hedges, boosters and attribution.

Prerequisites: [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft), [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft)

<a id="rdswriter-stance"></a>
#### Identifying writer stance and attributed views

`rds.writer-stance` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Track whose voice is speaking at each point in an academic text and how strongly the writer endorses, distances themselves from or rejects attributed views, using reporting verbs, attribution, hedges, boosters, attitude markers and concessive structures as cues. Attributing a cited view to the writer, or missing the writer's evaluation of a source, is a common cause of misrepresentation in student summaries and essays.

Prerequisites: [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution), [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language) (soft), [Distinguishing fact, opinion, claim and evidence](#rdsfact-opinion-claim) (soft)

<a id="rdscomprehension-monitoring"></a>
#### Monitoring comprehension

`rds.comprehension-monitoring` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Notice while reading when comprehension has broken down or is too shallow for the purpose, diagnose the likely cause and apply a matching repair: rereading, slowing down, reading on for clarification, parsing a sentence, checking a key word, consulting a background source or asking a question. Monitoring is a metacognitive skill, and readers often overestimate their understanding, especially of difficult or unfamiliar texts.

Prerequisites: [Reading with a purpose](#rdpreading-purpose) (soft), [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="rdsannotation"></a>
#### Purposeful annotation

`rds.annotation` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Annotate texts selectively and in one's own words, marking main claims, structure, key evidence, questions and links to a task, so that annotations support later summary, discussion or writing. Annotation helps to the extent that it requires processing; extensive highlighting without comment has low utility for learning, and annotations that do not separate the author's ideas from the reader's create risks for later source use.

Prerequisites: [Reading with a purpose](#rdpreading-purpose) (soft), [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas)

<a id="rdstextbook-reading"></a>
#### Reading textbooks

`rds.textbook-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Read introductory textbooks to learn disciplinary content, using their apparatus (chapter objectives, headings, definitions, worked examples, figures, summaries, review questions) to organise study and to check learning through self-testing. Textbooks are the main reading genre in many foundation and first-year programmes, and their dense definitions, taxonomies and technical explanations make them demanding despite their pedagogical design.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Previewing and surveying](#rdspreviewing) (soft), [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas)

<a id="rdsvisuals-in-text"></a>
#### Integrating text and visuals

`rds.visuals-in-text` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Integrate information from figures, tables and diagrams with the surrounding prose: follow references to visuals, consult them at the point they are needed, check whether the writer's description matches what they show, and notice what they show that the text does not mention. Many readers skip visuals or read them superficially, missing evidence on which the argument depends.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs), [Reading tables](#datreading-tables) (soft)

### Critical & Multiple-Text Reading

This domain addresses reading as evaluation and integration: reconstructing arguments, evaluating evidence and methods, surfacing assumptions and perspective, reading theoretical texts, reading across multiple sources and mapping literatures, and moving from comprehension to a reasoned response that serves writing. Critical reading is presented as questioning and evaluating rather than fault-finding, and as partly discipline-specific; the notion of critical thinking is itself contested in EAP. Research on multiple-document reading shows that attending to sources and integrating across texts are distinct skills that do not develop automatically from single-text comprehension.

<a id="rdccritical-reading"></a>
#### What critical reading involves

`rdc.critical-reading` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand critical reading as actively questioning a text's purpose, claims, evidence, reasoning, assumptions and significance in order to judge its value for one's own purposes, which includes recognising strengths as well as weaknesses. What counts as critical varies across disciplines, and the concept of critical thinking is itself contested, so learners need to see critical reading modelled in their field rather than as a universal checklist or as fault-finding.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft), [Critical thinking in academic contexts](#crtconcept) (soft)

<a id="rdcargument-identification"></a>
#### Identifying arguments in texts

`rdc.argument-identification` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Locate and reconstruct a writer's argument by identifying the main claim, the reasons and evidence offered, intermediate conclusions and the counter-arguments addressed, distinguishing argument from description, explanation and background. Reconstructing an argument accurately is the precondition for evaluating it and for representing it fairly in one's own writing.

Prerequisites: [Precise logical relations](#cohconnector-precision) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="rdcevidence-evaluation"></a>
#### Evaluating evidence in texts

`rdc.evidence-evaluation` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Judge whether the evidence a text offers is relevant to its claims, sufficient for their scope and of adequate quality, taking into account the type of evidence (empirical data, statistics, expert opinion, cases, anecdote), its provenance and whether the reasoning from evidence to claim is sound. Evaluating evidence determines how far a source can be relied on and how it should be represented.

Prerequisites: [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types)

<a id="rdcassumptions"></a>
#### Identifying assumptions and values

`rdc.assumptions` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Surface the unstated premises, values and framings on which an argument depends, including the assumptions that link evidence to claims, assumptions about definitions and scope, and value assumptions about what matters. Assumptions are often where disagreement between texts and disciplines really lies, and identifying them allows fair rather than superficial critique.

Prerequisites: [Making inferences](#rdsinference) (soft), [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification), [Toulmin model of argument](#argtoulmin) (soft)

<a id="rdcbias-perspective"></a>
#### Recognising bias and perspective

`rdc.bias-perspective` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise how a text's perspective is shaped by its author's position, interests, funding, disciplinary or theoretical commitments and intended audience, and how this shows in the selection of evidence, framing and language. Recognising perspective is not the same as dismissing a text as biased: all texts are written from a position, so readers judge how that position affects reliability and which perspectives are missing, while also attending to their own biases as readers.

Prerequisites: [Identifying writer stance and attributed views](#rdswriter-stance), [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation) (soft), [Identifying assumptions and values](#rdcassumptions) (soft)

<a id="rdcmethods-critique"></a>
#### Reading methods critically

`rdc.methods-critique` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Evaluate the design of empirical studies at the level of an informed non-specialist: whether the sample and setting support the generalisations made, whether measures capture the construct, whether the design supports causal claims, how data were analysed and whether limitations are acknowledged. Criteria differ between quantitative and qualitative research and across disciplines; formal discipline-specific appraisal tools belong to dhl.critical-appraisal and to methods training.

Prerequisites: [Navigating research articles](#rdsresearch-article-navigation), [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation), [Interpreting statistical claims](#numinterpreting-statistics) (soft)

<a id="rdcmultiple-texts"></a>
#### Reading across multiple texts

`rdc.multiple-texts` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Read across several texts on a topic by attending to source information (who wrote it, when, where and for whom), identifying where texts agree, conflict or address different questions, corroborating claims, and building an integrated understanding that keeps track of which source said what. Multiple-text comprehension is distinct from single-text comprehension and is the reading basis of synthesis in writing.

Prerequisites: [Identifying writer stance and attributed views](#rdswriter-stance) (soft), [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation) (soft)

<a id="rdcreading-for-writing"></a>
#### Reading for a writing task

`rdc.reading-for-writing` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Select, read and organise sources around a specific writing task: analysing the question to set reading priorities, reading for what the task needs, noting material in usable form with full source details, and reading with the developing argument in mind. Most university writing is source-based, and both the efficiency and the integrity of source use depend on how the reading was done.

Prerequisites: [Managing reading load](#rdpmanaging-reading-load) (soft), [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis)

<a id="rdcliterature-mapping"></a>
#### Mapping a body of literature

`rdc.literature-mapping` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Map a body of literature by identifying its main themes, debates, methodological approaches, key authors and schools of thought, and how they relate to one another over time, so that one's own work can be positioned within the scholarly conversation. Mapping is iterative and moves the reader from accumulating sources to understanding the structure of a field.

Prerequisites: [Managing reading load](#rdpmanaging-reading-load) (soft), [Reading across multiple texts](#rdcmultiple-texts), [Citation chaining](#infcitation-chaining) (soft)

<a id="rdctheoretical-texts"></a>
#### Reading theoretical and conceptual texts

`rdc.theoretical-texts` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Read dense theoretical and conceptual texts, common in the humanities and social sciences, by identifying key concepts and how the author defines them, reconstructing the argument at the level of ideas, relating abstractions to examples, and situating the text within the debate it addresses. Theoretical prose combines high abstraction, nominalisation and specialised senses of everyday words, and usually requires more than one reading.

Prerequisites: [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy) (soft), [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences), [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification) (soft)

<a id="rdccritical-questions"></a>
#### Generating critical questions

`rdc.critical-questions` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Generate and apply a set of critical questions to interrogate a text systematically: its purpose and audience, its main claims, the evidence and reasoning behind them, its assumptions and perspective, what it omits, and its implications and usefulness for the reader's purpose. Question sets are scaffolds to be internalised and adapted to disciplinary norms, not checklists to be completed mechanically.

Prerequisites: [What critical reading involves](#rdccritical-reading), [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification) (soft), [Critical questioning](#crtquestioning) (soft)

<a id="rdccritical-response"></a>
#### Formulating a critical response

`rdc.critical-response` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Move from comprehension and analysis to a reasoned evaluation of a text: state a position on its claims, value or usefulness, support it with reasons and evidence from analysis of the text and other sources, and express it with appropriate qualification. A critical response is neither a summary nor an unsupported opinion but an argued judgement that can be defended in discussion or writing.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation), [Identifying assumptions and values](#rdcassumptions) (soft), [Generating critical questions](#rdccritical-questions)

## Academic Listening

### Lecture & Academic Listening

Academic listening is modelled here as two interacting sets of processes: decoding, which turns the acoustic signal into words and clauses in real time, and meaning-building, which relates what has been decoded to context, discourse structure, speaker purpose and prior knowledge. The domain moves from perceptual topics (connected speech, segmentation, speech rate, accent) through lecture discourse (phases, organisational and relevance markers, main points, stance) to the settings in which students listen (live and recorded lectures, seminars, briefings, academic media), with metacognitive strategy running throughout. Listening is treated as something to be taught, not only tested, and accent variation is treated as normal in international universities rather than as deviation from a single native-speaker norm.

<a id="lislistening-processes"></a>
#### Listening processes: decoding and meaning-building

`lis.listening-processes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Understand listening as the real-time interaction of decoding processes (perceiving sounds and syllables, recognising words, parsing clauses) and meaning-building processes (enriching what is decoded with context, background knowledge and discourse structure, and deciding what matters). The distinction lets learners and teachers locate where comprehension breaks down instead of treating 'I didn't understand' as a single problem with a single remedy.

<a id="lisconnected-speech"></a>
#### Recognising connected speech

`lis.connected-speech` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise words whose form in natural speech differs from their citation form because of weak forms, elision, assimilation, linking and other reductions, which affect function words and frequent phrases most. Without this, learners fail to recognise words they already know in writing, and a few missed function words can make a whole clause impossible to parse.

Prerequisites: [High-frequency vocabulary base](#vochigh-frequency-base), [Listening processes: decoding and meaning-building](#lislistening-processes) (soft)

<a id="lisword-segmentation"></a>
#### Segmenting the speech stream

`lis.word-segmentation` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Identify where words begin and end in continuous speech, using stressed (strong) syllables, which in English often mark the start of content words, together with lexical knowledge, grammar and context to confirm or revise a hypothesis. Segmentation errors (hearing 'a notion' as 'an ocean') produce misrecognitions that then mislead interpretation of the rest of the utterance.

Prerequisites: [Recognising connected speech](#lisconnected-speech), [Word stress in academic vocabulary](#prnword-stress) (soft)

<a id="lisspeech-rate"></a>
#### Coping with natural speech rate

`lis.speech-rate` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Follow lectures and discussion delivered at natural, unscripted rates by building automatic word recognition through repeated exposure and by using compensatory strategies (continuing past gaps, using restatement and redundancy, prioritising) when decoding falls behind. A sense that speech is 'too fast' often reflects decoding that is not yet automatic rather than simply the speaker's rate.

Prerequisites: [Segmenting the speech stream](#lisword-segmentation), [Metacognitive listening strategies](#lismetacognitive-listening) (soft)

<a id="lisaccent-variation"></a>
#### Understanding varied accents

`lis.accent-variation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Adapt to the range of accents used by lecturers, supervisors and peers in international universities, including regional native-speaker varieties and the many accents of English used as a lingua franca. Comprehension of an unfamiliar accent typically improves with exposure, and treating varied accents as normal rather than as departures from one standard is part of listening competence and of participating on equal terms.

Prerequisites: [Segmenting the speech stream](#lisword-segmentation) (soft), [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

<a id="lislecture-structure"></a>
#### Lecture structure and phases

`lis.lecture-structure` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Recognise the recurring phases of university lectures — discourse structuring (previewing, reviewing, moving on), content, exemplification, conclusion, evaluation and interaction — and that these phases interleave and recur rather than following a fixed sequence. Knowing the typical shape of a lecture lets listeners anticipate what kind of information is coming and allocate attention accordingly.

Prerequisites: [Listening processes: decoding and meaning-building](#lislistening-processes) (soft), [Teaching and learning formats](#acuteaching-formats) (soft)

<a id="lislecture-discourse-markers"></a>
#### Lecture discourse markers

`lis.lecture-discourse-markers` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Recognise the macro-markers that signal a lecture's larger organisation (what I want to do today is…, let's turn now to…) and the micro-markers that segment and link ideas locally (so, now, right, OK, and then), and use them in real time to track where the lecture is and predict what comes next. Spoken markers are often unstressed and reduced, and organisation is also signalled by pausing and pitch, so recognising structure is partly a decoding task.

Prerequisites: [Signposting in spoken academic discourse](#cohspoken-signposting) (soft), [Lecture structure and phases](#lislecture-structure)

<a id="lisrelevance-markers"></a>
#### Emphasis and relevance markers

`lis.relevance-markers` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise the lexicogrammatical and prosodic means by which lecturers flag what matters: imperatives (remember that…, note that…), evaluative nouns and adjectives (the key point, crucially), explicit references to assessment, and emphatic stress, slowing, pausing and repetition. Relevance markers also flag content as less important (you don't need to know the details), so they guide what listeners prioritise, note and review.

Prerequisites: [Lecture discourse markers](#lislecture-discourse-markers) (soft), [Sentence stress and prominence](#prnprominence) (soft)

<a id="lisdigressions"></a>
#### Handling digressions and asides

`lis.digressions` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise when a lecturer departs from the main thread — for an anecdote, joke, personal aside, administrative notice or tangential example — judge whether the departure illustrates a main point or is incidental, and recognise the return to the topic. This stops learners mistaking asides for core content or losing the thread altogether.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main points and supporting detail](#lismain-points)

<a id="lismain-points"></a>
#### Identifying main points and supporting detail

`lis.main-points` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Select the central claims, concepts and organising points of an extended stretch of academic speech and distinguish them from elaboration, examples, evidence and restatement, in real time and without the option of re-reading. This selection underpins lecture note-taking, oral summaries and integrated listening-to-writing tasks.

Prerequisites: [Core academic vocabulary](#voccore-academic-vocabulary) (soft), [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft), [Coping with natural speech rate](#lisspeech-rate) (soft), [Emphasis and relevance markers](#lisrelevance-markers)

<a id="lispre-listening-preparation"></a>
#### Preparing for lectures

`lis.pre-listening-preparation` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Prepare for a lecture by previewing slides, module outlines and set readings, activating and building relevant background knowledge, predicting content and organisation, and pre-learning key terms including how they sound. Preparation reduces real-time processing load so that more attention is available for following the argument, provided predictions are checked rather than trusted.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge), [Previewing and surveying](#rdspreviewing) (soft)

<a id="lisspeaker-stance"></a>
#### Inferring speaker stance

`lis.speaker-stance` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Infer a lecturer's or speaker's evaluation of the ideas they present — endorsement, scepticism, criticism, irony, degree of certainty — from stance expressions, hedges and boosters, the framing of reported views, and prosody, and distinguish the speaker's own position from positions they report. This matters most where lectures present contested theories and evidence rather than settled facts.

Prerequisites: [Expressing stance in speech](#staspoken-stance) (soft), [Identifying main points and supporting detail](#lismain-points), [Intonation for discourse functions](#prnintonation) (soft)

<a id="lismulti-party-discussion"></a>
#### Following multi-party discussion

`lis.multi-party-discussion` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Follow seminar, tutorial and group discussions by tracking who is speaking, what position each participant takes, how each contribution responds to earlier ones, and where the discussion has reached, despite overlapping talk, varied accents and little explicit signposting. Following the discussion is a precondition for contributing relevantly to it.

Prerequisites: [Understanding varied accents](#lisaccent-variation) (soft), [Inferring speaker stance](#lisspeaker-stance), [Purposes and expectations of seminars](#semseminar-purposes) (soft)

<a id="lismultimodal-lectures"></a>
#### Integrating slides, visuals and speech

`lis.multimodal-lectures` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Coordinate attention between the lecturer's speech and what is shown — slides, diagrams, data displays, equations, demonstrations, board work — resolving spoken references to visuals (this curve here, as you can see) and integrating the two channels rather than reading slides at the expense of listening. Lecture content is often distributed across modes, so neither channel alone is sufficient.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main points and supporting detail](#lismain-points), [Preparing for lectures](#lispre-listening-preparation) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs) (soft)

<a id="lisrecorded-lectures"></a>
#### Using lecture capture strategically

`lis.recorded-lectures` · STRATEGIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use lecture recordings, automatic captions and transcripts as targeted review and repair tools — replaying sections marked as difficult, checking key terms and completing notes — rather than as a substitute for attending or a reason to rewatch whole lectures passively. Strategic use depends on knowing what was missed and why.

Prerequisites: [Metacognitive listening strategies](#lismetacognitive-listening), [Taking notes in lectures](#ntklecture-notes) (soft), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques) (soft)

<a id="lismetacognitive-listening"></a>
#### Metacognitive listening strategies

`lis.metacognitive-listening` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Plan for, monitor, solve problems during and evaluate listening, drawing on knowledge of oneself as a listener, of the demands of listening tasks and of the strategies available. Metacognitive regulation lets learners direct their own listening development beyond the classroom, but it complements rather than replaces practice in decoding.

Prerequisites: [Listening processes: decoding and meaning-building](#lislistening-processes), [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="lisspoken-instructions"></a>
#### Understanding spoken instructions and briefings

`lis.spoken-instructions` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand spoken instructions, assignment briefings, laboratory and fieldwork safety briefings and administrative announcements well enough to act on them correctly, attending to sequence, conditions, deadlines and the strength of obligation, and checking understanding against written sources. Errors here have direct practical, safety and assessment consequences.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology), [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [Recognising connected speech](#lisconnected-speech) (soft)

<a id="lisacademic-media"></a>
#### Listening to academic media

`lis.academic-media` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use academic podcasts, recorded conference talks, public lectures and documentaries as sources of knowledge: judging their credibility, following conversational, interview and panel formats that are less structured than lectures, extracting content relevant to a purpose, and recording details (speaker, date, platform, timestamps) so the material can be cited. Such media are increasingly set as course materials and used for independent study.

Prerequisites: [Understanding varied accents](#lisaccent-variation) (soft), [Identifying main points and supporting detail](#lismain-points), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation) (soft)

### Note-taking & Note-making

The domain treats note-taking (recording from lectures in real time) and note-making (building notes from reading and from later review) as distinct but connected practices. Following Kiewra (1985), notes serve both an encoding function, through the processing involved in taking them, and an external-storage function for later review, and the review function often matters more; the domain therefore covers selection, shorthand and format, lecture and reading notes, review, digital tools and collaboration. Claims about particular formats, or about longhand versus laptop notes, are presented with their evidential limits.

<a id="ntkfunctions-of-notes"></a>
#### Functions of notes

`ntk.functions-of-notes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Understand that notes serve two functions: taking them can promote encoding, as the note-taker selects, condenses and connects ideas, and the notes themselves are an external store for later review. Research suggests the review function often contributes more to learning, so notes must be complete and usable later, and the way they are taken affects how much processing occurs.

<a id="ntkselecting-content"></a>
#### Selecting what to note

`ntk.selecting-content` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Decide what to record — main ideas and the relationships between them, key terms and definitions, essential examples and evidence, and the sources mentioned — rather than attempting to transcribe, adjusting the selection to the purpose the notes will serve. Selection is the central intellectual act of note-taking and depends on recognising the structure and priorities of the input.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas), [Functions of notes](#ntkfunctions-of-notes) (soft)

<a id="ntkabbreviations-symbols"></a>
#### Abbreviations and symbols

`ntk.abbreviations-symbols` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Use conventional abbreviations and symbols (e.g., i.e., cf., →, ∴, ≠, ↑, ≈) and a consistent personal shorthand, including abbreviations for recurrent subject terms, to record quickly while keeping notes decodable later. Symbols for logical relations (cause, consequence, contrast, change) let notes preserve reasoning rather than just topics.

Prerequisites: [Academic abbreviations and Latin expressions](#vocabbreviations-latin) (soft), [Linking adverbials and connectors](#cohlinking-adverbials) (soft), [Functions of notes](#ntkfunctions-of-notes) (soft)

<a id="ntknote-formats"></a>
#### Note formats

`ntk.note-formats` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Choose and use note formats — indented linear outlines, Cornell-style pages with cue and summary areas, concept or mind maps, tables and matrices, flow diagrams — according to the structure of the input and the use the notes will serve. Format choice can make visible relationships that linear transcription obscures, but no single format is best for all purposes.

Prerequisites: [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns) (soft), [Selecting what to note](#ntkselecting-content)

<a id="ntklecture-notes"></a>
#### Taking notes in lectures

`ntk.lecture-notes` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Take notes during live or recorded lectures that capture the lecture's structure, main points, key terms, the content the lecturer flags as important, and the sources mentioned, in real time and with minimal loss of attention to the speaker. For most students, lecture notes are the main record of oral content for revision and assignments.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main points and supporting detail](#lismain-points), [Abbreviations and symbols](#ntkabbreviations-symbols) (soft), [Note formats](#ntknote-formats)

<a id="ntknotes-from-reading"></a>
#### Making notes from reading

`ntk.notes-from-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Make notes from academic reading that record full source details with page or location numbers and keep three kinds of content visibly separate: direct quotation (marked as such), condensed notes in one's own words, and one's own comments, questions and evaluations. Well-made reading notes are the main practical safeguard against unintentional plagiarism and the raw material for synthesis.

Prerequisites: [Purposeful annotation](#rdsannotation) (soft), [Selecting what to note](#ntkselecting-content), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft)

<a id="ntkreviewing-notes"></a>
#### Reviewing and reworking notes

`ntk.reviewing-notes` · STRATEGIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Review and rework notes after a lecture or reading through generative activities — completing gaps, summarising, generating and answering questions, reorganising into another format and self-testing at spaced intervals — rather than rereading or recopying them. Review is what turns notes from a record into learning.

Prerequisites: [Note formats](#ntknote-formats) (soft), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques)

<a id="ntkdigital-notes"></a>
#### Digital and handwritten note-taking

`ntk.digital-notes` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Choose between handwritten, typed, stylus and app-based note-taking according to the task, the content (for example equations or diagrams) and personal effectiveness, use devices in ways that avoid verbatim transcription and multitasking, and organise digital notes so they can be found and reused. The evidence on laptop versus longhand notes is mixed, so the choice should rest on how notes are taken rather than on the device alone.

Prerequisites: [Taking notes in lectures](#ntklecture-notes), [File and version management](#digfile-management) (soft)

<a id="ntkcollaborative-notes"></a>
#### Collaborative notes

`ntk.collaborative-notes` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Co-construct and share notes in study groups — agreeing a common format, dividing responsibilities, cross-checking accuracy and pooling gaps — while keeping individual processing and staying within institutional rules on collusion where notes feed into individually assessed work. Shared notes can fill individual gaps but can also spread errors and reduce the processing that taking notes provides.

Prerequisites: [Note formats](#ntknote-formats), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft), [Digital collaboration tools](#digdigital-collaboration) (soft)

## Academic Speaking

### Seminars & Discussion

Seminars, tutorials and discussion groups are where students are expected to co-construct understanding of readings and ideas in real time, and where participation is often assessed formally or informally. This domain treats discussion as negotiated, multi-turn interaction rather than a list of functional phrases: learners need to prepare substantively, find and hold turns, state and justify positions, mitigate disagreement, build on and summarise others' contributions and, at higher levels, facilitate. Participation is also shaped by anxiety, positioning and the way tutors and peers distribute the floor, so the domain addresses willingness to communicate and the conditions for participation, not only individual skill.

<a id="semseminar-purposes"></a>
#### Purposes and expectations of seminars

`sem.seminar-purposes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Explain what seminars, tutorials and discussion classes are for in a given programme — co-constructing understanding of readings and concepts, testing ideas and showing engagement — and how participation is expected, and sometimes assessed. Because these expectations are largely tacit and vary by discipline, level, class size and tutor, knowing them helps learners interpret silence, interruption and critique and decide how to contribute.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="sempreparation"></a>
#### Preparing for seminars

`sem.preparation` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Prepare for a seminar by reading the set texts with the discussion questions in mind, noting key claims with page references, reactions and points of uncertainty, and planning two or three possible contributions and questions in note form. Preparation reduces real-time processing load so that learners can attend to the discussion and enter it with substantive content rather than summary.

Prerequisites: [Generating critical questions](#rdccritical-questions) (soft), [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Purposes and expectations of seminars](#semseminar-purposes)

<a id="semturn-taking"></a>
#### Turn-taking and taking the floor

`sem.turn-taking` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Enter, hold and yield turns in multi-party academic discussion by recognising likely transition points, using brief floor-claiming and floor-holding moves, and judging when overlap or interruption is acceptable in a particular group. Because turns in student discussion are often taken by self-selection rather than allocated by the tutor, learners who wait to be invited may have few opportunities to contribute.

Prerequisites: [Following multi-party discussion](#lismulti-party-discussion), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="semopinions"></a>
#### Expressing and justifying views

`sem.opinions` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

State a position on a question or reading clearly and support it with reasons, examples or evidence, using language for stating positions, giving reasons and exemplifying, and signalling how firmly the view is held. In seminars both unsupported assertion and unpositioned summary are weak contributions; a short claim–reason–support move that can be extended under questioning is the basic unit of contribution.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="semagreeing-disagreeing"></a>
#### Agreeing and disagreeing

`sem.agreeing-disagreeing` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Agree, partially agree and disagree with peers, tutors and authors in ways that make the point of difference explicit while maintaining collegial relationships, typically by acknowledging the other position, locating the specific difference and giving a reason, with mitigation adjusted to the relationship and the stakes. Seminars value disagreement about ideas, but bald disagreement can damage relationships and over-hedged disagreement can obscure the speaker's position.

Prerequisites: [Expressing stance in speech](#staspoken-stance), [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="sembuilding-on-others"></a>
#### Building on others' contributions

`sem.building-on-others` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Link a contribution explicitly to what others have said by extending, qualifying, exemplifying, connecting or contrasting their points, and attribute those points to the speaker. Building on others is how ideas are co-constructed across turns in seminars, and it makes listening visible as well as thinking.

Prerequisites: [Turn-taking and taking the floor](#semturn-taking) (soft), [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft)

<a id="semquestions-clarification"></a>
#### Asking questions and seeking clarification

`sem.questions-clarification` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Formulate questions that seek clarification, elaboration, evidence or implications from tutors and peers, pinpointing which term, claim or step the question concerns and choosing direct or mitigated forms suited to the relationship. Asking questions is both a comprehension-repair strategy and a legitimate form of contribution, and subject lecturers have identified it as an oral demand that multilingual students can find difficult.

Prerequisites: [Metacognitive listening strategies](#lismetacognitive-listening) (soft), [Critical questioning](#crtquestioning) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="semchecking-understanding"></a>
#### Checking and confirming understanding

`sem.checking-understanding` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Confirm mutual understanding during discussion by paraphrasing others' points for confirmation, checking that one's own point has been understood, and reformulating it with simpler wording, an example or a definition when it has not. In multilingual groups whose members differ in accent, proficiency and disciplinary background, explicit confirmation and reformulation are routine collaborative moves rather than signs of weakness.

Prerequisites: [Asking questions and seeking clarification](#semquestions-clarification), [Self-repair and reformulation](#prnrepair) (soft)

<a id="semciting-in-speech"></a>
#### Referring to readings and evidence in discussion

`sem.citing-in-speech` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Refer to set readings, data and other sources in discussion with brief spoken attribution (author or text, the relevant point and, where useful, a page or section), keeping the source's claim distinct from one's own interpretation. Oral attribution makes contributions checkable, shows engagement with the reading and lets peers locate the passage under discussion.

Prerequisites: [Preparing for seminars](#sempreparation) (soft), [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Signal phrases and attribution](#srcsignal-phrases)

<a id="semfacilitating"></a>
#### Facilitating discussion

`sem.facilitating` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Lead or chair a discussion by framing the task with an open question, inviting and distributing contributions, probing and redirecting, signalling stages and time, and closing with a fair summary. Facilitation is commonly assessed in student-led seminars and is a mediation skill that requires attending to others' participation and the development of ideas as much as to one's own content.

Prerequisites: [Summarising discussion](#semsummarising-discussion), [Inclusive collaboration in multilingual groups](#grwinclusive-collaboration) (soft)

<a id="semsummarising-discussion"></a>
#### Summarising discussion

`sem.summarising-discussion` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Summarise a discussion orally or in brief notes by selecting the main positions, points of agreement and disagreement, unresolved questions and outcomes, organising them thematically and attributing them fairly. Summarising consolidates co-constructed understanding and is required when groups report back to a class or when a facilitator closes a discussion.

Prerequisites: [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Selecting what to note](#ntkselecting-content) (soft), [Building on others' contributions](#sembuilding-on-others)

<a id="semspeaking-anxiety"></a>
#### Managing speaking anxiety and willingness to communicate

`sem.speaking-anxiety` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand speaking anxiety and willingness to communicate (WTC) as situational and changeable — shaped by preparation, task familiarity, group relations, how the floor is distributed and perceived evaluation — and use preparation and in-the-moment strategies to participate. Treating reticence as a product of conditions rather than a fixed personal or cultural trait lets learners and teachers change the conditions as well as the behaviour.

Prerequisites: [Purposes and expectations of seminars](#semseminar-purposes) (soft), [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="semonline-discussion"></a>
#### Synchronous online discussion

`sem.online-discussion` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Participate in synchronous online and hybrid seminars by using raise-hand, chat and reaction functions to bid for and supplement turns, compensating for reduced gaze and gesture and for audio delay with explicit addressing, yielding and confirmation, and organising breakout-room tasks. Online discussion changes how turns are taken and how participation is made visible, so face-to-face strategies need adapting.

Prerequisites: [Turn-taking and taking the floor](#semturn-taking), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft), [Digital etiquette and online presence](#digdigital-etiquette) (soft)

<a id="semdebate"></a>
#### Structured academic debate

`sem.debate` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Take part in structured academic debates by building a case within a time limit, anticipating and rebutting the strongest opposing arguments, conceding where necessary and responding concisely to challenges. Debate trains rapid oral argument, but its adversarial logic differs from the exploratory, idea-developing norms of most seminars, and learners need to know when each mode is appropriate.

Prerequisites: [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession), [Arguing orally](#argoral-argument), [Exploratory and adversarial argument](#argexploratory-adversarial) (soft)

### Presentations

Academic presentations range from short assessed talks on foundation and pre-sessional programmes to conference papers, posters and research seminars at doctoral level.
