This domain treats the presentation as a spoken, multimodal genre designed for listeners who process information once, in real time: it needs explicit structure and signposting, visuals that support rather than duplicate speech, intelligible prosody and a delivery mode between reading a script and improvising. It gives particular attention to the interactive phases — openings and question-and-answer sessions — which research on conference presentations shows are central to how presenters engage audiences and establish credibility.

<a id="prspurpose-audience"></a>
#### Purpose and audience analysis

`prs.purpose-audience` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Analyse the purpose of a presentation (to inform, explain, persuade, report research or propose) and the audience's prior knowledge, expectations and relationship to the speaker, and adapt content selection, technical density, register and interaction accordingly. Audience analysis drives later decisions about structure, visuals and delivery, and is what distinguishes a presentation from a reading of an essay.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft)

<a id="prsstructure"></a>
#### Structuring a presentation

`prs.structure` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Organise a presentation into an introduction, body and conclusion with an explicit roadmap, a limited number of main points, internal summaries and a conclusion that returns to the purpose, designed for listeners who cannot re-read. Spoken structure needs more redundancy and explicit signalling than written text, so an essay cannot simply be read or summarised section by section.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written), [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience), [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining) (soft)

<a id="prsopenings"></a>
#### Opening a presentation

`prs.openings` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Open a presentation by establishing context and relevance, engaging the audience, stating the purpose or central claim and previewing the structure, selecting and ordering these moves to suit the audience and genre. The opening sets the audience's interpretive frame and the speaker's credibility, and research on conference introductions shows that interpersonal engagement matters here as much as content.

Prerequisites: [Reader engagement](#staengagement-markers) (soft), [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure)

<a id="prssignposting"></a>
#### Signposting in presentations

`prs.signposting` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use spoken signposting to announce structure, mark transitions, highlight key points, summarise and conclude, combining lexical markers with prosodic signals such as pausing and pitch reset at boundaries. Signposting helps listeners build a mental map of the talk, but its density should match the length and audience of the talk, since mechanical or excessive markers distract.

Prerequisites: [Signposting in spoken academic discourse](#cohspoken-signposting), [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure)

<a id="prsslide-design"></a>
#### Designing slides

`prs.slide-design` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Design slides and other visual aids that support the spoken message: one main point per slide, headings that state that point, minimal text, readable type, purposeful and simplified figures, clear visual hierarchy, accessible colour and contrast, and attribution of images and data. Slides should complement speech rather than duplicate it, because dense slide text competes with the speaker for the audience's attention.

Prerequisites: [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure) (soft), [Creating effective visuals](#datcreating-visuals) (soft), [Accessible academic documents](#mmdaccessibility) (soft)

<a id="prsexplaining-visuals"></a>
#### Talking about visuals and data

`prs.explaining-visuals` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Guide an audience through a chart, table or diagram by orienting them to what it shows (type, axes, units, categories), highlighting the one to three features that matter, and stating their significance for the argument, while synchronising speech with pointing or slide builds. Audiences cannot study a visual at their own pace, so the presenter must direct attention selectively.

Prerequisites: [Designing slides](#prsslide-design) (soft), [Saying numbers, formulae and symbols aloud](#prnnumbers-aloud) (soft), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary)

<a id="prsdelivery-voice"></a>
#### Vocal delivery

`prs.delivery-voice` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Control pace, volume, pausing, thought groups, prominence and intonation so that a presentation is intelligible and easy to follow, prioritising the prosodic features that most affect listeners' understanding over accent reduction. Prosody signals structure, new and contrastive information, and the speaker's engagement with the audience.

Prerequisites: [Sentence stress and prominence](#prnprominence), [Intonation for discourse functions](#prnintonation) (soft)

<a id="prsnonverbal"></a>
#### Non-verbal communication

`prs.nonverbal` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use gaze, facial expression, gesture, posture and position in the room to support the spoken message, direct attention to visuals and connect with the audience, adapting to the setting and to one's own physical and neurological needs. Non-verbal norms vary across contexts and individuals, so the aim is purposeful, non-distracting use rather than conformity to a single model of 'confident' body language.

Prerequisites: [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience) (soft), [Intercultural communication](#idnintercultural-communication) (soft)

<a id="prsspeaking-from-notes"></a>
#### Speaking from notes

`prs.speaking-from-notes` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Deliver a presentation from brief prompts (key words, cue cards, slide notes) rather than reading a full script or reciting a memorised text, using the notes to retrieve structure and key wording while formulating sentences in the moment, and scripting only selected high-risk segments. Read-aloud written prose is lexically dense and usually prosodically flat, which makes it hard for listeners to process.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written), [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure) (soft), [Developing speech fluency](#prnfluency) (soft)

<a id="prshandling-questions"></a>
#### Handling questions

`prs.handling-questions` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Manage question-and-answer sessions by listening to the whole question, clarifying or reformulating it, answering concisely and relevantly, acknowledging limits honestly, and deferring or redirecting when appropriate, including when the turn is a critical comment rather than a question. Q&A is the least predictable part of a presentation and a key site for demonstrating understanding and establishing credibility.

Prerequisites: [Expressing stance in speech](#staspoken-stance) (soft), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding), [Managing speaking anxiety and willingness to communicate](#semspeaking-anxiety) (soft)

<a id="prsgroup-presentations"></a>
#### Group presentations

`prs.group-presentations` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Plan and deliver a presentation as a group by agreeing a single message and structure, allocating sections by content rather than by person, making handovers that link sections, keeping slides, terminology and referencing consistent, and sharing the Q&A. Assessed group presentations test coordination as well as individual delivery, and a poorly integrated talk often reveals divided rather than shared planning.

Prerequisites: [Signposting in presentations](#prssignposting), [Rehearsal and timing](#prsrehearsal-timing) (soft), [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning)

<a id="prsposters"></a>
#### Poster presentations

`prs.posters` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Present an academic poster by preparing short and longer spoken overviews, gauging each visitor's background and interest, guiding them through the poster's reading path, and sustaining a one-to-one or small-group discussion that includes questions, critique and follow-up. Poster sessions are interactive and conversational, and much of a poster's value lies in the conversations it supports.

Prerequisites: [Talking about visuals and data](#prsexplaining-visuals) (soft), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Designing academic posters](#mmdposter-design)

<a id="prsresearch-presentations"></a>
#### Research and conference presentations

`prs.research-presentations` · GENRE · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Present research to specialist audiences in conference papers, research seminars and dissertation or progression presentations, framing the niche and contribution early, selecting findings and visuals that carry a clear take-home message, keeping to strict time limits and engaging with the discussion session. These presentations socialise researchers into disciplinary communities, and their introductions and discussion phases are particularly consequential for credibility.

Prerequisites: [Opening a presentation](#prsopenings), [Talking about visuals and data](#prsexplaining-visuals), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Rehearsal and timing](#prsrehearsal-timing), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche) (soft)

<a id="prsrecorded-online"></a>
#### Recorded and online presentations

`prs.recorded-online` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Produce recorded and live online presentations by planning for microphone, camera and screen-sharing, maintaining spoken-style delivery and audience engagement without live feedback, editing within the assessment's rules, and providing accurate captions or transcripts. Recorded formats invite scripting and repeated re-recording, so learners need to manage naturalness, authenticity and compliance with task regulations.

Prerequisites: [Designing slides](#prsslide-design) (soft), [Vocal delivery](#prsdelivery-voice), [Speaking from notes](#prsspeaking-from-notes) (soft)

<a id="prsrehearsal-timing"></a>
#### Rehearsal and timing

`prs.rehearsal-timing` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Rehearse a presentation aloud, with timing and under realistic conditions, using recordings and peer feedback to refine content, transitions and delivery, and adjust content in advance so that it fits the time limit. Rehearsal reduces the planning and formulation load of live delivery and is the main safeguard against overrunning.

Prerequisites: [Structuring a presentation](#prsstructure), [Developing speech fluency](#prnfluency) (soft), [Self-regulated learning](#lrnself-regulation) (soft)

### Pronunciation & Fluency

The domain adopts the intelligibility principle (Levis, 2005): the goal is speech that listeners can understand with reasonable effort, not a native-like accent. Building on Munro and Derwing's (1995) distinction between accentedness, comprehensibility and intelligibility, priority goes to the features most likely to affect understanding in academic speaking — word stress in academic vocabulary, thought groups and prominence, intonation for discourse functions, high-functional-load segmentals — together with fluency and repair. Pronunciation is linked to listening, since perception supports production, and to the presentations, seminars and teaching in which students use it; accent is treated as part of speakers' identity rather than as an error to be removed.

<a id="prnintelligibility"></a>
#### Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent

`prn.intelligibility` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Distinguish intelligibility (how much of a speaker's message a listener actually understands), comprehensibility (how easy the listener finds it to understand) and accentedness (how different the speech sounds from the listener's reference variety), and use the finding that these are partly independent to set pronunciation goals around being understood rather than sounding native. Intelligibility depends on listeners as well as speakers, and accent is part of a speaker's identity.

<a id="prnword-stress"></a>
#### Word stress in academic vocabulary

`prn.word-stress` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Place primary stress correctly on multi-syllable academic and technical words, including the shifts that occur across word families (ANalyse, aNALysis, anaLYtical) and the regularities associated with suffixes such as -ic, -ion, -ity and -ical, and reduce unstressed vowels appropriately. Misplaced word stress can cause listeners to misidentify words even when individual sounds are accurate.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology), [Academic affixes and Greco-Latin roots](#vocaffixes-roots) (soft), [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft)

<a id="prnprominence"></a>
#### Sentence stress and prominence

`prn.prominence` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Place prominence (nuclear or focal stress) on the word that carries new or contrastive information in each thought group, and de-accent information that is already given, so that listeners can identify what is important and what is being contrasted. Misplaced or absent prominence can lead listeners to misunderstand the point and to recall less of an explanation.

Prerequisites: [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new), [Word stress in academic vocabulary](#prnword-stress), [Thought groups and pausing](#prnthought-groups)

<a id="prnthought-groups"></a>
#### Thought groups and pausing

`prn.thought-groups` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Divide speech into thought groups — short, meaningful units usually corresponding to grammatical phrases or clauses — with pauses at group boundaries rather than within them and one main prominence in each group. Thought groups help listeners parse speech and give speakers natural planning points; pauses inside phrases make speech harder to follow.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft), [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft)

<a id="prnintonation"></a>
#### Intonation for discourse functions

`prn.intonation` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use pitch movement for discourse purposes in academic speaking: falling tones to present new information and close points, rising or fall-rise tones to refer to shared information, signal continuation or invite response, and pitch range and key to mark new topics and contrasts. Tone choice helps listeners follow organisation and affects how interactive and approachable a speaker sounds.

Prerequisites: [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Sentence stress and prominence](#prnprominence)

<a id="prnsegmental-priorities"></a>
#### Prioritised segmental features

`prn.segmental-priorities` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Identify and work on the consonant and vowel features in one's own speech that most affect intelligibility — typically contrasts with high functional load, word-final consonants and clusters that carry grammatical meaning, and vowel length or quality contrasts — rather than trying to perfect every sound. Priorities are individual: they depend on the speaker's other languages, on the listeners and on which features actually cause misunderstanding.

Prerequisites: [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility)

<a id="prnconnected-speech-production"></a>
#### Connected speech in production

`prn.connected-speech-production` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use linking within thought groups, weak forms of function words where they are unstressed, and natural reductions so that speech flows and stressed information stands out, while keeping content words and key terms clear. Moderate use of connected speech supports fluent delivery; how far full reduction helps intelligibility, particularly in lingua franca settings, is contested.

Prerequisites: [Recognising connected speech](#lisconnected-speech) (soft), [Sentence stress and prominence](#prnprominence) (soft)

<a id="prntechnical-terms"></a>
#### Pronouncing technical terms and names

`prn.technical-terms` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Find, check and practise the pronunciation of discipline-specific terms, proper names (theorists, places, organisations) and acronyms before using them in speech, recognising when an abbreviation is said as a word (UNESCO) or as letters (DNA) and that disciplinary communities may have their own conventions. Because key terms recur throughout an explanation, a mispronounced term can disrupt understanding of the whole.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Word stress in academic vocabulary](#prnword-stress)

<a id="prnfluency"></a>
#### Developing speech fluency

`prn.fluency` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Develop speech that flows at a rate appropriate to the audience, with pauses mainly at natural boundaries, few long hesitations inside clauses and efficient recovery, through planning, formulaic language and repeated practice. Fluency here concerns the listener's experience of smooth delivery and the speaker's processing efficiency, not speed for its own sake.

Prerequisites: [Spoken academic formulas](#phrspoken-academic-formulas) (soft), [Thought groups and pausing](#prnthought-groups) (soft), [Self-repair and reformulation](#prnrepair) (soft)

<a id="prnrepair"></a>
#### Self-repair and reformulation

`prn.repair` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Notice when one's own speech has gone wrong or a listener has not understood, and repair efficiently: correcting errors that affect meaning, reformulating, and using compensatory strategies such as paraphrase, description or approximation when a word is unavailable. Effective repair keeps communication going without excessive self-correction of minor errors.

Prerequisites: [Code glosses and exemplification](#stacode-glosses) (soft), [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft)

<a id="prnnumbers-aloud"></a>
#### Saying numbers, formulae and symbols aloud

`prn.numbers-aloud` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Say numbers and quantities, formulae, units, dates and symbols aloud accurately and intelligibly — large numbers and decimals, fractions and percentages, ranges and approximations, equations and units — following the conventions of one's discipline, and use stress to keep confusable numbers apart (thirTEEN versus THIRty). Errors with numbers in presentations and seminars can mislead listeners about findings.

Prerequisites: [Word stress in academic vocabulary](#prnword-stress) (soft), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions), [Language of mathematical expression](#nummathematical-expression) (soft)

### Group Work & Collaboration

Group projects, assessed group presentations and collaborative reports are common across disciplines, yet students often receive little guidance on how to collaborate, and multilingual students can be positioned as less competent and allocated marginal roles. This domain covers why universities assess collaboration and how groups plan and allocate work, run meetings, negotiate disagreement, write together, and document and evaluate contributions. It treats equitable participation as a shared responsibility of all group members and of course design, not as a deficit of particular students.

<a id="grwpurposes"></a>
#### Purposes of group work

`grw.purposes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Explain why universities use group work and assessed collaboration — for example to develop teamwork, to tackle tasks too large for individuals and to bring together diverse perspectives — and identify what a given task assesses: the product, the process, individual contributions or a combination. Understanding purposes and assessment design helps learners set realistic expectations and treat group tasks as collaborative learning rather than as work to be divided and stapled together.

Prerequisites: [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="grwroles-planning"></a>
#### Roles and planning

`grw.roles-planning` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Plan a group project by analysing the task, agreeing ground rules (communication channels, response times, decision-making, quality standards), allocating roles and tasks on the basis of expertise and development goals, and setting milestones with internal deadlines, recorded in a shared place. Explicit early planning reduces later conflict and helps ensure that every member, including multilingual members, has a defined and substantive role.

Prerequisites: [Purposes of group work](#grwpurposes), [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft)

<a id="grwmeetings"></a>
#### Running group meetings

`grw.meetings` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Run and take part in group meetings by preparing and circulating an agenda, chairing or timekeeping, reaching and recording decisions, and producing brief minutes with action points, owners and deadlines that are followed up at the next meeting. Well-structured meetings make decisions and responsibilities visible and accountable, which matters when contributions are later assessed.

Prerequisites: [Collaborative notes](#ntkcollaborative-notes) (soft), [Facilitating discussion](#semfacilitating) (soft), [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning)

<a id="grwconflict"></a>
#### Managing disagreement and conflict

`grw.conflict` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Manage disagreement and conflict in group work by distinguishing disagreements about ideas from problems of process or contribution, raising concerns early and specifically with reference to agreed ground rules, negotiating solutions, and escalating to tutors through appropriate channels with evidence when internal resolution fails. Unaddressed problems tend to surface late, when options are limited.

Prerequisites: [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing), [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning) (soft), [Intercultural communication](#idnintercultural-communication) (soft)

<a id="grwinclusive-collaboration"></a>
#### Inclusive collaboration in multilingual groups

`grw.inclusive-collaboration` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Collaborate equitably in multilingual groups by accommodating to varied accents and proficiency levels, creating space for all members to contribute substantively, drawing on members' different linguistic, disciplinary and educational backgrounds, and challenging exclusionary patterns such as allocating tasks by assumed language ability. Equitable participation is a responsibility of every member, including the most proficient users of English, and supports the quality of the group's work.

Prerequisites: [Turn-taking and taking the floor](#semturn-taking) (soft), [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf)

<a id="grwcollaborative-writing"></a>
#### Collaborative writing

`grw.collaborative-writing` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Co-author a group report or document by agreeing a structure and style sheet, using shared documents with comments, suggestion mode and version history, integrating sections into a coherent whole with consistent terminology, voice and referencing, and revising the whole text together. Collaborative writing requires coordination of text, not only division of labour, and must respect the boundary between group work and individually assessed components.

Prerequisites: [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning), [Global revision](#edtglobal-revision) (soft), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft), [Digital collaboration tools](#digdigital-collaboration) (soft)

<a id="grwcontribution-assessment"></a>
#### Assessing contributions

`grw.contribution-assessment` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Evaluate one's own and peers' contributions to group work against agreed criteria, document one's own role with evidence (minutes, version history, task logs), and describe contributions accurately in peer-evaluation forms and individual reflections. Peer evaluation can affect individual marks and can reproduce biases, for example equating fluent talk with contribution, so criterion-referenced, evidenced judgement matters.

Prerequisites: [Running group meetings](#grwmeetings) (soft), [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing) (soft), [Self- and peer-assessment](#fbkself-peer-assessment)

## Academic Writing

### Writing Process

Covers how academic texts are produced rather than what they look like: representing the task, generating and organising content, drafting, writing from sources, and managing time, language choice and affect across extended projects. It draws on cognitive process models (Flower & Hayes, 1981; Bereiter & Scardamalia, 1987) and L2 composing research (Zamel, 1983), which show that skilled writers plan, draft and review recursively and use writing to develop ideas. Hyland (2003) argues that process pedagogy needs to be complemented by explicit attention to genre and reader expectations, so these topics should be taught alongside PAR, ESS and the genre strands rather than as a free-standing ‘process approach’.

<a id="wprwriting-process"></a>
#### Writing as a recursive process

`wpr.writing-process` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand academic writing as interleaved planning, translating (putting ideas into language) and reviewing, revisited recursively rather than performed once in sequence, and distinguish knowledge-telling (writing down what one knows in the order it is recalled) from knowledge-transforming (reworking content to meet rhetorical goals). The model explains why much of the thinking in academic writing happens during drafting and revision, and why early surface correction can be counter-productive.

<a id="wprtask-analysis"></a>
#### Analysing assignment tasks

`wpr.task-analysis` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Unpack an assignment brief or question to identify the command word(s), the topic and its limits, required sources, genre and reader, word limit and marking criteria, and turn these into a working statement of what the text must do. Task representation shapes every later writing decision, and misreading the task is a common, avoidable cause of low marks.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words), [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft)

<a id="wprgenerating-ideas"></a>
#### Generating ideas

`wpr.generating-ideas` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use deliberate techniques—brainstorming, focused freewriting, question heuristics, mapping and talking ideas through—to produce more and better content than first recall provides, and then select from it in the light of the task. Separating generation from evaluation helps writers get beyond the first, most obvious points.

Prerequisites: [Writing as a recursive process](#wprwriting-process) (soft), [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Critical questioning](#crtquestioning) (soft)

<a id="wprplanning-outlining"></a>
#### Planning and outlining

`wpr.planning-outlining` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Turn a position and selected evidence into a hierarchical plan that sequences points, attaches support to each and shows how each part advances the answer, in a format (linear outline, map, table) suited to the writer and task. A plan is a working hypothesis to be revised during drafting, not a contract.

Prerequisites: [Note formats](#ntknote-formats) (soft), [Generating ideas](#wprgenerating-ideas), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="wprdrafting"></a>
#### Drafting

`wpr.drafting` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Produce a complete first draft from a plan, keeping momentum by deferring sentence-level correction, marking gaps to fill later and allowing the argument to develop as it is written. Treating the first draft as provisional makes revision possible and reduces the paralysis of trying to write a final version first time.

Prerequisites: [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining) (soft), [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit) (soft)

<a id="wprreading-to-write"></a>
#### Integrating reading into writing

`wpr.reading-to-write` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Move from reading and notes to one's own text by selecting source content for its relevance to the task, organising it around one's own points rather than the sources' order, and connecting it with commentary. This discourse-synthesis process underlies most university writing, which is source-based rather than written from personal knowledge.

Prerequisites: [Reading for a writing task](#rdcreading-for-writing) (soft), [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing) (soft), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage) (soft)

<a id="wpraudience-purpose"></a>
#### Audience and purpose

`wpr.audience-purpose` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise that most assessed university writing addresses an expert reader who knows the content but expects the writer to display understanding, make reasoning explicit and argue a position, and adjust content, explicitness and register to that reader and to the text's purpose. Mismatched assumptions about the reader explain many ‘too descriptive’ or ‘explain this’ comments.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="wprmanaging-long-projects"></a>
#### Managing extended writing projects

`wpr.managing-long-projects` · STRATEGIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Plan and sustain projects of several thousand words or more by breaking them into dated milestones, budgeting words and time across sections, writing regularly rather than in a final burst, scheduling feedback, and tracking versions and progress. Extended projects such as pre-sessional research projects, dissertations and theses often falter through poor management rather than weak language.

Prerequisites: [Working within word limits](#wprword-limits) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management)

<a id="wprwriting-blocks"></a>
#### Overcoming writing blocks

`wpr.writing-blocks` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise likely causes of being unable to start or continue writing—perfectionism, rigid rules, an unclear task, anxiety about language or evaluation, fatigue—and apply matched strategies such as freewriting, generative writing, lowering the stakes of drafts, starting with an easier section or breaking the task down. Blocks are common at every level, including among experienced researchers, and are usually solvable.

Prerequisites: [Drafting](#wprdrafting) (soft), [Self-regulated learning](#lrnself-regulation) (soft)

<a id="wprl1-in-composing"></a>
#### Using the first language in composing

`wpr.l1-in-composing` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use the first language and other languages strategically while composing in English—for generating ideas, planning, note-making, checking meaning and solving lexical problems—while drafting the text predominantly in English and using machine translation transparently and within local rules. Plurilingual composing is a resource, but translating whole L1 drafts tends to carry over L1 structures, reduces L2 practice and can breach assessment rules.

Prerequisites: [Generating ideas](#wprgenerating-ideas) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

<a id="wprword-limits"></a>
#### Working within word limits

`wpr.word-limits` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Plan to a word limit by allocating words across sections in proportion to their importance, cut over-length drafts by removing low-value content before trimming sentences, expand under-length drafts by developing analysis rather than adding background, and follow local conventions on what counts (references, headings, tables, appendices) and on tolerances. Meeting a limit is a matter of prioritisation, not only concision.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining) (soft)

<a id="wprformatting"></a>
#### Formatting and presentation

`wpr.formatting` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Present a text according to the required conventions—style guide, departmental template, cover sheet, page layout, heading levels, numbering of figures and tables, font and spacing—and apply them consistently using word-processing styles. Presentation rarely carries much weight in marking, but it affects readability, the reader's first impression and sometimes compliance with submission rules.

Prerequisites: [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing) (soft)

### Paragraph Development

Covers the paragraph as the main unit through which academic writers develop and support a point: controlling ideas, support and analysis, internal coherence, closure, division, paragraph functions and the integration of sources. Paragraph scaffolds such as topic-sentence models and PEEL are useful entry points for foundation and pre-sessional writers, but expert paragraphs vary considerably (Braddock, 1974), so teaching should move learners from templates towards functional judgements about what a paragraph needs to do in its context. Coherence is better taught through information flow and logical relations than through connector lists (Crossley & McNamara, 2012).

<a id="parparagraph-unit"></a>
#### The academic paragraph

`par.paragraph-unit` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Understand the academic paragraph as a unit that typically develops one controlling idea, signalled to the reader and developed through explanation and evidence, and that serves the purpose of the whole text. The one-idea paragraph is a reliable default for planning and reading, although expert writers vary paragraph structure according to function and discipline.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft)

<a id="partopic-sentences"></a>
#### Writing topic sentences

`par.topic-sentences` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Write sentences that state a paragraph's controlling idea precisely enough to predict and constrain what follows, and that connect the paragraph to the thesis or the previous paragraph. Effective topic sentences make claims rather than announce topics, and read in sequence they give the reader an outline of the argument.

Prerequisites: [Abstract and shell nouns](#vocabstract-shell-nouns) (soft), [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit)

<a id="parsupport"></a>
#### Developing support

`par.support` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Develop a controlling idea with a suitable combination of explanation, reasoning, evidence, examples and analysis, choosing support that is relevant and sufficient for the strength of the claim and stating how it supports the claim. Paragraphs that assert without support, or pile up examples without explanation, are a frequent cause of ‘underdeveloped’ or ‘descriptive’ feedback.

Prerequisites: [Exemplifying and illustrating](#funexemplifying) (soft), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="parpeel"></a>
#### Point–evidence–explanation–link structures

`par.peel` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Use PEEL-type scaffolds (point, evidence, explanation, link, and variants such as PEE or PEEL-C) to construct supported paragraphs while learning, and recognise their limits: many paragraphs need several pieces of evidence, a counter-point or no explicit link, and mechanical application produces formulaic text. The aim is to internalise the functions the scaffold names and then write without it.

Prerequisites: [Developing support](#parsupport), [Concluding and linking sentences](#parconcluding-sentences) (soft)

<a id="parinternal-coherence"></a>
#### Internal paragraph coherence

`par.internal-coherence` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Order and connect the sentences of a paragraph so that each follows logically from the previous one and from the controlling idea, using given-before-new information structure, thematic progression, consistent reference and lexical chains, with explicit connectors where a logical relation needs signalling. Coherence depends on logic and information flow far more than on the number of linking words.

Prerequisites: [Given–new information structure](#cohgiven-new) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences)

<a id="parconcluding-sentences"></a>
#### Concluding and linking sentences

`par.concluding-sentences` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Close a paragraph, where useful, with a sentence that draws out the significance of its content for the argument, qualifies it, or prepares the next point, rather than repeating the topic sentence. Not every paragraph needs a separate concluding sentence; the choice depends on the paragraph's length and function.

Prerequisites: [Encapsulation with signalling nouns](#cohsignalling-nouns) (soft), [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences)

<a id="parlength-division"></a>
#### Paragraph length and division

`par.length-division` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Decide where to begin a new paragraph according to shifts in point, function or stage of the argument, and keep paragraphs long enough to develop an idea but short enough not to overload the reader, generally avoiding one-sentence paragraphs and page-long blocks in essays. Conventions vary with genre, discipline and medium.

Prerequisites: [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit)

<a id="parparagraph-functions"></a>
#### Paragraph functions

`par.paragraph-functions` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Recognise and write paragraphs that serve different functions within a text—introductory, body (argument, evidence, counter-argument), transitional and concluding—and understand that each function calls for different internal organisation and language. Treating every paragraph as a body paragraph produces weak introductions, conclusions and transitions.

Prerequisites: [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure)

<a id="parintegrating-evidence"></a>
#### Integrating sources in paragraphs

`par.integrating-evidence` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Integrate source material into a paragraph so that the writer's point leads, sources are introduced and attributed, and each piece of evidence is followed by commentary that interprets, evaluates or connects it (introduce–cite–comment). This keeps the writer's voice in control and distinguishes argument from a chain of summaries.

Prerequisites: [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution) (soft), [Developing support](#parsupport), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing) (soft), [Signal phrases and attribution](#srcsignal-phrases)

### Essay Construction

Covers how an essay is built around a central argument: structure, thesis, introductions, body organisation, conclusions, relevance to the question, essay types, depth of focus, criticality, headings and titles. The essay is a major assessed genre in many humanities and social science programmes but is only one of many genre families in university writing (Nesi & Gardner, 2012), and the five-paragraph essay taught in many schools and test-preparation courses is a scaffold that does not match university essays in length, structure or source use (Caplan & Johns, 2019; Moore & Morton, 2005). Teaching should centre on argument (Wingate, 2012) and treat structural templates as means to that end.

<a id="essessay-structure"></a>
#### Academic essay structure

`ess.essay-structure` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Understand how an essay's introduction, body and conclusion relate to a central argument: the introduction frames the question and states the position, the body develops it through a sequence of supported points, and the conclusion draws the argument together. Structure serves the argument, so the number, order and length of sections follow from the line of reasoning rather than from a fixed template.

Prerequisites: [The academic paragraph](#parparagraph-unit) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="essthesis-statement"></a>
#### Thesis statements

`ess.thesis-statement` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write a thesis statement that answers the question with a specific, arguable position, scoped to what the essay can support within its length and evidence, and that may preview the main lines of reasoning. A thesis states what the essay will argue, not merely what it will discuss, and is revised as the argument develops.

Prerequisites: [Generalising and qualifying](#stageneralisation-qualification) (soft), [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure), [Types of claims](#argclaim-types) (soft)

<a id="essintroductions"></a>
#### Writing introductions

`ess.introductions` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write an introduction that establishes the context and relevance of the topic, narrows to the specific focus and any key definitions, states the thesis and, where appropriate, outlines the structure, moving efficiently from general to specific. The introduction sets the reader's expectations for the whole essay and is usually best finalised after the body is drafted.

Prerequisites: [Frame markers and advance organisers](#cohframe-markers) (soft), [Paragraph functions](#parparagraph-functions) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement)

<a id="essconclusions"></a>
#### Writing conclusions

`ess.conclusions` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write a conclusion that synthesises the argument rather than repeating it, gives a direct answer to the question, and indicates implications, limitations or questions for further work without introducing new evidence. The conclusion is where the essay's overall judgement is made fully explicit and calibrated to the evidence presented.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Paragraph functions](#parparagraph-functions) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement)

<a id="essbody-organisation"></a>
#### Organising the essay body

`ess.body-organisation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Sequence and group body paragraphs so that they build the argument logically—by importance, causal chain, comparison criteria, or concession and rebuttal—and organise source material thematically around the writer's points rather than source by source. The organisation should be visible to the reader through topic sentences and transitions.

Prerequisites: [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns) (soft), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement), [What synthesis is](#synsynthesis-concept) (soft)

<a id="essanswering-question"></a>
#### Answering the question

`ess.answering-question` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Keep every part of an essay relevant to the question as set—its command words, focus and limits—by checking the thesis, topic sentences and conclusion against the question and cutting or reframing material that does not contribute to the answer. Irrelevance and partial answers are among the reasons markers most often give for low marks.

Prerequisites: [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis), [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement)

<a id="essessay-types"></a>
#### Essay types

`ess.essay-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Recognise the main types of essay set at university—argumentative (exposition), discussion, explanation and cause–effect, compare–contrast, problem–solution, and commentary or challenge essays—and how their purposes shape the thesis, organisation and conclusion. Types often blend, and labels vary across disciplines, courses and textbooks.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns) (soft), [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure)

<a id="essheadings"></a>
#### Headings and sections

`ess.headings` · PROCEDURAL · B2–B2+ · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Judge whether headings are expected, permitted or discouraged in a given essay (practice varies by discipline, length and department) and, where they are used, write informative headings in a consistent hierarchy without letting them replace topic sentences and transitions. Reports and dissertations use headings conventionally; many essay traditions do not.

Prerequisites: [Formatting and presentation](#wprformatting) (soft), [Organising the essay body](#essbody-organisation)

<a id="essdepth-focus"></a>
#### Depth versus breadth

`ess.depth-focus` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Narrow the scope of an essay to a manageable number of points, cases or perspectives so that each can be analysed in depth, and justify the focus explicitly. Covering everything relevant at a superficial level is a common cause of ‘descriptive’ feedback; within a word limit, depth of analysis is usually valued over breadth of coverage.

Prerequisites: [Working within word limits](#wprword-limits) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement), [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question) (soft)

<a id="esscriticality"></a>
#### Demonstrating criticality in essays

`ess.criticality` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Move beyond describing content to analysing how and why, evaluating the strength of evidence and arguments, comparing perspectives and reaching reasoned judgements, and make these moves visible in the text through evaluative language, concession and explicit reasoning. Criticality is among the qualities markers mention most often, and it is understood somewhat differently across disciplines.

Prerequisites: [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language) (soft), [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation)

<a id="esstitles"></a>
#### Writing titles

`ess.titles` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write informative titles for self-chosen essays, reports, dissertations and articles that indicate the topic, focus and, where appropriate, the argument or method, using conventions such as two-part colon titles, and apply the capitalisation rules of the required style. A title is often the first thing a marker, examiner or database user sees.

Prerequisites: [Capitalisation conventions](#sencapitalisation) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft)

### Revising, Editing & Proofreading

Covers how writers improve drafts: distinguishing revision of argument and structure from sentence-level editing and surface proofreading, and the techniques, feedback sources, tools and ethical limits involved. Sommers (1980) found that inexperienced writers tend to treat revision as rewording, so teaching should put global revision and criteria-based self-assessment before editing. The value of written corrective feedback has been contested (Truscott, 1996; Ferris, 1999), but meta-analytic evidence suggests it can improve accuracy (Kang & Han, 2015); learners therefore need routines for targeted self-editing, trained peer review (Lundstrom & Baker, 2009) and critical use of tools and permitted third-party help.

<a id="edtrevision-editing-proofreading"></a>
#### Revision, editing and proofreading

`edt.revision-editing-proofreading` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Distinguish three levels of work on a draft—revision (changing argument, content and structure), editing (improving sentences for clarity, accuracy and style) and proofreading (correcting surface errors in the final version)—and sequence them so that effort is not spent polishing text that may later be cut. The distinction underpins efficient self-review, well-targeted feedback requests and institutional rules on third-party help.

Prerequisites: [Writing as a recursive process](#wprwriting-process)

<a id="edtglobal-revision"></a>
#### Global revision

`edt.global-revision` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Revise a draft at whole-text level by re-examining the thesis, the line of argument, the selection and sufficiency of evidence, the order of sections and the fit with the task and reader, and make substantial changes—adding, cutting, moving or rewriting sections—where needed. Global revision usually improves a text far more than sentence-level correction does.

Prerequisites: [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtreverse-outlining"></a>
#### Reverse outlining

`edt.reverse-outlining` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Test the structure of a draft by writing a one-line statement of each paragraph's point (and, optionally, its function), then reading the statements in sequence to find gaps, repetition, misplaced paragraphs, paragraphs with more than one point and mismatches with the thesis. The technique turns a vague sense that ‘something is wrong’ into specific revision decisions.

Prerequisites: [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Global revision](#edtglobal-revision) (soft)

<a id="edtstyle-editing"></a>
#### Editing for style and clarity

`edt.style-editing` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Edit sentences to make them clearer, more concise and appropriate in register—bringing subjects and verbs together, reducing wordiness and redundancy, managing information density, choosing precise vocabulary and revising informal or vague expressions—while preserving meaning and the writer's voice. Style editing follows global revision and precedes proofreading.

Prerequisites: [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft), [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Clarity and end-weight](#senclarity-end-weight) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtgrammar-editing"></a>
#### Editing for grammatical accuracy

`edt.grammar-editing` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Edit drafts for grammatical accuracy by targeting one's own recurrent error types (for example articles, subject–verb agreement, tense, sentence boundaries) in focused passes, using feedback history, reference resources and personal checklists, rather than reading once for ‘all errors’. Targeted self-editing is more efficient than undirected checking and builds independence from teacher correction.

Prerequisites: [Monitoring recurrent grammatical errors](#grmerror-monitoring), [Sentence boundaries](#sensentence-boundaries) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtproofreading"></a>
#### Proofreading

`edt.proofreading` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Check a final version for surface errors—spelling, typing errors, punctuation, missing or repeated words, formatting, and consistency of spelling variety, terms and numbers—using techniques that counter the tendency to read what one meant rather than what is on the page, such as reading aloud, changing the font or layout, reading in reverse order for spelling, and using a checklist. Proofreading is the last stage, after revision and editing.

Prerequisites: [Spelling conventions and consistency](#vocspelling-conventions) (soft), [Apostrophes, hyphens and dashes](#senapostrophes-hyphens-dashes) (soft), [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading)

<a id="edtcriteria-self-assessment"></a>
#### Self-assessing drafts against criteria

`edt.criteria-self-assessment` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use marking criteria, rubrics, checklists and exemplars to judge the quality of one's own draft, identify the gap between current and target performance, and plan the revisions most likely to close it. Developing this evaluative judgement is central to becoming an independent writer and to making good use of feedback.

Prerequisites: [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria)

<a id="edtpeer-review"></a>
#### Peer review of drafts

`edt.peer-review` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Give specific, criteria-referenced and constructive feedback on a peer's draft, prioritising global issues, and receive and evaluate peer feedback, deciding what to act on. Reviewing others' writing also develops the reviewer's own evaluative judgement and revision skills.

Prerequisites: [Global revision](#edtglobal-revision) (soft), [Self-assessing drafts against criteria](#edtcriteria-self-assessment) (soft), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="edtediting-tools"></a>
#### Using editing tools critically

`edt.editing-tools` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use spelling and grammar checkers, automated writing evaluation, readability measures and AI-based rewriting tools critically: knowing what they detect well and poorly, evaluating each suggestion against one's own knowledge and reference sources, and staying within institutional rules on permitted assistance. Tools can support accuracy but can also introduce errors, change meaning, flatten the writer's voice and, if over-used, raise integrity concerns.

Prerequisites: [Editing for grammatical accuracy](#edtgrammar-editing), [Proofreading](#edtproofreading) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works) (soft)

<a id="edtproofreading-ethics"></a>
#### Acceptable third-party proofreading

`edt.proofreading-ethics` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand institutional policies on proofreading and editing by third parties (friends, paid proofreaders, editors, AI tools): which kinds of change are typically permitted (surface errors, formatting consistency) and prohibited (rewriting content, argument or structure), what must be disclosed, and how editorial support for theses and publications differs from that permitted for assessed coursework. Crossing these limits can constitute misconduct even when help is well intended.

Prerequisites: [Revision, editing and proofreading](#edtrevision-editing-proofreading), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft)

## Sources & Academic Integrity

### Information Literacy & Source Evaluation

Covers how scholarly knowledge is produced and published, how learners turn an assignment topic into a searchable question, how they search catalogues and databases, and how they judge whether a source is trustworthy and fit for a specific purpose. Evaluation is treated as contextual judgement, in line with the ACRL frame 'Authority Is Constructed and Contextual', rather than as checklist compliance, and lateral reading is included as an empirically grounded alternative to judging a site on its own terms. The language demands are real: generating search terms draws on vocabulary depth and disciplinary terminology, and judging a source depends on reading its genre signals and paratext.

<a id="infscholarly-communication"></a>
#### How scholarly knowledge is produced

`inf.scholarly-communication` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Explain how academic knowledge is made public and checked: peer review, journal articles, monographs and edited books, conference papers, preprints and grey literature, and the versions a single study may pass through. This understanding underpins calibrated judgements about authority, currency and status, for example why a preprint is cited with caution and why a textbook lags behind the journal literature.

Prerequisites: [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft)

<a id="infsource-types"></a>
#### Types of sources

`inf.source-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Classify sources by their relation to the object of study (primary, secondary, tertiary), by the kind of knowledge they offer (empirical study, review, theoretical or conceptual work, practice or policy document) and by their intended readership (scholarly or popular). Classification tells the learner what a source can legitimately support, and it depends on the discipline and question: a newspaper report is a secondary source for a chemist but may be a primary source for a historian.

<a id="infresearch-question"></a>
#### Focusing a topic for searching

`inf.research-question` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Narrow a broad assignment topic into a focused question by specifying context, population, period, variables or perspective, and test whether it is answerable within the word limit and the available literature. A focused question yields usable search concepts and inclusion decisions; an unfocused one produces unmanageable or irrelevant results and descriptive writing.

Prerequisites: [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge) (soft), [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis)

<a id="infsearch-strategies"></a>
#### Search strategies

`inf.search-strategies` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Build and refine searches by breaking a question into concepts, generating synonyms, spelling variants and broader or narrower terms for each, and combining them with Boolean operators, truncation, phrase searching and filters. Searching is iterative: learners read early results to harvest better terms and revise the strategy rather than typing a whole question into a search box.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question)

<a id="infdatabases"></a>
#### Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools

`inf.databases` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose between the library catalogue, the discovery layer, subject databases and Google Scholar according to what each indexes and how it can be searched, and use each tool's features (subject headings, filters, export, citation links, full-text links). Knowing what a tool covers stops learners concluding that 'there is nothing on this topic' after one search in an unsuitable tool.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication) (soft), [Search strategies](#infsearch-strategies)

<a id="infsource-evaluation"></a>
#### Evaluating sources

`inf.source-evaluation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Judge whether a source is credible and fit for a specific purpose by weighing its authority, currency, relevance, purpose and evidence base against the task. Evaluation is contextual: the same source may be acceptable as an example of public debate but inadequate as evidence for a causal claim, and checklists help only when applied with judgement.

Prerequisites: [What critical reading involves](#rdccritical-reading) (soft), [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="inflateral-reading"></a>
#### Lateral reading

`inf.lateral-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Evaluate an unfamiliar website, organisation, author or claim by leaving it and opening new tabs to find out what independent, reliable sources say about it, rather than judging it from its appearance and 'About' page. Wineburg and McGrew found that professional fact-checkers worked this way and reached sounder judgements more quickly than historians and students who read vertically within the site.

Prerequisites: [Reading on screen](#rdpdigital-reading) (soft), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation)

<a id="infproblematic-publishing"></a>
#### Predatory publishing, retractions and misinformation

`inf.problematic-publishing` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise the main routes by which unreliable material enters the record: deceptive ('predatory') venues that take fees without genuine peer review, published work that is later corrected or retracted, and misinformation that circulates through repeated citation, social media and fabricated references from generative AI tools. Learners need to verify venues and article status, and to understand that publication alone is not proof.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation), [Open access and legal access](#infopen-access) (soft)

<a id="infcitation-chaining"></a>
#### Citation chaining

`inf.citation-chaining` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use a relevant source as a node in the scholarly conversation: follow its references backward to earlier work and use 'cited by' tools to follow later work forward. Chaining finds sources that keyword searches miss, reveals key works in a debate and shows how a claim has been taken up, extended or challenged.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems) (soft)

<a id="infsearch-records"></a>
#### Recording searches and sources

`inf.search-records` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Keep records that allow searches to be repeated and sources to be found and cited accurately: tools searched, search strings, filters, dates and result counts, together with complete bibliographic details and locators for each source used. Good records prevent lost references and inaccurate citations, and protect against unattributed borrowing when the origin of a note has been forgotten.

Prerequisites: [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft), [Search strategies](#infsearch-strategies)

<a id="infopen-access"></a>
#### Open access and legal access

`inf.open-access` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand the routes by which scholarly work is made freely available (open-access journals with or without author charges, self-archiving in repositories, preprints) and the legitimate ways of obtaining paywalled work (library subscriptions, interlibrary loan, author copies, legal open-access finders). This lets learners find accessible versions, cite the right version and recognise the legal and security problems of unauthorised download sites.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication), [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft)

<a id="infsystematic-searching"></a>
#### Systematic and scoping searching

`inf.systematic-searching` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Design and report a transparent, reproducible search for a systematic, scoping or structured literature review: a protocol with a defined question, sources, full search strings for each database, supplementary methods such as chaining, explicit eligibility criteria and a record of screening decisions. Expectations depend on the field: such searching is standard in health and other evidence-synthesis fields and increasingly expected in education and social-policy dissertations.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases), [Citation chaining](#infcitation-chaining) (soft), [Recording searches and sources](#infsearch-records)

### Summarising, Paraphrasing & Quoting

Covers the core textual practices by which writers bring others' words and ideas into their own texts: quoting, paraphrasing and summarising, the reporting language that attributes and evaluates source content, and the signalling that keeps the writer's voice distinct from the sources'. The domain follows research suggesting that source-use problems in L2 writing are often developmental, linked to comprehension, vocabulary and an incomplete sense of why sources are used, rather than to intent to deceive (Howard; Pecorari; Shi; Keck). Instruction therefore pairs language work with purpose: learners transform sources in order to serve their own argument, and close dependence on source wording is treated as something to be taught through, not only policed.

<a id="srcsource-functions"></a>
#### Functions of sources in academic writing

`src.source-functions` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise the jobs that sources do in academic texts (attributing an idea, supporting a claim, supplying an example or definition, establishing what is known, contrasting positions, justifying a method and positioning the writer's contribution) and choose sources to do those jobs in one's own writing. Understanding function moves learners from showing that they have read to using reading to build an argument.

Prerequisites: [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept) (soft)

<a id="srcquoting"></a>
#### Quoting

`src.quoting` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Decide when direct quotation is warranted (the wording itself is the object of analysis, is a definition or formulation that cannot be bettered, or carries authority in the field) and quote accurately and economically, marking omissions and insertions and giving a locator. Norms differ by discipline: quotation is routine in the humanities and much social science but rare in the natural sciences and engineering.

Prerequisites: [Punctuating quotations and brackets](#senquotation-punctuation), [Functions of sources in academic writing](#srcsource-functions) (soft)

<a id="srcintegrating-quotations"></a>
#### Integrating quotations

`src.integrating-quotations` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Embed quotations grammatically and rhetorically: introduce them with a frame that names the source and signals relevance or stance, fit quoted fragments into the syntax of the surrounding sentence, and follow them with commentary that interprets the quotation and connects it to the writer's point. Unframed, 'dropped' quotations leave the reader to infer their relevance and blur the writer's voice.

Prerequisites: [Quoting](#srcquoting), [Signal phrases and attribution](#srcsignal-phrases)

<a id="srcparaphrasing"></a>
#### Paraphrasing

`src.paraphrasing` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Restate a specific idea from a source in substantially new wording and sentence structure while preserving its meaning, scope and degree of certainty, and attribute it. Paraphrase lets writers integrate sources into their own argument rather than stitching quotations together, and it depends on full comprehension of the source before reformulation begins.

Prerequisites: [Hedging devices](#stahedging) (soft), [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences) (soft), [Paraphrasing techniques](#srcparaphrase-techniques), [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite)

<a id="srcparaphrase-techniques"></a>
#### Paraphrasing techniques

`src.paraphrase-techniques` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Control the lexicogrammatical operations used in reformulation: substitution with near-synonyms, word-class change, voice change, nominalisation and its reverse, clause reordering and combining, and change of perspective. Learners also need the limits of each operation: synonym substitution alone yields patchwriting, near-synonyms can shift meaning or strength, and technical terms should usually be kept.

Prerequisites: [Word families and derivational morphology](#vocword-families-morphology), [Lexical precision and near-synonyms](#vocprecision-near-synonyms) (soft), [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation) (soft), [Passive voice and agency](#grmpassive-voice) (soft)

<a id="srcpatchwriting"></a>
#### Patchwriting

`src.patchwriting` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand patchwriting (reproducing a source's structure and much of its wording with some words deleted, substituted or rearranged) as a common and contested stage in learning to write from sources, distinct from deliberate copying, and use strategies to move beyond it. Howard and Pecorari argue that it often reflects incomplete comprehension and limited linguistic resources rather than intent to deceive, although institutional policies may still classify it as plagiarism.

Prerequisites: [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft)

<a id="srcsummarising-passage"></a>
#### Summarising a passage

`src.summarising-passage` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Condense a paragraph or short section to its main idea and essential support, in the writer's own words and to a stated length, and attribute it to the source. Passage summary is the building block for note-making, summary–response writing and synthesis, and it depends on identifying main ideas and on reformulation.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas), [Paraphrasing techniques](#srcparaphrase-techniques) (soft), [Signal phrases and attribution](#srcsignal-phrases) (soft)

<a id="srcsummarising-text"></a>
#### Summarising a whole text

`src.summarising-text` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Summarise an article or chapter by building its macrostructure: deleting trivial and redundant material, replacing lists with superordinate terms, and selecting or constructing statements that capture each section's gist (Brown and Day's macrorules). The summary represents the author's purpose, argument and main findings in proportion, using reporting language to show the author's moves.

Prerequisites: [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage), [Reporting verbs](#srcreporting-verbs) (soft)

<a id="srcreporting-verbs"></a>
#### Reporting verbs

`src.reporting-verbs` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose reporting verbs by what the source did (research acts such as find and observe; cognitive acts such as believe and assume; discourse acts such as argue, claim and note) and by the stance they convey towards the reported claim (accepting, neutral or distancing), and use their grammatical patterns accurately. Reporting verbs are a principal resource for representing sources precisely and signalling the writer's position on them.

Prerequisites: [Identifying writer stance and attributed views](#rdswriter-stance) (soft), [Signal phrases and attribution](#srcsignal-phrases)

<a id="srccitation-forms"></a>
#### Integral and non-integral citation

`src.citation-forms` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose between integral citation, where the cited author is an element of the sentence ('Smith (2020) argues that…'), and non-integral citation, where the reference sits in brackets or a number and the information is foregrounded, according to the rhetorical effect required. Author-prominent forms highlight who said something, often to contrast positions; information-prominent forms present content as established or bundle several supporting sources. The balance between forms varies across disciplines.

Prerequisites: [Signal phrases and attribution](#srcsignal-phrases), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft)

<a id="srcsignal-phrases"></a>
#### Signal phrases and attribution

`src.signal-phrases` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Use attribution frames ('according to X', 'X argues that', 'as X notes', 'in X's view') to introduce source material and to mark where attributed content begins and continues, so that readers can tell which propositions belong to a source. Signal phrases are the first resource learners need for transparent source use and underpin quotation, paraphrase and summary.

Prerequisites: [Reporting structures](#grmreporting-structures), [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft)

<a id="srcvoice-boundaries"></a>
#### Distinguishing own voice from source voice

`src.voice-boundaries` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Make clear where source content begins and ends and where the writer's own interpretation, evaluation or argument takes over, using attribution, citation placement, averral markers and evaluative commentary. Readers need these signals to assign responsibility for claims, and markers judge both originality and integrity by them.

Prerequisites: [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing) (soft), [Signal phrases and attribution](#srcsignal-phrases)

<a id="srcsecondary-citation"></a>
#### Secondary citation

`src.secondary-citation` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Cite a source known only through another author's account transparently (for example with 'as cited in' in APA style), recognise when this is acceptable (the original is unobtainable or in an inaccessible language), and otherwise locate and read the original, since secondary accounts can simplify or misrepresent it. Citing an unread original as if it had been read misrepresents the writer's reading.

Prerequisites: [Citation chaining](#infcitation-chaining) (soft), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft)

<a id="srcmultilingual-sources"></a>
#### Using sources in other languages

`src.multilingual-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use sources written in languages other than English transparently: translate or paraphrase their content accurately, mark one's own translations of quotations, give the original wording where conventions require, and reference the original-language work with a translated title as the style requires. Non-English scholarship can be a valuable resource, especially on local contexts, and using it well depends on clear signalling rather than silent translation.

Prerequisites: [Quoting](#srcquoting) (soft), [Paraphrasing](#srcparaphrasing), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

### Synthesis

Covers discourse synthesis: building a new text that integrates several sources around the writer's own organising ideas rather than summarising them one by one.
