The domain moves from the concept of synthesis, through tools for organising sources (matrices and thematic grouping), to the language of relating sources, identifying gaps and tensions, and writing synthesis paragraphs, and includes integrated reading- and listening-to-writing tasks. Spivey's account of synthesis as organising, selecting and connecting, together with research on integrated writing (Plakans; Plakans and Gebril; Grabe and Zhang), frames synthesis as a constructive reading–writing process rather than a set of linking phrases.

<a id="synsynthesis-concept"></a>
#### What synthesis is

`syn.synthesis-concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand synthesis as building a new, writer-organised account from several sources (selecting relevant content, organising it around themes or claims, and connecting sources to one another) rather than summarising sources in turn. Synthesis underlies literature reviews and source-based essays, and markers commonly distinguish it from author-by-author description.

Prerequisites: [Functions of sources in academic writing](#srcsource-functions), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage) (soft)

<a id="synsynthesis-matrix"></a>
#### Synthesis matrices

`syn.synthesis-matrix` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Record sources in a grid, with sources as rows and themes, aims, methods, findings or positions as columns, so that comparisons across sources become visible. The matrix is a note-making and planning tool that supports the shift from source-by-source to idea-by-idea organisation; columns can be set by the teacher or task at first and generated by the learner later.

Prerequisites: [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text) (soft), [What synthesis is](#synsynthesis-concept)

<a id="synthematic-grouping"></a>
#### Grouping sources thematically

`syn.thematic-grouping` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify themes, issues or positions that cut across a set of sources and group the sources, or parts of them, under those themes so that a text can be organised by idea. Thematic grouping requires classifying by explicit criteria and recognising that one source may contribute to several themes.

Prerequisites: [Classifying and categorising](#funclassifying) (soft), [Reading for a writing task](#rdcreading-for-writing) (soft), [Synthesis matrices](#synsynthesis-matrix) (soft)

<a id="synrelating-sources"></a>
#### Writing relationships between sources

`syn.relating-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Express how sources relate to one another (agreement, corroboration, extension, qualification, contrast and contradiction) using precise reporting verbs, comparison and contrast language, and citation forms that group sources or set them against each other. Making these relationships explicit is what turns a set of citations into synthesis.

Prerequisites: [Comparing and contrasting](#funcomparing-contrasting) (soft), [Reading across multiple texts](#rdcmultiple-texts), [Grouping sources thematically](#synthematic-grouping)

<a id="syngaps-tensions"></a>
#### Identifying gaps and tensions

`syn.gaps-tensions` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify what a body of literature leaves unresolved (conflicting findings, competing interpretations, methodological limitations, under-studied populations or contexts, and untested assumptions) and express it accurately and with appropriate hedging. Gaps and tensions justify new work and give reviews and essays an argumentative direction, but a claimed gap must be grounded in the literature actually reviewed.

Prerequisites: [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique) (soft), [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping) (soft), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources)

<a id="synsynthesis-paragraphs"></a>
#### Writing synthesis paragraphs

`syn.synthesis-paragraphs` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write paragraphs controlled by the writer's point, in which several sources are brought into dialogue as evidence, contrast or qualification, and the writer's commentary explains what the combination shows. A synthesis paragraph typically opens with the writer's claim, uses grouped and contrasted citations, keeps voice boundaries clear, and closes by drawing out significance for the wider argument.

Prerequisites: [Writing topic sentences](#partopic-sentences), [Integral and non-integral citation](#srccitation-forms) (soft), [Distinguishing own voice from source voice](#srcvoice-boundaries), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources)

<a id="synintegrated-tasks"></a>
#### Integrated source-based tasks

`syn.integrated-tasks` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Complete tasks that require reading and/or listening to sources and producing a written (or spoken) response that selects, transforms and integrates their content, such as summary–response tasks, reading-into-writing assessments and lecture-plus-reading syntheses. Success depends on comprehension, selective note-making, transformation of source language and organisation around the task rather than around the sources.

Prerequisites: [Taking notes in lectures](#ntklecture-notes) (soft), [Integrating reading into writing](#wprreading-to-write) (soft), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage), [What synthesis is](#synsynthesis-concept) (soft)

### Citation & Referencing

Citation is both a technical system and a rhetorical practice. This domain moves from why and when academics cite, through the mechanics of the main referencing systems (author–date, numeric and note-based), locators and reference lists, to the rhetorical functions of citation and how these vary across disciplines. Formatting is treated as learnable and checkable, ideally with reference-management tools, so that teaching time goes to the harder judgements: what needs citing, whether a cited source really supports the claim, how citation positions the writer, and what disciplinary readers expect.

<a id="citwhy-cite"></a>
#### Why academics cite

`cit.why-cite` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Explain the purposes citation serves in academic writing: giving credit, letting readers verify and follow up claims, showing how a text joins an ongoing scholarly conversation, and positioning the writer in relation to other work. Seeing citation as communicative rather than only as a rule for avoiding penalties underpins every later decision about what, when and how to cite.

<a id="citwhen-to-cite"></a>
#### When to cite

`cit.when-to-cite` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Decide which content requires citation (others' specific ideas, findings, data, wording, visuals, frameworks and lines of argument) and which does not (the writer's own analysis and genuinely common knowledge). Because common knowledge is relative to discipline, readership and level of study, learners need a working procedure for doubtful cases rather than a fixed list.

Prerequisites: [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite), [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities) (soft)

<a id="citreferencing-systems"></a>
#### Referencing systems and style guides

`cit.referencing-systems` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Distinguish the main families of referencing system — author–date (APA 7, Chicago author–date, Harvard variants), MLA's author–page system, numeric (IEEE, Vancouver) and note-based (Chicago notes and bibliography, OSCOLA) — and recognise that the departmental, module or journal guide, not a universal rule, decides which applies. 'Harvard' has no single authoritative edition, so learners must follow the version their institution specifies.

Prerequisites: [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite) (soft)

<a id="citauthor-date-in-text"></a>
#### In-text citation: author–date

`cit.author-date-in-text` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Format author–date in-text citations accurately in narrative and parenthetical forms — one, two and three or more authors, et al., group authors, several works in one citation, same-author same-year works and secondary citation — following the specified guide (usually APA 7 or an institutional Harvard). Accurate, well-placed in-text citation lets readers move without friction from a claim to its reference entry and see exactly which content is attributed.

Prerequisites: [Academic abbreviations and Latin expressions](#vocabbreviations-latin) (soft), [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems)

<a id="citnumeric"></a>
#### Numeric citation systems

`cit.numeric` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Use numeric systems such as IEEE and Vancouver: number sources in order of first citation, reuse a source's number when citing it again, format bracketed or superscript numbers, multiple citations and ranges, and compile the reference list in numerical order. Numeric styles dominate engineering, computing and much of medicine, where cited authors usually stay in the background and naming them is a deliberate choice.

Prerequisites: [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems)

<a id="citnote-based"></a>
#### Note-based citation

`cit.note-based` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Use footnote or endnote systems — Chicago notes and bibliography and, in UK law, OSCOLA — formatting full and shortened notes, pinpoint references, subsequent references and, where required, a bibliography or tables of cases and legislation. Notes are the norm in history, much of the humanities and law; they can also carry brief discursive commentary, which must be kept distinct from citation and from the main argument.

Prerequisites: [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list)

<a id="citreference-list"></a>
#### Compiling a reference list

`cit.reference-list` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Build reference-list entries from their core elements — who, when, what and where, including DOIs or stable URLs — for the source types most often cited, and order, format and label the list as the specified guide requires. Learners need to identify the source type and locate the elements in the source itself rather than copy entries from search results or model lists.

Prerequisites: [Capitalisation conventions](#sencapitalisation) (soft), [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft), [Referencing systems and style guides](#citreferencing-systems)

<a id="citdiverse-sources"></a>
#### Citing diverse source types

`cit.diverse-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Cite sources beyond books and journal articles — web pages, reports and other grey literature, datasets, software, standards, audiovisual material, social media, personal communications and outputs of generative AI tools — by identifying who is responsible, which version was used, when, and how a reader can retrieve it. Where the reader cannot retrieve a source, learners follow how their guide and institution handle it rather than inventing a format.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list)

<a id="citlocators"></a>
#### Page numbers and locators

`cit.locators` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Give the precise location of cited material — page, paragraph, section, timestamp, table or figure — whenever quoting, when the guide requires or recommends it for paraphrase, and when pinpointing a specific claim in a long work. Locators let readers verify exactly what was taken from where; their form differs between systems and source types.

Prerequisites: [Quoting](#srcquoting)

<a id="citaccuracy-consistency"></a>
#### Citation accuracy and consistency

`cit.accuracy-consistency` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Check that every in-text citation has a matching reference entry and vice versa, that names, dates, titles, page ranges and identifiers are correct and consistently formatted, and that each cited source actually supports the claim attached to it. Reference-manager output, database exports and references produced by generative AI tools all need verifying against the source or an authoritative record.

Prerequisites: [Proofreading](#edtproofreading) (soft), [In-text citation: author–date](#citauthor-date-in-text) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list), [Reference management software](#digreference-managers) (soft)

<a id="citcitation-functions"></a>
#### Rhetorical functions of citation

`cit.citation-functions` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise and use the range of functions citations perform — attribution, exemplification, pointing to further reading, statement of use, application, evaluation, establishing links between sources, and comparing one's own findings with others' — and choose citation form and reporting language to fit the function. Using citation beyond simple attribution has been associated with more highly rated postgraduate writing.

Prerequisites: [Functions of sources in academic writing](#srcsource-functions), [Reporting verbs](#srcreporting-verbs) (soft), [Integral and non-integral citation](#srccitation-forms) (soft)

<a id="citdisciplinary-citation"></a>
#### Disciplinary variation in citation

`cit.disciplinary-citation` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Describe how citation practice differs across disciplines — in citation density, use of direct quotation, integral versus non-integral forms, reporting-verb choice and preferred systems — and relate these differences to how each field builds knowledge. Learners use this to read their own discipline's texts as models rather than applying one generic EAP template.

Prerequisites: [Rhetorical functions of citation](#citcitation-functions) (soft), [Disciplinary knowledge-making](#acudisciplinary-knowledge) (soft)

### Academic Integrity

Academic integrity is taught here as values and practices rather than a list of offences. The domain moves from shared values and an informed understanding of plagiarism (including the developmental status of patchwriting and the contested role of intention) to collusion, contract cheating, reuse of one's own work and research integrity, and to the procedural knowledge learners need: interpreting similarity reports, navigating policies and processes, and managing pressure without shortcuts. Rules differ between institutions and modules, so teaching centres on locating and reading the rules that apply, and similarity scores are never treated as verdicts.

<a id="intintegrity-values"></a>
#### Values of academic integrity

`int.integrity-values` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Explain the values that underpin academic integrity — honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility and courage, as set out by the International Center for Academic Integrity — and connect them to everyday practices such as acknowledging sources, contributing fairly to group work and reporting data truthfully. A values framing presents integrity as a shared commitment of a scholarly community rather than only a set of prohibitions.

<a id="intplagiarism"></a>
#### Understanding plagiarism

`int.plagiarism` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Explain plagiarism as presenting others' words, ideas, data or work as one's own without adequate acknowledgement, and distinguish its forms: verbatim copying, patchwriting, missing or misleading citation, and borrowing structure or ideas. Learners should also understand why institutions weigh intention differently, why patchwriting is widely regarded as a developmental stage rather than deception, and that notions of textual ownership are historically and institutionally situated rather than universal.

Prerequisites: [Why academics cite](#citwhy-cite), [Values of academic integrity](#intintegrity-values) (soft)

<a id="intrecycling-own-work"></a>
#### Reusing one's own work

`int.recycling-own-work` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand when and how one's own previous work may be reused. In coursework, most institutions treat resubmitting previously assessed work for credit as misconduct unless explicitly permitted; in research writing, reuse of text, data or findings (redundant or duplicate publication, text recycling) is governed by institutional, journal and publisher policies. Transparency — citing the earlier work, disclosing overlap and seeking permission where required — is the common principle.

Prerequisites: [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="intcollusion"></a>
#### Collusion and legitimate collaboration

`int.collusion` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Distinguish collusion — unauthorised collaboration on work assessed as individual, such as sharing drafts, answers or code, co-writing, or completing part of someone else's work — from legitimate collaboration such as discussing ideas, study groups, structured peer review and assessed group work. The boundary is set by each assessment's rules, so learners need to read the brief and ask when unsure.

Prerequisites: [Purposes of group work](#grwpurposes) (soft), [Values of academic integrity](#intintegrity-values) (soft)

<a id="intcontract-cheating"></a>
#### Contract cheating

`int.contract-cheating` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise contract cheating — having a third party complete assessed work, whether paid or unpaid, through essay mills, 'tutoring' or 'editing' services that write content, file-exchange sites, friends or family — and the risks it carries, including detection, serious academic penalties, fraud, loss of personal data and blackmail by providers. Knowing how providers market themselves helps learners resist offers when under pressure.

Prerequisites: [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism)

<a id="intsimilarity-reports"></a>
#### Interpreting similarity reports

`int.similarity-reports` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Read a text-matching (similarity) report critically: it shows overlaps between a submission and indexed material, not plagiarism; matches must be interpreted by type (quoted and cited text, reference entries, common phrases, patchwriting, unattributed copying); and, where permitted, reports can be used formatively to improve source use. An overall percentage has no fixed meaning, and whether misconduct has occurred is decided by people applying policy, not by software.

Prerequisites: [Patchwriting](#srcpatchwriting) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism)

<a id="intpolicy-processes"></a>
#### Integrity policies and processes

`int.policy-processes` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Locate and interpret the integrity regulations that apply: definitions of misconduct, module-level rules, procedures for suspected cases, possible outcomes, and rights to support, representation and appeal. Knowing the process reduces fear, helps learners respond appropriately and in time if concerns are raised, and makes clear that policies and terminology vary between institutions and modules.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism), [Interpreting similarity reports](#intsimilarity-reports) (soft)

<a id="intpressure-risks"></a>
#### Managing integrity risk under pressure

`int.pressure-risks` · STRATEGIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise the conditions under which shortcuts become tempting — clustered deadlines, illness, financial or visa-related pressure, low confidence in English, unclear tasks — and act early: plan workload, request extensions or submit extenuating-circumstances claims through proper channels, use academic and wellbeing support, and ask for clarification rather than guessing or outsourcing.

Prerequisites: [Contract cheating](#intcontract-cheating) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft), [Using academic support services](#lrnsupport-services), [Making requests](#prgrequests) (soft)

<a id="intresearch-integrity"></a>
#### Research integrity

`int.research-integrity` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand the standards of responsible research: honesty in proposing, conducting and reporting research; the avoidance of fabrication, falsification and plagiarism; questionable research practices such as selective reporting, undisclosed changes to hypotheses or analyses and inappropriate authorship; and data management that makes research verifiable. Research students apply these standards to their own data, records, theses and publications.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft)

## Argument & Critical Thinking

### Critical Thinking

This domain treats critical thinking as a contested, partly discipline-specific set of practices and dispositions — questioning, weighing evidence, recognising inference patterns and their limits, considering perspectives fairly and reflecting on one's own position — that can be made explicit and taught. It deliberately rejects the deficit framing that some groups of learners 'cannot think critically': differences in readiness to critique published work are treated as differences in prior schooling, assessment genres and licence to question, not as cognitive or cultural deficits. The domain supplies the reasoning that the Academic Argumentation domain turns into written and spoken argument.

<a id="crtconcept"></a>
#### Critical thinking in academic contexts

`crt.concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand critical thinking in university study as a contested cluster of practices and dispositions — questioning claims, weighing evidence, judging reasoning and acknowledging uncertainty — whose concrete form varies by discipline and task. Treating it as explicit, learnable academic practice rather than a personal or cultural trait lets learners see what markers actually reward.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="crtdescription-analysis-evaluation"></a>
#### Description, analysis and evaluation

`crt.description-analysis-evaluation` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Distinguish three kinds of academic move — describing what something is or says, analysing how or why it works (its parts, causes, relationships and implications) and evaluating its value, validity or significance against stated criteria — and recognise which moves a task and its markers expect. Most 'too descriptive' feedback signals an imbalance among these moves rather than a lack of knowledge.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Critical thinking in academic contexts](#crtconcept) (soft)

<a id="crtquestioning"></a>
#### Critical questioning

`crt.questioning` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Habitually interrogate claims, texts, data and one's own ideas with questions such as how do we know, according to whom, compared with what, under what conditions and so what. Questioning is the generative engine of critical reading, analysis and research, and can be practised as a deliberate routine before it becomes a disposition.

Prerequisites: [Identifying main ideas and supporting detail](#rdsmain-ideas) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation) (soft)

<a id="crtevidence-types"></a>
#### Types and strength of evidence

`crt.evidence-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise the main kinds of evidence used in academic argument — experimental and statistical data, qualitative accounts, documentary and textual evidence, expert testimony and anecdote — and judge what each can and cannot establish for a particular claim. Evidence hierarchies, such as those used in evidence-based medicine, are useful but field-bound tools, not universal rankings.

Prerequisites: [Distinguishing fact, opinion, claim and evidence](#rdsfact-opinion-claim), [Types of sources](#infsource-types) (soft)

<a id="crtreasoning-types"></a>
#### Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning

`crt.reasoning-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise and use the main patterns of inference: deduction, in which the conclusion follows necessarily if the premises are true; induction, which generalises or predicts from observed cases with probability rather than certainty; and abduction, which infers the most plausible explanation for observations. Knowing which pattern an argument uses tells the reader what degree of certainty it can claim and how it might fail.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="crtcorrelation-causation"></a>
#### Correlation and causation

`crt.correlation-causation` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Distinguish associations between variables from causal relationships by considering confounding, reverse causation, chance and selection effects, and know in broad terms which research designs (randomised experiments, natural experiments, controlled longitudinal designs) can support causal claims. This underpins accurate reading and reporting of research and the choice of causal verbs in writing.

Prerequisites: [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Basic statistical vocabulary](#numstatistical-vocabulary) (soft)

<a id="crtfallacies"></a>
#### Recognising fallacies

`crt.fallacies` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise common informal fallacies — straw man, ad hominem, false dilemma, hasty generalisation, slippery slope, appeal to popularity and post hoc reasoning — as misuses of otherwise legitimate argument patterns, and explain what makes a particular instance fallacious in its context. The aim is diagnostic precision when evaluating and constructing arguments, not label-spotting.

Prerequisites: [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="crtcognitive-biases"></a>
#### Cognitive biases

`crt.cognitive-biases` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand how systematic tendencies in human judgement — especially confirmation bias and motivated reasoning, but also availability and anchoring — shape what we search for, notice, believe and report, and adopt procedural safeguards in reading and research, such as seeking disconfirming evidence, setting criteria in advance and inviting others' scrutiny. Awareness alone rarely removes bias; routines help.

Prerequisites: [Critical questioning](#crtquestioning) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="crtmultiple-perspectives"></a>
#### Considering multiple perspectives

`crt.multiple-perspectives` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify the range of defensible positions on an issue and represent each fairly — including those one rejects — in terms its proponents would recognise, before weighing them against explicit criteria. Fair representation is a precondition for credible counter-argument, synthesis and discussion writing, and protects against straw-man reasoning.

Prerequisites: [Identifying writer stance and attributed views](#rdswriter-stance), [Summarising a passage](#srcsummarising-passage) (soft), [Critical questioning](#crtquestioning) (soft)

<a id="crtepistemic-beliefs"></a>
#### Beliefs about knowledge

`crt.epistemic-beliefs` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Reflect on and develop one's beliefs about the nature of knowledge — its certainty, simplicity, source and justification — moving from treating knowledge as fixed facts transmitted by authorities, through treating all views as equally valid, towards evaluating claims as better or worse supported. These beliefs shape how learners read conflicting sources, respond to open questions and feedback, and build arguments.

Prerequisites: [Critical thinking in academic contexts](#crtconcept) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="crtevaluating-theories"></a>
#### Evaluating theories and models

`crt.evaluating-theories` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Evaluate a theory, model or framework against criteria such as explanatory power, scope, empirical support, internal coherence, parsimony and usefulness for a stated purpose, and compare rival frameworks on those criteria. This moves learners from reporting what a theory says to justifying why they use, adapt or reject it.

Prerequisites: [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types) (soft), [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory) (soft)

<a id="crtapplying-theory"></a>
#### Applying theory to cases

`crt.applying-theory` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use concepts, theories or models as analytical tools to explain a case, dataset, text or situation: selecting a relevant concept, mapping its components onto specific evidence, and drawing out what the analysis reveals, including where the case does not fit. Application is the core move in case studies, problem questions and many dissertation analyses.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Reading theoretical and conceptual texts](#rdctheoretical-texts) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation)

<a id="crtproblem-solving"></a>
#### Analytical problem-solving

`crt.problem-solving` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Define a problem precisely (its symptoms, causes, stakeholders and constraints), generate a range of possible responses, evaluate them against explicit criteria such as effectiveness, feasibility, cost and ethics, and justify a recommendation with its trade-offs acknowledged. This process underlies problem–solution essays, case analyses, design reports and policy writing.

Prerequisites: [Problem, solution and evaluation](#funproblem-solution) (soft), [Critical questioning](#crtquestioning) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft)

<a id="crtreflexivity"></a>
#### Reflexivity and positionality

`crt.reflexivity` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise how one's own position — background, experiences, values, languages, disciplinary training and relationship to participants — shapes the questions asked, the evidence noticed and the interpretations made, and account for this transparently. Reflexivity is expected in qualitative and practice-based research and increasingly in reflective and critical genres across disciplines.

Prerequisites: [Cognitive biases](#crtcognitive-biases) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft), [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs)

### Academic Argumentation

This domain treats argument as the organising purpose of most assessed academic writing and much academic talk: taking a position, supporting claims with evidence and explicit reasoning, anticipating and answering objections, and sequencing sub-claims into a cumulative case. Hirvela (2017) argues that L2 writing pedagogy has given argumentation less attention than its centrality warrants, and Wingate (2012) found that students often receive feedback on 'argument' without being shown what it consists of. Toulmin's model, argumentation schemes and argument mapping are offered as analytical tools rather than templates, and disciplinary variation in what counts as a good argument (Andrews, 2010) is made explicit.

<a id="argargument-concept"></a>
#### What counts as an academic argument

`arg.argument-concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand an academic argument as a reasoned position on a question, developed through claims supported by evidence and explicit reasoning and responsive to other views — not a quarrel, a one-sided opinion or a well-organised collection of facts. Grasping this reframes most assessed writing as argument and explains feedback such as 'What is your argument?'

Prerequisites: [Distinguishing fact, opinion, claim and evidence](#rdsfact-opinion-claim) (soft)

<a id="argclaim-types"></a>
#### Types of claims

`arg.claim-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Distinguish claims of fact (what is the case), definition (what something is or should be classed as), cause (what produces what), value (how good, important or right something is) and policy (what should be done), and recognise that each type calls for different kinds of support. Identifying the claim type an assignment question calls for clarifies what evidence and reasoning the answer needs.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argtoulmin"></a>
#### Toulmin model of argument

`arg.toulmin` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use Toulmin's model — claim, data or grounds, warrant, backing, qualifier and rebuttal — to analyse how a single argument works and where it is vulnerable. Its value in EAP lies in making visible the usually implicit link (warrant) between evidence and claim and the role of qualification and exceptions, not in providing an essay template.

Prerequisites: [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argsupporting-claims"></a>
#### Supporting claims

`arg.supporting-claims` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Link each claim to evidence and reasoning that are relevant, sufficient and appropriate to the claim's type and strength — drawing on sources, data, examples and explanation — and make clear how each piece of support bears on the claim. This is the basic unit of argumentative writing and speaking, from foundation paragraphs to thesis chapters.

Prerequisites: [Exemplifying and illustrating](#funexemplifying) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argwarrants"></a>
#### Making reasoning explicit

`arg.warrants` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

State, where readers need it, the reasoning that connects evidence to claim — the principle, mechanism, assumption or criterion that makes the evidence relevant — and support that reasoning when it may be contested. Explicit warrants distinguish analysis from the juxtaposition of facts and claims, especially for readers who do not share the writer's assumptions.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Toulmin model of argument](#argtoulmin), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims)

<a id="argcounterarguments"></a>
#### Anticipating counter-arguments

`arg.counterarguments` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify the strongest objections, alternative explanations and contrary evidence that a knowledgeable reader could raise against one's position, and decide which must be addressed in the text. Anticipating objections is a hallmark of mature argument and of higher-rated student essays, but learners rarely do it spontaneously.

Prerequisites: [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims)

<a id="argrebuttal-concession"></a>
#### Rebuttal and concession

`arg.rebuttal-concession` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Respond to counter-arguments by conceding what is valid, rebutting what is not with evidence or reasoning, and qualifying one's own position where necessary, using language that acknowledges the other view while keeping the writer's stance dominant. Well-managed concession and rebuttal build credibility and show dialogic engagement with the field.

Prerequisites: [Dialogic positioning (engagement)](#stadialogic-positioning) (soft), [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language), [Anticipating counter-arguments](#argcounterarguments)

<a id="argposition"></a>
#### Taking and sustaining a position

`arg.position` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Commit to a defensible, appropriately qualified position on a question, state it where the genre expects (often in the introduction) and maintain it consistently so that every section of the text contributes to it. A sustained position is what readers and marking criteria most often mean by 'argument' and distinguishes argumentative from merely informative writing.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims)

<a id="argextended-argument"></a>
#### Structuring an extended argument

`arg.extended-argument` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Sequence sub-claims, evidence and responses to objections into a cumulative case in which each section advances the main position and depends logically on what precedes it, with signposting that makes the argumentative structure visible. This is required in long essays, dissertations and theses, where structures organised by topic or by source often fail to build an argument.

Prerequisites: [Text structure patterns](#cohtext-patterns) (soft), [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession) (soft), [Taking and sustaining a position](#argposition), [Argument mapping](#argargument-mapping) (soft)

<a id="argrhetorical-appeals"></a>
#### Rhetorical appeals

`arg.rhetorical-appeals` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise and use appeals to reason (logos), to the writer's credibility (ethos) and to the reader's values and emotions (pathos) as they operate in academic contexts, where logos dominates but credibility is built through accurate citation, calibrated claims and disciplinary language, and value appeals are usually restrained. Recognising appeals helps learners read persuasive texts critically and adjust persuasion for academic and public audiences.

Prerequisites: [Metadiscourse](#stametadiscourse) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argargument-mapping"></a>
#### Argument mapping

`arg.argument-mapping` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Represent the structure of an argument as a diagram of claims, reasons, co-premises and objections, showing how each supports or challenges another, in order to analyse a source's reasoning or to plan one's own. Mapping externalises reasoning that is otherwise held in working memory, making gaps, unstated premises and weak links visible.

Prerequisites: [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification), [Supporting claims](#argsupporting-claims) (soft)

<a id="argoral-argument"></a>
#### Arguing orally

`arg.oral-argument` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Construct, support and defend arguments in real-time academic talk — seminars, tutorials, Q&A sessions and supervision — by stating positions concisely, supporting them with reasons and sources, responding to challenges, and conceding or refining positions as discussion develops. Oral argument differs from written argument in its pace, its co-construction across speakers and its reliance on mitigation to maintain collegiality.

Prerequisites: [Expressing and justifying views](#semopinions), [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing) (soft), [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession) (soft)

<a id="argexploratory-adversarial"></a>
#### Exploratory and adversarial argument

`arg.exploratory-adversarial` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Distinguish argument as collaborative, open-ended inquiry — exploring a question, testing ideas and reaching provisional conclusions — from argument as persuasive advocacy for a settled position, and recognise which orientation a genre, task or interaction calls for. Many academic genres and events (discussion essays, literature reviews, seminars) are largely exploratory, whereas others (debates, proposals, policy briefs) are more adversarial.

Prerequisites: [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft), [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs) (soft), [What counts as an academic argument](#argargument-concept)

<a id="argevaluating-arguments"></a>
#### Evaluating arguments

`arg.evaluating-arguments` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Judge the quality of an argument by assessing whether its premises are acceptable, relevant to the conclusion and jointly sufficient to support it, whether its warrants hold, and how well it deals with objections — and express that judgement in measured academic language. This is the core analytical move in critical reviews, literature reviews and peer review.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation) (soft), [Recognising fallacies](#crtfallacies) (soft), [Making reasoning explicit](#argwarrants), [Argument mapping](#argargument-mapping) (soft)

### Identity, Plurilingualism & Intercultural Competence

This domain addresses who learners are as academic language users: how writing and speaking construct identity, how plurilingual repertoires can be used strategically, how English functions as a lingua franca in international universities, and how intercultural and rhetorical differences can be understood without stereotyping. It adopts a resource rather than a deficit orientation, treats culture as dynamic, multiple and partly institutional rather than as fixed national traits, and presents contrastive rhetoric and native-speaker norms as historically influential but contested. Several topics are as much curriculum principles for teachers as content for learners, and are written so that they can serve both.

<a id="idnacademic-identity"></a>
#### Academic and writer identity

`idn.academic-identity` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand that academic writing and speaking construct an identity — through choices of stance, self-mention, citation, register and topic — and that entering a discipline involves negotiating new identities alongside existing ones. Seeing identity as discoursally constructed helps learners make deliberate choices about how they present themselves, and makes tensions between existing selves and academic conventions discussable rather than private.

Prerequisites: [Impersonality and authorial presence](#regimpersonality-presence) (soft), [Academic literacies perspective](#acuacademic-literacies) (soft)

<a id="idnplurilingual-repertoire"></a>
#### Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire

`idn.plurilingual-repertoire` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use all one's languages strategically for academic learning — reading and note-making across languages, discussing ideas in shared languages, planning with the first language and checking terminology bilingually — while producing assessed work in the required language and within institutional rules. Treating the repertoire as an integrated resource supports comprehension, conceptual depth and confidence.

Prerequisites: [Linguistic diversity as resource](#idndiversity-as-resource) (soft), [Language learning strategies](#lrnlanguage-learning-strategies) (soft)

<a id="idnelf"></a>
#### English as a lingua franca in academia

`idn.elf` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand that much academic communication in English takes place among multilingual users for whom English is a shared additional language; that effectiveness in such interaction depends on intelligibility, accommodation and negotiation of meaning rather than conformity to a native-speaker model; and that all participants, including first-language English users, share responsibility for mutual understanding.

Prerequisites: [Intelligibility, comprehensibility and accent](#prnintelligibility) (soft), [Linguistic diversity as resource](#idndiversity-as-resource) (soft)

<a id="idnintercultural-communication"></a>
#### Intercultural communication

`idn.intercultural-communication` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Manage differences in expectations, communication styles and assumptions in academic interaction with peers, tutors and supervisors by noticing when misunderstanding may stem from differing norms, suspending quick judgements, checking interpretations and negotiating shared ways of working. Intercultural competence here treats culture as dynamic and multiple — institutional, disciplinary and group-level as well as national — rather than as fixed national traits.

Prerequisites: [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft), [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

<a id="idnintercultural-rhetoric"></a>
#### Intercultural rhetoric

`idn.intercultural-rhetoric` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand how views of rhetorical variation across languages and cultures have developed — from Kaplan's contrastive rhetoric, through Connor's intercultural rhetoric, to critical contrastive rhetoric — and use this to analyse why writing conventions differ and change, without assuming that any culture's rhetoric is deficient or that an individual's writing is determined by their first language. The practical outcome is treating conventions as learnable, situated choices.

Prerequisites: [Disciplinary variation in style](#regdisciplinary-style) (soft), [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation) (soft), [Genre and discourse community](#gesgenre-concept)

<a id="idndiversity-as-resource"></a>
#### Linguistic diversity as resource

`idn.diversity-as-resource` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Recognise and challenge deficit framings that treat multilingual students as lacking (language, critical thinking, autonomy), and understand linguistic and cultural diversity as a resource for learning, collaboration and knowledge-making. For learners, this means recognising the assets in their own repertoires and experience; for programmes, it means making academic expectations explicit for all students rather than remediating some.

Prerequisites: [Academic literacies perspective](#acuacademic-literacies) (soft), [Transition and adjustment](#acutransition) (soft)

<a id="idnstandards-legitimacy"></a>
#### Standards, native-speakerism and legitimacy

`idn.standards-legitimacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Examine critically the assumption that native-speaker English is the benchmark for academic English, distinguishing the conventions of academic genres (which all writers must learn) from nativeness, and understanding how native-speakerism, gatekeeping and language ideologies affect assessment, publication and participation. This enables learners to claim legitimacy as multilingual academic users while making informed choices about conventions.

Prerequisites: [Myths and change in academic style](#regstyle-myths-variation) (soft), [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

## Academic Genres

### Essay & Examination Genres

This domain covers the essay, examination, review and source-based genres most often assessed in foundation, pre-sessional and taught programmes, together with the genre concepts and analytic strategies learners need to handle genres they have not been explicitly taught. Each genre topic integrates component skills from the writing, sources, argument, discourse and reading strands, and describes typical purposes, stages and language; because conventions vary by discipline, department and assignment brief, every topic stresses analysis of local exemplars, briefs and marking criteria rather than a single template. The domain draws on all three genre traditions (ESP, Sydney School and New Rhetoric) and on corpus evidence about genre families in UK student writing.

<a id="gesgenre-concept"></a>
#### Genre and discourse community

`ges.genre-concept` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand a genre as a recognisable, purpose-driven type of communicative event (an essay, a lab report, a case study, an email to a tutor) whose typical stages, content and language are shaped by the shared purposes and expectations of a discourse community. Seeing conventions as motivated responses to purpose, reader and context, rather than as arbitrary rules, lets learners explain why texts differ across modules and adapt to genres they have not been taught.

Prerequisites: [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft)

<a id="gesgenre-analysis"></a>
#### Analysing genres independently

`ges.genre-analysis` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Analyse exemplars of a target genre, alongside the assignment brief and marking criteria, to identify its purpose, stages or moves, typical content and characteristic language, and turn these observations into decisions for one's own text. This rhetorical consciousness-raising lets learners master genres an EAP course cannot cover and protects them against a generic model that the local department does not use.

Prerequisites: [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation) (soft), [Genre and discourse community](#gesgenre-concept)

<a id="gesgenre-families"></a>
#### Genre families in university assessment

`ges.genre-families` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Know the range of genres in which students are assessed (essays, critiques, explanations, exercises, case studies, literature surveys, methodology recounts such as lab reports, research reports, proposals, design specifications, problem questions, and narrative or reflective recounts) and the broad social purposes they serve, from demonstrating knowledge to preparing for professional practice. This map helps learners recognise what an assignment is asking for and see that the essay is not the only, or even the dominant, genre in many disciplines.

Prerequisites: [Genre and discourse community](#gesgenre-concept), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="gesargumentative-essay"></a>
#### Argumentative essay

`ges.argumentative-essay` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Produce a thesis-led essay that takes a defensible position on a contested question and builds a cumulative, source-supported case for it, engaging with alternative views and qualifying the position where the evidence requires. Typical stages are an introduction that frames the issue and states the thesis, body paragraphs that each advance a reason with evidence and commentary, treatment of counter-positions, and a conclusion that answers the question in the light of the argument; characteristic language includes stance markers, hedges and boosters, concession–counter patterns and evaluative reporting of sources.

Prerequisites: [Integrating sources in paragraphs](#parintegrating-evidence), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement), [Structuring an extended argument](#argextended-argument)

<a id="gesdiscussion-essay"></a>
#### Discussion essay

`ges.discussion-essay` · GENRE · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Produce an essay that represents two or more perspectives on an issue fairly, weighs them against evidence and explicit criteria, and reaches a reasoned, qualified conclusion. Typical stages are statement of the issue, presentation of perspectives (in blocks or point by point), evaluation and judgement; characteristic language includes attribution and reporting verbs, contrastive connectors, concession and evaluative lexis. In UK higher education even a 'discuss' essay is usually expected to arrive at a position, and often to signal it early.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating), [Organising the essay body](#essbody-organisation) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives)

<a id="gescompare-contrast-essay"></a>
#### Compare-and-contrast essay

`ges.compare-contrast-essay` · GENRE · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Produce an essay that compares two or more theories, cases, methods, policies or texts against explicit criteria and uses the comparison to make a point, such as which option is more appropriate or what a difference reveals, rather than listing similarities and differences. Typical stages are introduction of the items and the basis of comparison, criterion-led (point-by-point) or item-led (block) comparison, evaluation of the significance of the differences, and a conclusion stating the outcome; characteristic language includes comparative structures, contrastive connectors and hedged evaluative claims.

Prerequisites: [Comparing and contrasting](#funcomparing-contrasting), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating) (soft), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft)

<a id="gesexplanation-essay"></a>
#### Explanation and cause–effect essay

`ges.explanation-essay` · GENRE · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Produce an essay that explains how or why a phenomenon occurs: its causes (factorial explanation), its consequences (consequential explanation) or the mechanism or sequence by which it happens, evaluating the relative importance and certainty of the factors. Typical stages are identification of the phenomenon, an explanation sequence organised by factor, consequence or step, and a conclusion that weighs the factors; characteristic language includes causal verbs and nouns (lead to, result in, a consequence of), nominalised causal chains, hedged causal claims and sequencing.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process) (soft), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Organising the essay body](#essbody-organisation) (soft), [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft)

<a id="gesproblem-solution-essay"></a>
#### Problem–solution essay

`ges.problem-solution-essay` · GENRE · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Produce an essay that defines a problem, establishes its causes and significance, presents possible responses and evaluates them against criteria such as effectiveness, feasibility, cost and equity, ending with a justified recommendation. The genre realises the situation–problem–response–evaluation pattern; characteristic language includes problem-signalling nouns (challenge, barrier, shortfall), evaluative lexis, conditional and hypothetical reasoning, and recommending language.

Prerequisites: [Problem, solution and evaluation](#funproblem-solution), [Recommending](#funrecommending) (soft), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating), [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft)

<a id="gesexam-essay"></a>
#### Examination essay

`ges.exam-essay` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write an essay under timed, usually closed-book conditions that answers the set question directly from revised knowledge, with a clear position, a quickly planned structure and selective use of remembered evidence. Compared with coursework essays, examiners typically expect a compact introduction, sustained relevance and less precisely referenced sources; expectations about citation, length and structure vary by department and should be checked in past papers, examiners' comments and module guidance.

Prerequisites: [Thesis statements](#essthesis-statement) (soft), [Planning revision](#exmrevision-planning) (soft), [Timed writing](#exmtimed-writing)

<a id="gesshort-answer"></a>
#### Short-answer responses

`ges.short-answer` · GENRE · B1–B2+ · foundation, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Write concise, accurate answers, from a sentence to a paragraph, that define, identify, explain or briefly justify, as required in coursework exercises, short-answer exam questions and in-class tests. Markers reward precision, correct terminology and completeness relative to the marks available rather than introductions or elaboration; typical language includes formal definitions, compact causal and procedural explanation, and dense noun phrases.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Defining terms](#fundefining), [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining), [Analysing exam questions](#exmquestion-analysis) (soft)

<a id="gescritique"></a>
#### Critical review of an article

`ges.critique` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write a review of a single research article or theoretical text that summarises its aims, approach and main claims accurately and concisely, then evaluates it against appropriate criteria (such as quality of evidence, methodological soundness, coherence of argument, contribution and relevance) and reaches a balanced overall judgement. Typical stages are context and purpose, summary, evaluation of strengths and limitations, and conclusion; characteristic language includes evaluative reporting verbs, mitigated criticism, praise–criticism pairs and hedged judgements.

Prerequisites: [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language), [Evaluating evidence in texts](#rdcevidence-evaluation), [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique) (soft), [Summary–response paper](#gessummary-response) (soft)

<a id="gesbook-review"></a>
#### Book review

`ges.book-review` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write a review of a scholarly book for an academic readership that situates the book in its field, describes its aims, scope and structure selectively, evaluates its argument, evidence and contribution, and recommends it, or not, to specified readers. Unlike an article critique set as coursework, a published book review is a public evaluative act within a discipline: reviewers balance and often mitigate criticism, and follow venue conventions on length, structure and bibliographic header.

Prerequisites: [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping) (soft), [Critical review of an article](#gescritique)

<a id="gesreflective-writing"></a>
#### Reflective writing

`ges.reflective-writing` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write structured reflection that describes a significant experience selectively, examines one's responses and assumptions, analyses the experience using relevant theory or evidence, and draws conclusions for future practice or learning. Assessed in education, health, business, engineering and EAP itself, the genre combines first-person, past-tense recount with present-tense analysis and future-oriented action planning; markers usually reward depth of analysis and links to theory rather than description or disclosure, and briefs differ widely on structure and citation.

Prerequisites: [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Chronological narration](#funchronology-narration) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory)

<a id="gesannotated-bibliography"></a>
#### Annotated bibliography

`ges.annotated-bibliography` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Compile an annotated bibliography in which each correctly formatted reference is followed by a concise annotation that summarises the source's purpose, approach and main findings or argument and, depending on the brief, evaluates its quality and explains its relevance to the writer's project. The genre isolates and trains selective summarising, source evaluation and relevance judgement; annotations are usually short paragraphs, report the source's claims with present-tense reporting verbs and mark evaluative and relevance comments as the writer's own.

Prerequisites: [Reading for a writing task](#rdcreading-for-writing) (soft), [Evaluating sources](#infsource-evaluation), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list)

<a id="gesliterature-review-assignment"></a>
#### Literature review assignment

`ges.literature-review-assignment` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write a coursework literature review, stand-alone or as a section of a larger assignment, that maps and synthesises the literature on a defined question thematically, evaluates the strength and limits of the evidence, identifies agreements, tensions and gaps, and positions a question or argument in relation to them. Typical stages are scope and rationale, thematic sections in which sources are placed in dialogue, and a conclusion summarising the state of knowledge and its gaps; characteristic language includes varied integral and non-integral citation, evaluative reporting verbs, comparison, and the present perfect for the state of a field.

Prerequisites: [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions), [Writing synthesis paragraphs](#synsynthesis-paragraphs), [Annotated bibliography](#gesannotated-bibliography) (soft)

<a id="gessummary-response"></a>
#### Summary–response paper

`ges.summary-response` · GENRE · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Write a paper that first summarises a source text accurately and even-handedly, then responds to it with a reasoned position (agreeing, disagreeing, qualifying or applying it to another context) supported by reasons and evidence. The genre makes explicit the boundary between reporting another writer's ideas and evaluating them and is a common bridge from reading to source-based argument; characteristic language includes attributive signal phrases and reporting verbs in the summary, and stance markers and concession in the response.

Prerequisites: [Formulating a critical response](#rdccritical-response), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [Distinguishing own voice from source voice](#srcvoice-boundaries)

### Report & Practice-Based Genres

This domain covers report-based, practice-based and professional genres in which students document investigations, analyse cases, justify designs, propose projects, summarise for decision-makers, reflect on practice and write for non-academic readers. Most are discipline-specific in their sections, terminology and evidence, so each topic sets out common purposes, stages and language while stressing that lab manuals, templates, handbooks and assignment briefs override any generic model. The domain draws on genre-family research on UK student writing, analyses of particular student genres such as the lab report and the business case report, and research on reflective writing.

<a id="grpreport-conventions"></a>
#### Report conventions

`grp.report-conventions` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand how academic and professional reports typically differ from essays: they respond to a defined problem, question or brief for a specified reader, are divided into numbered, headed sections that support selective reading, separate findings from interpretation, integrate captioned tables and figures, and often end with conclusions and recommendations. Section names and order vary widely across lab, project, business, technical and policy reports, so learners need to read the brief and departmental templates rather than assume a single report format.

Prerequisites: [Academic essay structure](#essessay-structure) (soft), [Genre families in university assessment](#gesgenre-families) (soft), [Figure and table captions](#datcaptions-labelling) (soft)

<a id="grplab-report"></a>
#### Laboratory report

`grp.lab-report` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write a laboratory report that records and interprets a practical investigation, typically with an introduction stating the relevant theory and the aim or hypothesis, a method, results presented in tables and figures with data commentary, a discussion that interprets the results against theory and expected values and evaluates sources of error and uncertainty, and a conclusion. Although it resembles the IMRaD research article, the student lab report usually serves to demonstrate understanding of established theory, procedure and data handling, often for an experiment with a known outcome, and its sections, tense and voice conventions vary with discipline, module and lab manual.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary), [Language of experimentation](#dsehypothesis-language) (soft)

<a id="grpempirical-project-report"></a>
#### Empirical project report

`grp.empirical-project-report` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write a report of a small-scale empirical study that the student has designed and conducted (for example a survey, interview study, observation or small experiment), presenting the rationale and research questions, a focused review of literature, methods with justification and ethical considerations, findings, a discussion relating findings to the literature, limitations and conclusions. Common on pre-sessional extended projects and in undergraduate and master's research modules, the genre apprentices learners into research reporting; its sections, length and quantitative or qualitative conventions vary by discipline and brief.

Prerequisites: [Literature review assignment](#gesliterature-review-assignment) (soft), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft)

<a id="grpcase-study"></a>
#### Case study analysis

`grp.case-study` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write a case study analysis that applies disciplinary concepts, theories or frameworks to a real or hypothetical case (an organisation, patient, client, project, school or event) to diagnose the key issues, evaluate options and, usually, recommend action. Typical stages are a brief case overview, identification and prioritisation of issues, analysis through theory or frameworks, evaluation of options, and recommendations, sometimes with implementation; the text may be report-like with headings or essay-like, and conventions differ sharply across business, health, law, education and engineering.

Prerequisites: [Recommending](#funrecommending) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory), [Analytical problem-solving](#crtproblem-solving), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions) (soft)

<a id="grpbusiness-report"></a>
#### Business report

`grp.business-report` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write a business report that investigates an organisational problem or opportunity for a specified decision-maker, analyses relevant evidence and data (often with management frameworks), presents findings and conclusions, and makes prioritised, feasible recommendations. Typical sections include an executive summary, introduction with terms of reference, findings or analysis, conclusions, recommendations and appendices; because the reader is assumed to be busy and decision-oriented, front-loading, informative headings and visuals matter, and assignment briefs often simulate a client or manager.

Prerequisites: [Recommending](#funrecommending), [Analytical problem-solving](#crtproblem-solving) (soft), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft)

<a id="grptechnical-design-report"></a>
#### Technical and design report

`grp.technical-design-report` · GENRE · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write a technical or design report that documents an engineering, computing or design project: the problem and requirements or specification, constraints, generation and evaluation of options against criteria, the selected design and its justification, implementation, testing or validation against requirements, and evaluation with recommendations for further work. The reader must be able to follow, assess and potentially reproduce decisions, so the genre relies on precise technical description, requirement and specification language, comparative evaluation, and close integration of diagrams, tables and calculations.

Prerequisites: [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions), [Referring to visuals in text](#datreferring-to-visuals) (soft)

<a id="grpproject-proposal"></a>
#### Project proposal (student)

`grp.project-proposal` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write a proposal for a student project or dissertation that defines the topic and research questions or aims, justifies them in relation to the literature and a gap or practical need, outlines methods and data sources, addresses ethics and feasibility, and presents a realistic timeline. The proposal persuades a supervisor or module team that the project is worthwhile, answerable and achievable; characteristic language includes future and conditional forms, purpose and justification expressions, and hedged statements about expected outcomes.

Prerequisites: [Managing extended writing projects](#wprmanaging-long-projects) (soft), [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions)

<a id="grpexecutive-summary"></a>
#### Executive summary

`grp.executive-summary` · GENRE · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write an executive summary that lets a decision-maker grasp a report's purpose, key findings, conclusions and recommendations without reading the full document. It stands alone, usually appears at the front, is short relative to the report, orders content by importance to the reader rather than by the report's section order, and states recommendations explicitly; unlike an academic abstract, it foregrounds implications and actions over method.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Recommending](#funrecommending) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions)

<a id="grppolicy-brief"></a>
#### Policy brief

`grp.policy-brief` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write a short, evidence-based document that presents a policy problem, summarises and weighs the relevant evidence, sets out options with their trade-offs and recommends action to a non-specialist policy audience such as officials, practitioners or NGOs. Typical features are a front-loaded set of key messages, a concise problem statement, accessible synthesis of research with its strength signalled, options, and concrete recommendations addressed to named actors, presented in a few pages with headings and visuals; tone is authoritative but calibrated to the evidence, and organisations use their own templates.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Recommending](#funrecommending), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Executive summary](#grpexecutive-summary) (soft)

<a id="grpprofessional-portfolio"></a>
#### Professional practice portfolio

`grp.professional-portfolio` · GENRE · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Compile and write a professional practice portfolio for a placement, practicum or work-based module: dated logs of activities, reflective accounts of significant incidents, and evidence mapped to professional standards or competencies, held together by a commentary that synthesises development and plans further learning. Assessed in nursing and allied health, teacher education, social work, engineering and business placements, the genre combines factual recording, reflection and evidence-based argument, requires strict attention to confidentiality, and follows highly specific institutional and professional-body templates.

Prerequisites: [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing), [Reflective learning logs](#lrnreflective-logs) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria) (soft)

<a id="grppublic-writing"></a>
#### Academic blogs and public-facing writing

`grp.public-writing` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write academic blog posts, opinion pieces and other outreach texts that communicate research or disciplinary perspectives to non-specialist or mixed readers. Compared with academic genres, these texts lead with the main point, use a more personal and engaging voice, concrete examples, short paragraphs and links rather than formal citation, and signal uncertainty without technical hedging; they also require judgements about representing evidence fairly, the writer's public identity and platform norms.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Reader engagement](#staengagement-markers) (soft), [Rhetorical appeals](#argrhetorical-appeals) (soft), [Digital etiquette and online presence](#digdigital-etiquette) (soft)

<a id="grpdissertation-overview"></a>
#### Undergraduate and master's dissertation

`grp.dissertation-overview` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand and plan the undergraduate or taught master's dissertation as a genre: an extended, largely independent piece of research or scholarship whose typical macrostructures include empirical IMRaD-like chapters, topic-based or theoretical structures and practice-based variants, with expectations of scope, length and originality that are lower than for doctoral theses. Learners need to see how proposal, literature review, methodology, findings, discussion and conclusion form one argument across chapters, and how supervision and assessment work; because expectations vary across disciplines and institutions, the handbook, marking criteria and past dissertations are the key sources.

Prerequisites: [Analysing genres independently](#gesgenre-analysis) (soft), [Literature review assignment](#gesliterature-review-assignment), [Empirical project report](#grpempirical-project-report) (soft), [Project proposal (student)](#grpproject-proposal) (soft)

<a id="grpstudent-abstract"></a>
#### Abstracts for student work

`grp.student-abstract` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write an abstract for a dissertation, project report or poster that summarises, in a stand-alone paragraph within a strict word limit, the context or problem, aim, method, main findings and conclusion or implication. Student abstracts follow moves similar to those of research abstracts but are judged mainly on accuracy, completeness and concision; tense typically shifts between present for context and conclusions and past for methods and findings, and some fields require structured formats with headings.

Prerequisites: [Tense when reporting research](#grmtense-in-reporting) (soft), [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad) (soft)

## Research Communication

### Research Writing

Research Writing covers the part-genres, macrostructures and practices through which postgraduate and early-career writers report and position their own research: introductions built on CARS-type moves, literature reviews, framework and methods sections, results, discussions, conclusions, abstracts, theses and proposals. Move analyses (Swales 1990, 2004; Kanoksilapatham 2005; Yang & Allison 2003; Kwan 2006) are descriptive models to test against disciplinary exemplars, not templates, and IMRaD is dominant in experimental fields but far from universal. The domain also covers the sociopragmatic and self-regulatory work of extended research writing, such as supervision, sustaining output and writing reflexively, which Kamler and Thomson (2014) treat as both text work and identity work.

<a id="resimrad"></a>
#### IMRaD and its variants

`res.imrad` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP
