Recognise the Introduction–Methods–Results–Discussion macrostructure as a conventional way of reporting empirical research, explain what each section does for the reader, and identify the variants used in one's own field, such as merged Results and Discussion, a separate Conclusions section, a theory section before Methods, or argument-led organisation in the humanities. Knowing the macrostructure lets writers put content where readers expect it, while recognising IMRaD as a disciplinary convention rather than a universal norm.

Prerequisites: [Navigating research articles](#rdsresearch-article-navigation), [Genre and discourse community](#gesgenre-concept) (soft), [Disciplinary knowledge-making](#acudisciplinary-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="resintroductions-cars"></a>
#### Research article introductions

`res.introductions-cars` · GENRE · B2+–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write research introductions that create a research space: establishing a territory (claiming centrality, generalising, reviewing previous research), establishing a niche, and occupying it by announcing the present research, its questions, and optionally its findings, value and structure. Writers adapt the model to their field and genre, including the cyclical moves, extended background and thesis-structure statements common in some disciplines and in PhD introductions, and they use it as an analytical lens on exemplars rather than as a fill-in template.

Prerequisites: [Writing introductions](#essintroductions) (soft), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions) (soft), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche)

<a id="resliterature-review"></a>
#### Literature review in research writing

`res.literature-review` · GENRE · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write the literature review of a thesis, dissertation or article as an argument that positions the writer's own study: selecting and organising literature by theme, debate or problem, evaluating rather than merely reporting it, and building cumulatively towards the niche the study fills. Unlike a stand-alone coursework review, the research literature review is judged by how well it justifies the present study's questions, framework and design.

Prerequisites: [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping), [Writing synthesis paragraphs](#synsynthesis-paragraphs), [Rhetorical functions of citation](#citcitation-functions) (soft), [Literature review assignment](#gesliterature-review-assignment) (soft), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche)

<a id="resmethods"></a>
#### Methods sections

`res.methods` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write methods sections that describe the study (setting, participants or materials, instruments, procedures, data analysis) in enough detail for evaluation or replication and that establish credibility by justifying choices, citing precedents and addressing validity, reliability or trustworthiness. Move structure, level of detail, voice and the balance of description and justification vary considerably across disciplines and between laboratory, survey and qualitative traditions, so writers calibrate against field exemplars and reporting guidelines.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad)

<a id="resresults"></a>
#### Results sections

`res.results` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Present findings in a logical order that answers the research questions, using visuals and prose together: locating the reader in a table or figure, highlighting what is significant, and adding the degree of commentary the field allows (from minimal to substantial interpretation). Writers decide what to report in text and what to leave in visuals, separate reporting from interpretation where the genre requires it, and keep claims proportionate to the data.

Prerequisites: [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary)

<a id="resdiscussion"></a>
#### Discussion sections

`res.discussion` · GENRE · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write discussion sections that move from results to meaning: restating key findings in relation to the research questions, comparing them with previous research, explaining expected and unexpected outcomes, evaluating the study's limitations, and drawing implications. Discussions are typically cyclical, repeating statement–comparison–explanation sequences for each major finding, and their persuasiveness depends on claims calibrated to the evidence and on alternative explanations considered rather than dismissed.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources) (soft), [Results sections](#resresults), [Writing about limitations](#reslimitations) (soft)

<a id="resconclusions-implications"></a>
#### Conclusions and implications

`res.conclusions-implications` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write concluding sections and chapters that consolidate the research: briefly restating aims and key findings, answering the research questions, articulating the contribution, drawing calibrated implications for theory, practice or policy, evaluating the study and recommending specific further research. In theses, the conclusion also looks across chapters and back to the field, and conventions vary on whether conclusions form a separate section in articles.

Prerequisites: [Recommending](#funrecommending) (soft), [Writing conclusions](#essconclusions) (soft), [Discussion sections](#resdiscussion), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution)

<a id="resabstracts"></a>
#### Research abstracts

`res.abstracts` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write informative abstracts for articles, dissertations and theses that represent the whole study within a tight word limit, typically situating the research, stating its purpose, describing the method, summarising key results and stating conclusions or implications, either as continuous prose or under the headings of a structured abstract. Abstracts are read as stand-alone texts by searchers, editors and examiners, so they are both a summary and a promotional genre, and their move emphasis varies across disciplines and journals.

Prerequisites: [Abstracts for student work](#grpstudent-abstract) (soft), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft)

<a id="reslimitations"></a>
#### Writing about limitations

`res.limitations` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify the limitations that genuinely affect how a study's findings can be interpreted (sample, design, measurement, analysis, scope) and write about them so that readers can judge the claims, by stating each limitation, its likely effect on the findings and, where possible, how it was mitigated or could be addressed. Well-written limitations protect the contribution by showing the writer's command of the design, whereas generic or apologetic lists undermine it.

Prerequisites: [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language) (soft), [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique), [Methods sections](#resmethods) (soft)

<a id="resresearch-questions"></a>
#### Research questions and hypotheses

`res.research-questions` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Formulate and present an aim, research questions and, where the design calls for them, hypotheses that are answerable, appropriately scoped and aligned with the stated gap and the chosen methods. Writers also choose how to present them, as numbered questions, objectives or a purpose statement, and use consistent wording across the introduction, methods and discussion. Well-formed questions are the reference point that examiners and reviewers use to judge the coherence of the whole study.

Prerequisites: [Predicting and hypothesising](#funhypothesising) (soft), [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question), [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions) (soft)

<a id="resgap-niche"></a>
#### Establishing a research gap

`res.gap-niche` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Construct the textual case that a niche exists, through counter-claiming, indicating a gap, raising a question, continuing a tradition or adding to what is known, using the lexicogrammar that realises these moves: contrastive connectors, negative or restrictive quantifiers (few studies, little attention), present perfect for the state of research and evaluative lexis. The niche must follow from the literature reviewed and lead to the present study, and it should be stated with appropriate caution, since an overstated gap ('no research exists') is easily refuted.

Prerequisites: [Tense when reporting research](#grmtense-in-reporting) (soft), [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language) (soft), [Identifying gaps and tensions](#syngaps-tensions)

<a id="rescontribution"></a>
#### Claiming contribution

`res.contribution` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

State what the research adds (new evidence, a new method, a new context, a theoretical refinement or a practical application) and why it matters, at a level of confidence the evidence supports. Writers relate the contribution explicitly to the niche established earlier, choose between self-mention and impersonal framing, and balance promotional and cautious language according to field and genre. Doctoral examiners and journal reviewers look for this statement, and its absence or overstatement is a common reason for criticism.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Establishing a research gap](#resgap-niche)

<a id="resthesis-macrostructure"></a>
#### Thesis macrostructure

`res.thesis-macrostructure` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose and justify an overall organisation for a dissertation or thesis — traditional simple (IMRaD-like), traditional complex (several studies), topic-based, or publication-based (compilation of articles with framing chapters) — and plan chapters that fit the research, the institution's regulations and disciplinary norms. Writers understand what each chapter type contributes, how a publication-based thesis builds coherence across stand-alone papers, and what examiners will look for in the whole document.

Prerequisites: [Undergraduate and master's dissertation](#grpdissertation-overview) (soft), [IMRaD and its variants](#resimrad), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process) (soft)

<a id="reschapter-metatext"></a>
#### Chapter introductions, conclusions and metatext

`res.chapter-metatext` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Guide readers through a long document with metatext above the paragraph level: chapter introductions that state purpose and preview structure, chapter conclusions that summarise and link forward, cross-references between chapters, and sign-posting that reminds the reader how the current section serves the thesis argument. Writers calibrate the density of metatext so that it orients the reader without padding or repeating content.

Prerequisites: [Frame markers and advance organisers](#cohframe-markers), [Transitions between paragraphs and sections](#cohparagraph-transitions) (soft), [Summarising and concluding](#funsummarising-concluding) (soft), [Thesis macrostructure](#resthesis-macrostructure)

<a id="restheoretical-framework"></a>
#### Theoretical and conceptual frameworks

`res.theoretical-framework` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write a theoretical or conceptual framework section that explains which theories, models or concepts the study uses, why they were chosen over alternatives, how key constructs are defined and operationalised, and how the framework shapes the questions, data collection and analysis. The section should do analytical work later in the text rather than function as a free-standing summary of theory, and it should acknowledge disciplinary differences in whether and how frameworks are presented.

Prerequisites: [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory), [Literature review in research writing](#resliterature-review) (soft), [Working with concepts](#dssoperationalising-concepts) (soft)

<a id="resqualitative-reporting"></a>
#### Reporting qualitative findings

`res.qualitative-reporting` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Present qualitative findings as an argued interpretation: organising them by theme or category, selecting and introducing participant extracts that illustrate and evidence analytic claims, commenting on each extract, and keeping the analyst's voice distinct from participants' voices. Writers show the range and prevalence of patterns without spurious counting, attend to negative cases, and present extracts ethically and consistently, with pseudonyms, transcription conventions and edits marked.

Prerequisites: [Averral versus attribution](#staaverral-attribution) (soft), [Integrating quotations](#srcintegrating-quotations), [Results sections](#resresults) (soft)

<a id="resquantitative-reporting"></a>
#### Reporting quantitative findings

`res.quantitative-reporting` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Report descriptive and inferential statistics in the conventional format of the field or style guide: test statistics, degrees of freedom, exact p-values, effect sizes and confidence intervals in text and tables, with numbers, symbols and units styled correctly. Writers interpret statistical results in words a reader can follow, distinguish statistical significance from practical importance, and avoid the language of proof or of 'trends towards significance'.

Prerequisites: [Writing numbers, units and symbols in text](#sennumbers-units-style) (soft), [Results sections](#resresults), [Interpreting statistical claims](#numinterpreting-statistics)

<a id="resresearch-proposal"></a>
#### Research proposals

`res.research-proposal` · GENRE · B2+–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write proposals for doctoral admission, confirmation or funding that persuade reviewers a project is significant, original, feasible and well designed: establishing the problem and gap, stating aims and questions, outlining methods, timeline and resources, anticipating risks and ethics, and articulating expected contributions and benefits. Proposals are promissory and evaluative, written in future and conditional terms for readers who must decide quickly, and funders' templates and criteria shape their structure.

Prerequisites: [Project proposal (student)](#grpproject-proposal) (soft), [Literature review in research writing](#resliterature-review), [Methods sections](#resmethods) (soft), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft)

<a id="resethics-documentation"></a>
#### Ethics applications and participant documents

`res.ethics-documentation` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Complete research ethics applications that describe procedures, risks, benefits, consent, confidentiality and data management accurately, and write participant information sheets and consent forms in plain, accessible and, where needed, translated language that enables genuinely informed consent. The genres serve two audiences, the ethics committee and participants, with different register demands, and they commit the researcher to procedures that later methods sections must match.

Prerequisites: [Inclusive and bias-free language](#reginclusive-language) (soft), [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process) (soft), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity)

<a id="ressupervision"></a>
#### Working with supervisors

`res.supervision` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Manage the supervisory relationship through talk and writing: negotiating expectations, preparing agendas and submitting drafts with specific requests for feedback, interpreting and responding to written comments, disagreeing respectfully, and recording decisions and actions after meetings. Supervisory conventions vary across institutions, disciplines and individuals, so learners make these expectations explicit rather than assume them.

Prerequisites: [Running group meetings](#grwmeetings) (soft), [Seeking feedback](#fbkseeking-feedback), [Office hours and tutorials](#prgoffice-hours) (soft)

<a id="reswriting-productivity"></a>
#### Sustaining thesis writing

`res.writing-productivity` · STRATEGIC · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Sustain writing across a multi-year project by establishing regular, low-stakes writing routines, setting and monitoring realistic writing goals, using writing groups, retreats and accountability partners, separating generative writing from revision, and treating writing as part of doing research rather than as 'writing up' at the end. The aim is steady output and recovery from setbacks without compromising wellbeing.

Prerequisites: [Managing extended writing projects](#wprmanaging-long-projects), [Overcoming writing blocks](#wprwriting-blocks) (soft), [Sustainable study habits and wellbeing](#lrnwellbeing) (soft)

<a id="respositionality-writing"></a>
#### Writing positionality statements

`res.positionality-writing` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write positionality and reflexivity statements that explain how the researcher's identities, experiences, relationships to participants and theoretical commitments may have shaped access, data and interpretation, and what was done about it. Effective statements are analytic and specific to the study rather than confessional or biographical, use first-person reference according to field conventions, and are integrated with methodological decisions rather than isolated in a separate box.

Prerequisites: [Impersonality and authorial presence](#regimpersonality-presence) (soft), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention), [Reflexivity and positionality](#crtreflexivity), [Methods sections](#resmethods) (soft)

### Publication & Dissemination

Publication & Dissemination covers the genres, practices and politics through which research reaches readers and is evaluated: the publishing process, journal choice, the occluded genres around submission (cover letters, reviewer reports, responses to reviewers), conference abstracts, the doctoral viva, authorship and contributorship, public engagement and academic self-presentation. Many of these genres are hidden from novices because they are rarely published, so making exemplars and expectations visible is central to teaching. The domain also addresses the opportunities and inequities multilingual scholars meet in English-medium publishing (Flowerdew 1999; Lillis & Curry 2010) and treats them as structural issues, not individual deficits.

<a id="pubpublishing-process"></a>
#### The academic publishing process

`pub.publishing-process` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand the stages through which a manuscript becomes a publication — submission, editorial screening and desk rejection, peer review (single-anonymous, double-anonymous, open), decisions (accept, minor or major revision, reject and resubmit, reject), revision, acceptance, production and post-publication correction or retraction — and the roles of editors, reviewers and authors in each. Understanding the process as a negotiation within a discourse community helps novice authors interpret decisions, plan timelines and avoid treating rejection as a final verdict on their work.

Prerequisites: [How scholarly knowledge is produced](#infscholarly-communication), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity) (soft)

<a id="pubjournal-selection"></a>
#### Selecting a journal

`pub.journal-selection` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C1 · pgr · EGAP

Choose a target journal by matching the manuscript's contribution, method and audience to a journal's aims and scope, readership, article types, recent content, review practices, indexing, open-access options and costs, and turnaround, while screening out predatory or deceptive venues. Writers then read and apply the author guidelines and shape the manuscript for that journal's conversation, ideally choosing the journal before drafting rather than after.

Prerequisites: [Predatory publishing, retractions and misinformation](#infproblematic-publishing), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process)

<a id="pubcover-letters"></a>
#### Cover letters to editors

`pub.cover-letters` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · pgr · EGAP

Write submission letters that identify the manuscript and article type, state concisely what it contributes and why it fits the journal's scope and readership, and make the declarations the journal requires (originality, no simultaneous submission, conflicts of interest, ethics, data availability, suggested or excluded reviewers). The letter is an occluded genre, rarely seen by novices, whose readers are busy editors deciding whether to send a paper for review, so it must be brief, specific and professional.

Prerequisites: [Reusing one's own work](#intrecycling-own-work) (soft), [Selecting a journal](#pubjournal-selection), [Emails to academic staff](#prgemails-to-staff) (soft)

<a id="pubresponding-reviewers"></a>
#### Responding to reviewers

`pub.responding-reviewers` · GENRE · C1–C2 · pgr · EGAP

Write response-to-reviewer letters that thank reviewers, address every comment point by point, describe precisely what was changed and where, and explain politely and with evidence when a requested change has not been made. The genre requires managing face, since authors must disagree with gatekeepers while remaining cooperative, as well as clear organisation and consistency between the response and the revised manuscript.

Prerequisites: [Language of concession and counter-argument](#staconcession-language) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process), [Acting on feedback](#fbkacting-on-feedback) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="pubpeer-review-reports"></a>
#### Writing peer review reports

`pub.peer-review-reports` · GENRE · C1–C2 · pgr · EGAP

Write reviewer reports for journals or conferences that summarise the manuscript and its claimed contribution, evaluate its significance, methods, evidence and argument, distinguish major from minor issues, make actionable suggestions and give a recommendation consistent with the comments. Reports must be constructive, appropriately mitigated and respectful, and reviewers must meet ethical obligations of confidentiality, competence, timeliness and disclosure of conflicts of interest.

Prerequisites: [Language for evaluating others' work](#staevaluating-others-language), [Reading methods critically](#rdcmethods-critique), [Peer review of drafts](#edtpeer-review) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process)

<a id="pubconference-abstracts"></a>
#### Conference abstracts

`pub.conference-abstracts` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write abstracts and proposals for conference submission that persuade reviewers of relevance to the conference theme, interest and feasibility, often before the research is complete. Writers adapt the research-abstract moves to a call for papers: framing the topic for the conference audience, stating the aim and approach, reporting results or expected outcomes honestly, and signalling what attendees will gain, within strict word limits and format requirements.

Prerequisites: [Predicting and hypothesising](#funhypothesising) (soft), [Research abstracts](#resabstracts)

<a id="pubviva"></a>
#### Thesis defence and viva

`pub.viva` · GENRE · C1–C2 · pgr · EGAP

Prepare for and take part in the oral examination of a thesis: understanding the local format and possible outcomes, re-reading the thesis strategically, anticipating questions about contribution, design, findings, limitations and future work, and answering by explaining, defending, conceding and clarifying in extended spoken interaction with expert examiners. Formats vary widely, from closed UK-style vivas with internal and external examiners to public defences with an opponent in parts of continental Europe and committee defences in North America, while in some systems, such as many Australian universities, there is no oral examination.

Prerequisites: [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Arguing orally](#argoral-argument), [Writing about limitations](#reslimitations) (soft), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution), [Oral examinations](#exmoral-exams) (soft)

<a id="pubauthorship"></a>
#### Authorship and contribution

`pub.authorship` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand what qualifies someone for authorship, using criteria such as the ICMJE's, and how contributions are described with contributor-role taxonomies such as CRediT, how author order conventions differ across disciplines, and what counts as honorary, guest or ghost authorship. Researchers should be able to discuss authorship early in collaborations, including with supervisors, and to write accurate contribution statements and acknowledgements, including declarations of the use of AI tools, which journals do not accept as authors.

Prerequisites: [Collaborative writing](#grwcollaborative-writing) (soft), [Assessing contributions](#grwcontribution-assessment) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process)

<a id="pubmultilingual-scholars"></a>
#### Publishing as a multilingual scholar

`pub.multilingual-scholars` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 · pgr · EGAP

Understand the opportunities and inequities of English-medium publishing for multilingual scholars: the dominance of English in high-status indexes, the extra time and cost of writing in an additional language, the material constraints faced by scholars outside well-resourced institutions, reviewer and editor attitudes to non-standard English, and the role of literacy brokers such as co-authors, editors, translators and language professionals. Scholars can use this understanding to make informed choices about language, venue, collaboration and support, and to recognise structural barriers as structural rather than personal failings.

Prerequisites: [Standards, native-speakerism and legitimacy](#idnstandards-legitimacy) (soft), [The academic publishing process](#pubpublishing-process)

<a id="pubresearch-engagement"></a>
#### Communicating research beyond academia

`pub.research-engagement` · PROCEDURAL · C1–C2 · in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Communicate research to non-academic audiences such as the public, practitioners, policymakers and industry through lay summaries, blogs, social media, talks, visual summaries and impact case material, adapting content, structure, register and claims to each audience while keeping them accurate. Researchers also plan engagement deliberately, as many funders and evaluation systems expect, and manage the risks of oversimplification and online visibility.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Claiming contribution](#rescontribution) (soft), [Infographics and visual summaries](#mmdinfographics) (soft), [Digital etiquette and online presence](#digdigital-etiquette) (soft)

<a id="pubacademic-cv-bio"></a>
#### Academic CVs, biographies and personal statements

`pub.academic-cv-bio` · GENRE · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write the self-presentation genres of academic careers: academic CVs, with conventional sections, consistent referencing of outputs and evidence of research, teaching and service; short biographical statements for conferences, journals and profiles, often in the third person; and personal statements for postgraduate study, scholarships and posts, which must show fit, motivation and capability with evidence. Each genre balances self-promotion with modesty according to its conventions, and conventions differ across countries and sectors.

Prerequisites: [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft), [Academic and writer identity](#idnacademic-identity) (soft)

## Data, Numeracy & Multimodality

### Data Commentary & Visuals

Data commentary is where numerical and visual evidence becomes argument: learners must read visuals accurately, select what matters, and write or speak about it with claims calibrated to the evidence rather than exhaustive description. The domain runs from visual literacy (visual types, reading graphs and tables, recognising misleading displays) through the lexicogrammar of trends, proportions and diagrams, to the genre of data commentary and the conventions for labelling, referring to and creating visuals. IELTS Writing Task 1 is treated as a contrast case: it rewards selective description of key features, whereas university data commentary also interprets, explains, compares and qualifies.

<a id="datvisual-types"></a>
#### Types of visuals and their functions

`dat.visual-types` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Recognise the main visual forms in academic texts (line graphs, bar charts, pie charts, scatterplots, tables, flowcharts, diagrams, maps and photographs) and the relationship each is designed to show: change over time, comparison between categories, part–whole composition, distribution, association between variables, sequence or spatial layout. Knowing what a visual is for guides how it is read and which language is needed to describe it.

<a id="datreading-graphs"></a>
#### Reading graphs and charts

`dat.reading-graphs` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Read graphs and charts systematically: check the title, axes, scales, units, legend and source before interpreting; then extract values, compare across series, and identify the overall pattern, exceptions and notable features. Accurate reading at the levels of individual data, relationships between data and inferences beyond the data underpins both data commentary and critical evaluation of others’ visuals.

Prerequisites: [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft)

<a id="datreading-tables"></a>
#### Reading tables

`dat.reading-tables` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Navigate tables by identifying what rows and columns represent, the units, totals, notes, abbreviations and significance markers; locate specific values; and compare across rows or columns to identify the main pattern. Research articles often carry findings in tables that the prose mentions only briefly, so efficient table reading is essential for accurate source use.

Prerequisites: [Scanning for specific information](#rdsscanning) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft)

<a id="datdescribing-trends"></a>
#### Describing trends and change

`dat.describing-trends` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Describe change over time precisely using verbs (rise, fall, fluctuate, level off, peak), nouns (an increase in, a decline of), degree and speed modifiers (slightly, sharply, a gradual rise), prepositions of quantity and time (from … to, by, at, over the period) and appropriate tense. Precise trend language lets writers summarise patterns economically and select only the changes that matter to their point.

Prerequisites: [Tense and aspect in academic writing](#grmtense-aspect) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs)

<a id="datdescribing-proportions"></a>
#### Describing proportions and comparisons

`dat.describing-proportions` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Express proportions and comparisons in data precisely, using fractions, percentages, ratios and multiples (a third of, 42% of, a ratio of 3:1, twice as many as), quantifiers (the majority, a substantial minority, nearly all) and comparative and superlative structures with numbers. This allows exact, checkable comparisons between groups and categories rather than vague impressions.

Prerequisites: [Comparative structures](#grmcomparison-structures), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

<a id="datdata-commentary"></a>
#### Data commentary

`dat.data-commentary` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write commentary that uses data as evidence: locate the visual and summarise what it shows, highlight the findings that matter for the writer’s purpose, and interpret them by explaining, comparing with expectations or previous studies, acknowledging limitations and drawing implications whose strength matches the evidence. Data commentary is the core of results and discussion writing across disciplines and differs from description-only tasks such as IELTS Writing Task 1.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration), [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft), [Describing trends and change](#datdescribing-trends), [Describing proportions and comparisons](#datdescribing-proportions), [Referring to visuals in text](#datreferring-to-visuals)

<a id="datdescribing-diagrams"></a>
#### Describing processes, diagrams and maps

`dat.describing-diagrams` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Verbalise non-numerical visuals: the stages, branches and loops of processes and cycles in flowcharts; the components and spatial relations in diagrams of structures and systems; and spatial change shown in maps and plans. This draws on sequencing language, voice choice, purpose and result expressions and locative prepositions, and supports explaining scientific, technical and planning visuals in reports, examinations and presentations.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft)

<a id="datcaptions-labelling"></a>
#### Figure and table captions

`dat.captions-labelling` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Number, title and caption figures and tables according to the required style, write informative titles and notes that let the visual stand alone, define abbreviations, symbols and units, and acknowledge the source of reproduced or adapted visuals. Correct labelling makes visuals referable in the text and, when visuals or data come from others, is part of academic integrity.

Prerequisites: [When to cite](#citwhen-to-cite) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft)

<a id="datcreating-visuals"></a>
#### Creating effective visuals

`dat.creating-visuals` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose the visual form that best serves a message and audience, and design it for clarity and honesty: an appropriate chart type, honest scales and baselines, minimal non-data decoration, direct labelling, accessible colour and enough context (units, sample size, uncertainty). Well-designed visuals let readers check claims quickly and are increasingly assessed in reports, posters and theses.

Prerequisites: [Audience and purpose](#wpraudience-purpose) (soft), [Recognising misleading visuals](#datmisleading-visuals)

<a id="datmisleading-visuals"></a>
#### Recognising misleading visuals

`dat.misleading-visuals` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise design and data choices that distort a visual’s message (truncated or inconsistent axes, selective time windows, dual axes, area and 3D distortion, mismatched chart types, missing denominators or uncertainty) and judge whether conclusions drawn from the visual are justified. This is a component of statistical literacy and of the critical evaluation of sources.

Prerequisites: [What critical reading involves](#rdccritical-reading) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs)

<a id="datreferring-to-visuals"></a>
#### Referring to visuals in text

`dat.referring-to-visuals` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Integrate visuals into prose by referring to them explicitly and purposefully (Figure 2 shows; as shown in Table 1; see Figure 3), placing references where the reader needs them, choosing verbs that suit visual subjects and the strength of the claim (show, indicate, illustrate, suggest), and telling the reader what to notice. This links visual evidence to the argument instead of leaving visuals to speak for themselves.

Prerequisites: [Verbs for academic and research processes](#vocacademic-verbs) (soft), [Integrating text and visuals](#rdsvisuals-in-text) (soft), [Figure and table captions](#datcaptions-labelling) (soft)

### Academic Numeracy Language

Academic numeracy language is the lexicogrammar and conceptual precision needed to talk and write about quantities, measurements, statistics and mathematical relations in English. Errors here are rarely ‘just language’: confusing per cent with percentage points, using ‘significant’ loosely or misplacing a unit changes the claim. The domain runs from accurate number expressions and approximation, through units and mathematical expression, to statistical vocabulary and consumer-level interpretation of statistical claims, drawing on research into statistical literacy (Gal, 2002) and risk communication (Gigerenzer et al., 2007). EAP teachers without statistical training may wish to co-teach the later topics with subject or mathematics-support colleagues.

<a id="numnumber-expressions"></a>
#### Expressing quantities accurately

`num.number-expressions` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · EGAP

Express numbers, fractions, decimals, percentages, ratios and rates accurately in prose, together with the grammar around them: articles and of-phrases (a third of, two-thirds of the sample), agreement (a quarter of respondents were), per cent versus percentage, number of versus amount of, rates (per capita, per 1,000) and multiplicative forms. Accurate quantity expressions are the basis of every claim made from data.

Prerequisites: [Subject–verb agreement with complex subjects](#grmsubject-verb-agreement) (soft), [Determiners and quantifiers](#grmdeterminers-quantifiers) (soft)

<a id="numapproximation"></a>
#### Approximation and precision

`num.approximation` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Approximate and round quantities appropriately (approximately, roughly, just under, well over, in the region of, nearly a third, an estimated) and decide when exact figures or approximation better serve the reader and purpose. Approximators also function as hedges: they signal the writer’s commitment to exactness and must be consistent with the figures and with the precision of the measurement.

Prerequisites: [Comparative structures](#grmcomparison-structures) (soft), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

<a id="numstatistical-vocabulary"></a>
#### Basic statistical vocabulary

`num.statistical-vocabulary` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand and use core statistical terms accurately in reading and writing: population and sample, variable, mean, median, mode, range, standard deviation, distribution, correlation, outlier, significance, error and bias. Several of these words have everyday meanings that differ from their technical sense (significant, normal, error, random, confidence, bias), so precise use is essential for credible methods and results writing.

Prerequisites: [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy) (soft), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions) (soft)

<a id="numinterpreting-statistics"></a>
#### Interpreting statistical claims

`num.interpreting-statistics` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Read and interpret inferential statistics at an informed consumer level: understand what p-values, confidence intervals and effect sizes do and do not indicate, distinguish statistical significance from practical importance, and recognise how sample size and design limit conclusions. This allows learners to evaluate quantitative sources and report them accurately without overstating them.

Prerequisites: [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft), [Basic statistical vocabulary](#numstatistical-vocabulary), [Relative and absolute change](#numrelative-absolute) (soft)

<a id="numrelative-absolute"></a>
#### Relative and absolute change

`num.relative-absolute` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Distinguish relative from absolute change (per cent versus percentage points; relative versus absolute risk; rates versus counts) and choose and interpret framings that do not mislead. The same result can be presented as ‘a 50% reduction’ or as ‘a fall from 2 in 1,000 to 1 in 1,000’, and readers and writers need to recognise how framing shapes judgement.

Prerequisites: [Describing trends and change](#datdescribing-trends) (soft), [Describing proportions and comparisons](#datdescribing-proportions)

<a id="numunits-measurement"></a>
#### Units and measurement

`num.units-measurement` · LINGUISTIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · ESAP

Report measurements using SI units and symbols correctly (symbols versus names, a space between number and unit, prefixes, no plural or full stop on symbols, compound units such as m/s or kg m⁻³) and follow discipline and style-guide conventions where these differ. Correct use of units is a precondition for credibility and replicability in science, engineering and health writing.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

<a id="nummathematical-expression"></a>
#### Language of mathematical expression

`num.mathematical-expression` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Read and write mathematical expressions in academic prose: verbalising equations and inequalities, introducing and defining variables (where x denotes; let n be), integrating displayed equations into sentences with appropriate punctuation, and using conventional phrasing for relationships (is proportional to, tends to, is a function of). This is needed wherever formulae carry the argument, as in science, engineering, economics and quantitative social science.

Prerequisites: [Defining terms](#fundefining) (soft), [Expressing quantities accurately](#numnumber-expressions)

### Multimodal Composition

Academic communication increasingly combines writing with images, layout, colour, sound and movement, and university assessment now includes posters, infographics, podcasts, video essays and e-portfolios. This domain treats multimodal composition as meaning-making with distinct semiotic resources (Kress & van Leeuwen, 2006), addresses the design of key academic multimodal genres (MacIntosh-Murray, 2007), and treats accessibility as a design requirement from the outset rather than an afterthought (World Wide Web Consortium, 2023). Academic expectations of argument, evidence and attribution carry over into these genres, even though the means of realising them change.

<a id="mmdmultimodal-literacy"></a>
#### Multimodal literacy

`mmd.multimodal-literacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand how image, layout, colour, typography and text combine to make meaning, and analyse how a multimodal text directs attention through salience, reading path, framing and the placement of information. This awareness underpins both critical reading of academic multimodal texts and the design of posters, slides and digital assignments.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Types of visuals and their functions](#datvisual-types) (soft)

<a id="mmdposter-design"></a>
#### Designing academic posters

`mmd.poster-design` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Design an academic poster that communicates a clear main message at a glance and supports conversation in a poster session: a hierarchy of title, key finding and supporting sections; a logical reading path; economical text; visuals that carry the evidence; and legibility at viewing distance. The poster is a genre with its own norms, not a shrunken paper.

Prerequisites: [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Creating effective visuals](#datcreating-visuals) (soft), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)

<a id="mmdinfographics"></a>
#### Infographics and visual summaries

`mmd.infographics` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Condense information into visual summaries (infographics, visual abstracts and one-page summaries) for academic and public audiences: select the essential message, structure it visually, and preserve accuracy, attribution and appropriate qualification. Visual summaries are increasingly used in assessment and research dissemination, and they risk oversimplification when impact is pursued at the expense of accuracy.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences) (soft), [Creating effective visuals](#datcreating-visuals) (soft), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)

<a id="mmddigital-assessments"></a>
#### Video essays and digital assessments

`mmd.digital-assessments` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Produce assessed multimodal genres (podcasts, video essays, narrated presentations, e-portfolios and websites), understanding their purposes, criteria and conventions, and orchestrating speech, text, image and sound to build an academic argument with appropriate attribution of sources and media. These genres keep the core academic expectations of argument, evidence and citation while changing the means of expression.

Prerequisites: [Recorded and online presentations](#prsrecorded-online) (soft), [Citing diverse source types](#citdiverse-sources) (soft), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)

<a id="mmdaccessibility"></a>
#### Accessible academic documents

`mmd.accessibility` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Produce documents, slides and media that are accessible to readers with disabilities and to assistive technologies: structured headings created with styles, meaningful alternative text for images and charts, sufficient colour contrast, meaning not conveyed by colour alone, descriptive link text, readable typography, and captions or transcripts for audio-visual content. Accessibility is an institutional and often legal expectation, and it improves clarity for all readers.

Prerequisites: [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy) (soft), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing)

## Learning & Autonomy

### Self-Regulated Learning

This domain covers the knowledge and strategies that let learners direct their own academic and language development: metacognitive knowledge, the self-regulation cycle, autonomy, motivation, goal setting, time management, study techniques, language learning strategies, reflective logs, support services, wellbeing and learning content through English. It is anchored in self-regulated learning research (Zimmerman, 2002; Flavell, 1979), autonomy research (Holec, 1981; Benson, 2011) and cognitive research on durable learning (Dunlosky et al., 2013). These topics are best taught through learners' real tasks and evidence (feedback, self-test results, time logs), not as detached 'study skills'.

<a id="lrnself-regulation"></a>
#### Self-regulated learning

`lrn.self-regulation` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand self-regulated learning as a cyclical process of forethought (analysing the task, setting goals, planning strategies, appraising one's self-efficacy), performance (self-monitoring and controlled strategy use) and self-reflection (evaluating outcomes against standards, attributing causes and adapting). The model gives learners and teachers a shared language for locating where independent study breaks down.

Prerequisites: [Motivation and the L2 self](#lrnmotivation) (soft), [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge)

<a id="lrngoal-setting"></a>
#### Setting language and academic goals

`lrn.goal-setting` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Set specific, near-term and checkable language and academic goals derived from actual course and assessment demands, and turn them into planned actions, including if–then plans for predictable obstacles. Well-formed goals turn a general wish to 'improve my English' into decisions about what to practise this week and how progress will be judged.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning](#lrnself-regulation), [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="lrntime-management"></a>
#### Time and workload management

`lrn.time-management` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Plan, prioritise and protect study time across concurrent deadlines: estimating task duration realistically, scheduling backwards from deadlines, allocating time to independent study as well as taught sessions, and recognising and countering procrastination. For L2 learners, realistic estimates must include the extra time that reading and writing in English may take.

Prerequisites: [Setting language and academic goals](#lrngoal-setting)

<a id="lrnindependent-study"></a>
#### Independent study

`lrn.independent-study` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand the scale and purpose of independent study in higher education systems where expected study hours far exceed contact hours (in many UK institutions one credit conventionally represents about ten notional learning hours), and plan that time productively: preparing for and consolidating taught sessions, following up reading, practising language and progressing assessments without waiting for instructions.

Prerequisites: [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques) (soft), [Learner autonomy](#lrnautonomy) (soft)

<a id="lrnstudy-techniques"></a>
#### Evidence-based study techniques

`lrn.study-techniques` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Choose and use study techniques that cognitive research rates highly for durable learning (above all retrieval practice and distributed practice, with interleaving and self-explanation as promising options) instead of relying on lower-utility habits such as rereading, highlighting and copying notes. The techniques apply to language items (vocabulary, formulaic phrases, citation conventions) as well as disciplinary content.

Prerequisites: [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge)

<a id="lrnautonomy"></a>
#### Learner autonomy

`lrn.autonomy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand learner autonomy as a developing capacity to take control over one's learning (choosing objectives, content and methods, and monitoring and evaluating progress) that grows through supported practice, rather than as studying alone or without a teacher. Autonomy underpins the independent study, strategy choice and continued development that universities expect but rarely teach explicitly.

Prerequisites: [Metacognitive knowledge](#lrnmetacognitive-knowledge) (soft)

<a id="lrnlanguage-learning-strategies"></a>
#### Language learning strategies

`lrn.language-learning-strategies` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Select, combine and evaluate strategies for continuing to develop academic English beyond the course: for example, noticing and recording useful language from disciplinary reading, deliberate vocabulary study, seeking opportunities for spoken interaction and feedback, using the L1 as a resource, and managing affect. Strategies are chosen to fit the task and goal rather than out of habit.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning](#lrnself-regulation), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques) (soft), [Learner autonomy](#lrnautonomy) (soft)

<a id="lrnreflective-logs"></a>
#### Reflective learning logs

`lrn.reflective-logs` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Keep a structured learning log or journal that records goals, actions taken, evidence of progress and next steps, and use it to make decisions. The key distinction is between analytical reflection, which explains and leads to action, and description of activities or feelings alone.

Prerequisites: [Setting language and academic goals](#lrngoal-setting), [Learner autonomy](#lrnautonomy) (soft)

<a id="lrnsupport-services"></a>
#### Using academic support services

`lrn.support-services` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify the academic and personal support a university offers (library and subject librarians, writing or academic skills centres, in-sessional EAP, maths and statistics support, disability, wellbeing and advice services) and use it early, with a prepared and specific request, as a normal part of good study practice rather than a last resort.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [How universities are organised](#acuuniversity-structures) (soft)

<a id="lrnwellbeing"></a>
#### Sustainable study habits and wellbeing

`lrn.wellbeing` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Sustain study across a term or degree by anticipating workload peaks, protecting sleep, breaks and social connection, recognising early signs that stress is affecting learning, and knowing when and how to seek help. Wellbeing is treated here as a condition for learning, not as a clinical intervention.

Prerequisites: [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft), [Using academic support services](#lrnsupport-services) (soft)

<a id="lrnmotivation"></a>
#### Motivation and the L2 self

`lrn.motivation` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand motivation for continued language and academic development through Dörnyei's L2 Motivational Self System (the ideal L2 self, the ought-to L2 self and the learning experience), and use a specific, plausible image of oneself as a competent academic user of English, linked to concrete current actions, to sustain effort beyond an entry requirement or the end of an EAP course.

<a id="lrnmetacognitive-knowledge"></a>
#### Metacognitive knowledge

`lrn.metacognitive-knowledge` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Knowledge of oneself as a learner and user of English, of what particular academic tasks demand, and of which strategies suit which tasks and conditions (Flavell's person, task and strategy variables). Accurate metacognitive knowledge allows learners to plan, monitor and adjust their learning rather than relying on habit or on how familiar material feels.

<a id="lrncontent-through-english"></a>
#### Learning subject content through English

`lrn.content-through-english` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand that in foundation, English-medium and degree contexts, disciplinary concepts and the English used to express them are learned together: technical terms, characteristic text types and ways of explaining are part of the content. Learners use this understanding to plan for the double load, for example by previewing terminology, using L1 resources strategically and checking conceptual understanding as well as language.

Prerequisites: [Lexical coverage and vocabulary size](#voclexical-coverage) (soft), [Background knowledge and schemata](#rdpbackground-knowledge) (soft), [Language learning strategies](#lrnlanguage-learning-strategies) (soft)

### Feedback & Assessment Literacy

This domain covers how learners come to understand university assessment and use feedback: assessment purposes and rules, grading systems, marking criteria, feedback literacy, acting on feedback, using written corrective feedback, seeking feedback and self- and peer-assessment. It draws on Sadler's (1989) argument that learners must come to share the assessor's concept of quality, on feedback as a support for self-regulation (Nicol & Macfarlane-Dick, 2006; Hattie & Timperley, 2007) and on student feedback literacy (Carless & Boud, 2018). On written corrective feedback, the contested evidence (Truscott, 1996; Ferris, 1999; Bitchener & Ferris, 2012; Kang & Han, 2015) should be presented honestly.

<a id="fbkassessment-literacy"></a>
#### University assessment literacy

`fbk.assessment-literacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand how university assessment works: the difference between formative and summative assessment; how learning outcomes, assessment briefs and marking criteria relate; the range of assessment types; and the rules on weighting, deadlines, word limits, extensions, mitigating circumstances and resits. This knowledge lets learners focus effort where it counts and recognise formative opportunities that carry no marks.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft), [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="fbkmarking-criteria"></a>
#### Interpreting marking criteria

`fbk.marking-criteria` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Unpack the marking criteria, rubrics and grade descriptors for a specific task: identify what each criterion values, how adjacent bands differ, and what evaluative language such as 'critical', 'well-structured' or 'appropriate use of sources' means in observable textual terms, using exemplars to make tacit standards concrete.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy), [Grading systems](#fbkgrading-systems) (soft)

<a id="fbkgrading-systems"></a>
#### Grading systems

`fbk.grading-systems` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand how marks are expressed and interpreted in the learner's institution (for example, UK percentage bands and undergraduate degree classifications, master's pass, merit and distinction thresholds, or GPA-style systems) and what grade bands signal about the quality of work, so that marks are neither over- nor under-interpreted against expectations carried over from other systems.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft)

<a id="fbkfeedback-literacy"></a>
#### Feedback literacy

`fbk.feedback-literacy` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand feedback as information that learners must actively make sense of and use, not something simply delivered to them. This involves appreciating its purpose, developing the capacity to make evaluative judgements, managing emotional responses and taking action (Carless and Boud's four features). Feedback is recognised in many forms: written comments, rubric marks, peer comments, discussion in class, tutorials and automated tools.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="fbkacting-on-feedback"></a>
#### Acting on feedback

`fbk.acting-on-feedback` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Turn feedback into a feed-forward action plan: categorise comments, identify issues that recur across assignments, decide which apply to the next (often different) task, act on them and check whether the change worked.

Prerequisites: [Setting language and academic goals](#lrngoal-setting) (soft), [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria) (soft), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy)

<a id="fbkcorrective-feedback"></a>
#### Using written corrective feedback

`fbk.corrective-feedback` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use written corrective feedback on language (direct corrections, correction codes, underlining or margin indications) to understand the nature of errors, self-correct where feedback is indirect, and track recurring error patterns, recognising that one-off corrections rarely carry over to new writing without follow-up.

Prerequisites: [Clause elements and sentence analysis](#grmclause-elements) (soft), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft)

<a id="fbkseeking-feedback"></a>
#### Seeking feedback

`fbk.seeking-feedback` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Request feedback that is specific, timely and usable (on a draft, plan or idea) from tutors, supervisors and peers, framing the request politely and within institutional norms, such as what tutors may comment on before submission, and following up to clarify comments.

Prerequisites: [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy), [Making requests](#prgrequests) (soft)

<a id="fbkself-peer-assessment"></a>
#### Self- and peer-assessment

`fbk.self-peer-assessment` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Judge the quality of one's own and peers' work against criteria and exemplars, give feedback that is specific and criterion-referenced, and calibrate these judgements over time by comparing them with tutors' judgements. The aim is evaluative judgement, not self-grading.

Prerequisites: [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft)

### Exams & Timed Performance

This domain covers university examinations and timed performance: understanding exam formats, analysing exam questions, planning revision, writing under time pressure, approaching objective-format questions, open-book and take-home exams, oral examinations and managing test anxiety. It draws on cognitive research on retrieval and spacing (Dunlosky et al., 2013; Roediger & Karpicke, 2006), research on planning under time pressure (Ellis & Yuan, 2004), writing assessment (Weigle, 2002) and test anxiety (Putwain, 2008; Horwitz et al., 1986). Emphasis is on matching preparation and in-exam behaviour to the demands of each format rather than on generic exam tips.

<a id="exmexam-formats"></a>
#### University exam formats

`exm.exam-formats` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand the main formats of university examination (unseen and seen written papers, open-book and take-home exams, multiple-choice and short-answer tests, problem-based papers, oral and practical examinations such as OSCEs in health programmes, and computer-based or online variants) and what kind of knowledge and performance each rewards, so that preparation and in-exam behaviour match the format.

Prerequisites: [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft)

<a id="exmquestion-analysis"></a>
#### Analysing exam questions

`exm.question-analysis` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Analyse an exam question quickly and accurately, identifying the command word or words, topic, focus and limiting words, implicit sub-questions and the expected answer type, and allocate time across questions in proportion to marks.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words), [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="exmrevision-planning"></a>
#### Planning revision

`exm.revision-planning` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Plan revision as a spaced, active schedule that begins weeks before the exam period: mapping the syllabus and past-paper demands, distributing sessions across topics, building sessions around retrieval and timed practice, and adjusting the plan according to self-test results.

Prerequisites: [Reviewing and reworking notes](#ntkreviewing-notes) (soft), [Time and workload management](#lrntime-management) (soft), [Evidence-based study techniques](#lrnstudy-techniques), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="exmtimed-writing"></a>
#### Timed writing

`exm.timed-writing` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Produce coherent, relevant written answers under time pressure: planning rapidly (a brief outline and a position), allocating time between planning, writing and checking, writing fluently without sources to hand, and checking for one's own most frequent errors. Applies to exam essays, short-answer papers and timed tasks in language tests and progression assessments.

Prerequisites: [Planning and outlining](#wprplanning-outlining), [Drafting](#wprdrafting) (soft), [Analysing exam questions](#exmquestion-analysis)

<a id="exmobjective-questions"></a>
#### Objective question strategies

`exm.objective-questions` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Approach multiple-choice, true/false, matching and short-item questions strategically: reading stems precisely (including negatives and qualifiers), predicting an answer before reading the options, eliminating distractors for stated reasons, managing time and flagged items, and applying the paper's rules on guessing and negative marking.

Prerequisites: [Generalising and qualifying](#stageneralisation-qualification) (soft), [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="exmopen-book"></a>
#### Open-book and take-home exams

`exm.open-book` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Prepare for and complete open-book and take-home exams, recognising that they assess application, analysis and synthesis rather than recall: organising and indexing permitted materials in advance, managing extended time windows, and observing paper-specific rules on collaboration, sources, citation and AI use.

Prerequisites: [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Collusion and legitimate collaboration](#intcollusion) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats)

<a id="exmoral-exams"></a>
#### Oral examinations

`exm.oral-exams` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Prepare for and perform in oral assessments such as questioning on submitted work, individual oral examinations, interactive orals and the speaking components of language assessments: anticipating question types, structuring spoken answers, asking for clarification or repetition, gaining thinking time appropriately and recovering from breakdowns.

Prerequisites: [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

<a id="exmtest-anxiety"></a>
#### Managing test anxiety

`exm.test-anxiety` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise how anxiety in evaluative situations (written exams, timed tests, oral assessments and high-stakes proficiency tests) can interfere with performance, particularly through worry that takes up attention, and use preparation, reappraisal and in-exam techniques to reduce its impact, while knowing when anxiety calls for professional support or reasonable adjustments.

Prerequisites: [Sustainable study habits and wellbeing](#lrnwellbeing) (soft), [University exam formats](#exmexam-formats) (soft)

### Proficiency Tests & Progression

This domain helps learners understand the language assessments that control access to and progression through university study: the purposes of different assessments, CEFR levels and entry requirements, the formats and constructs of academic proficiency tests, the differences between test tasks and university tasks, strategies for integrated test tasks, and pre-sessional progression assessment. It draws on language assessment theory (Bachman & Palmer, 2010; Weigle, 2002), the CEFR (Council of Europe, 2001, 2020; North, 2014) and research on test washback and on the gap between test and university writing (Green, 2007; Moore & Morton, 2005). Test formats, score requirements and visa rules change, so content is framed at the level of task types and constructs, and learners are directed to current official sources.

<a id="tstassessment-purposes"></a>
#### Proficiency, achievement and progression assessment

`tst.assessment-purposes` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Distinguish the purposes of different language assessments (proficiency, placement, diagnostic, achievement, and progression or gatekeeping decisions) and understand that a score is evidence supporting a particular interpretation and decision, not a fixed property of the learner. This helps learners interpret their results realistically and use each assessment for its intended purpose.

<a id="tstcefr-entry"></a>
#### CEFR levels and entry requirements

`tst.cefr-entry` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Understand what CEFR levels describe (communicative ability in can-do terms across activities, including 'plus' levels and uneven profiles across skills), how university and visa entry requirements are expressed through test scores and levels, and why the relationship between a test score and a CEFR level rests on test owners' published alignment claims, which are approximate and debated. Requirements are set by institutions and government bodies and change over time.

Prerequisites: [Proficiency, achievement and progression assessment](#tstassessment-purposes)

<a id="tsttest-formats"></a>
#### Academic proficiency test formats

`tst.test-formats` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Understand the task types and constructs of the major academic English proficiency tests used for university entry (for example, reading with selected-response items, lecture-style listening, independent and integrated writing, and interview-based or computer-delivered speaking), including differences in delivery mode and in human, automated or hybrid scoring, so that preparation targets what each test actually measures.

Prerequisites: [Proficiency, achievement and progression assessment](#tstassessment-purposes) (soft)

<a id="tsttest-vs-university"></a>
#### Test tasks versus university tasks

`tst.test-vs-university` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise how proficiency-test tasks differ from university tasks. Test writing is typically short, timed, drawn from personal knowledge or brief inputs, on general topics and scored against generic criteria; university writing is typically extended, source-based, discipline-specific, varied in genre and judged against disciplinary expectations. Learners use this understanding to avoid carrying test templates into university work.

Prerequisites: [Functions of sources in academic writing](#srcsource-functions) (soft), [Genre families in university assessment](#gesgenre-families) (soft), [Academic proficiency test formats](#tsttest-formats)

<a id="tstintegrated-test-tasks"></a>
#### Integrated test tasks

`tst.integrated-test-tasks` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Handle integrated test tasks that require reading and/or listening before writing or speaking: noting key points from the input efficiently, identifying the relationship between sources (for example, a talk that challenges a reading), selecting and transforming content in one's own words, and organising the response around the task's specific demand within the time limit.

Prerequisites: [Integrated source-based tasks](#synintegrated-tasks), [Timed writing](#exmtimed-writing) (soft), [Academic proficiency test formats](#tsttest-formats)

<a id="tstpresessional-progression"></a>
#### Pre-sessional progression assessment

`tst.presessional-progression` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional · EGAP

Understand how pre-sessional and similar pathway programmes assess progression, typically through coursework such as an extended source-based essay or report, a small research project, presentations or seminars, portfolios and sometimes timed tasks, and how criteria, weightings, integrity rules and progression thresholds work, so that learners can plan effort across components and use formative stages.

Prerequisites: [Interpreting marking criteria](#fbkmarking-criteria), [CEFR levels and entry requirements](#tstcefr-entry) (soft), [Test tasks versus university tasks](#tsttest-vs-university) (soft)

## Academic Socialisation & Communication

### Academic Culture & Expectations

Makes explicit how universities and disciplines are organised, what they expect of learners and how those expectations are communicated, often tacitly. The domain moves from orientation knowledge (structures, teaching formats, roles and address) through discourse-community and disciplinary awareness to perspectives (academic literacies, critical EAP, EMI and transnational education) that treat conventions as situated, variable and open to question rather than as fixed norms to be absorbed. Throughout, difficulty is framed as a mismatch between practices, not as a deficit in the learner.

<a id="acuuniversity-structures"></a>
#### How universities are organised

`acu.university-structures` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify the organisational units (faculty, school, department, programme, module), staff roles (module leader, personal tutor, programme director, supervisor, professional services staff) and decision-making processes (programme regulations, assessment boards, student representation) that affect one's study, and use this knowledge to find information and the right person to contact. Organisational knowledge underpins effective help-seeking and correspondence, and terms and structures vary considerably between institutions and countries.

<a id="acuteaching-formats"></a>
#### Teaching and learning formats

`acu.teaching-formats` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Recognise the purposes, participation expectations and preparation demands of the main formats of university teaching (lectures, seminars, tutorials, laboratory and studio sessions, workshops, and VLE-based or blended learning) and the relationship between timetabled contact and independent study. Knowing what each format is for lets learners prepare appropriately and interpret what counts as taking part.

Prerequisites: [How universities are organised](#acuuniversity-structures) (soft)

<a id="acuacademic-expectations"></a>
#### Expectations of university learners

`acu.academic-expectations` · CONCEPTUAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Interpret commonly stated expectations of university learners (independent study, critical engagement with sources, contribution to discussion, responsibility for one's own learning) as situated practices whose meaning varies by discipline, level and institution, rather than as fixed personal traits. Making these expectations concrete helps learners act on them and recognise where their prior experience already meets them.

Prerequisites: [Teaching and learning formats](#acuteaching-formats) (soft)

<a id="acudiscourse-communities"></a>
#### Discourse communities and disciplines

`acu.discourse-communities` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand disciplines and other academic groups as discourse communities with shared goals, channels of communication, characteristic genres and specialised lexis, in which membership develops through participation over time. The concept helps learners see academic conventions as purposeful practices of particular communities rather than universal rules, while recognising that communities are internally diverse and their boundaries contested.

Prerequisites: [How universities are organised](#acuuniversity-structures) (soft)

<a id="acudisciplinary-knowledge"></a>
#### Disciplinary knowledge-making

`acu.disciplinary-knowledge` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Compare how disciplines construct knowledge (what counts as evidence, how claims are warranted, the role of theory, cumulative versus interpretive knowledge-building) and relate these differences to the genres, citation practices and stance valued in one's own field. Recognising epistemological difference explains why writing advice that works in one field misleads in another.

Prerequisites: [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities)

<a id="acuhidden-curriculum"></a>
#### Tacit expectations

`acu.hidden-curriculum` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Identify the unstated conventions behind university tasks and feedback, such as what tutors mean by 'argument', 'structure', 'critical' or 'too descriptive', how criteria are applied in practice, and which behaviours are valued but not taught, and use questioning, exemplars and feedback to make them explicit. Tacit expectations disadvantage students whose prior schooling did not share them, so surfacing them is a matter of equity as well as skill.

Prerequisites: [Assignment command words](#voccommand-words) (soft), [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations)

<a id="acuacademic-literacies"></a>
#### Academic literacies perspective
