`acu.academic-literacies` · CONCEPTUAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand the academic literacies view of reading and writing as social practices shaped by identity, power and institutional context, as distinct from study-skills and academic socialisation models, and use it to interpret one's own experience of feedback, conventions and voice. The perspective reframes difficulty as arising from contested and often unstated expectations rather than from individual deficit.

Prerequisites: [Discourse communities and disciplines](#acudiscourse-communities) (soft), [Tacit expectations](#acuhidden-curriculum)

<a id="acutransition"></a>
#### Transition and adjustment

`acu.transition` · STRATEGIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Manage academic and sociocultural adjustment to a new educational context by identifying specific unfamiliar practices and sources of stress, learning the practices of the new setting, drawing on prior knowledge and multilingual repertoires, and building peer, academic and professional support. Adjustment is uneven and individual rather than a fixed sequence of stages, and it involves the institution adapting as well as the student.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft)

<a id="acuroles-address"></a>
#### Academic roles and forms of address

`acu.roles-address` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose appropriate forms of address and reference for academic and professional staff (titles such as Dr and Professor, first names, or no title) by checking local practice, the person's own signature or introduction, and the institutional relationship. Norms vary across institutions, countries, disciplines and individuals, and misjudged address in either direction can affect how a message is received.

Prerequisites: [How universities are organised](#acuuniversity-structures)

<a id="acucritical-eap"></a>
#### Critical perspectives on EAP

`acu.critical-eap` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Examine whose norms EAP teaches, whose interests they serve and how they might be questioned or negotiated, including native-speaker standards, the dominance of English in scholarship and the pragmatic aim of fitting students to existing demands, and identify where learners can exercise informed choice or seek change. A critical perspective supports agency without pretending that conventions carry no consequences.

Prerequisites: [Standards, native-speakerism and legitimacy](#idnstandards-legitimacy) (soft)

<a id="acuemi-tne"></a>
#### EMI and transnational education contexts

`acu.emi-tne` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Understand the conditions of studying through English in non-anglophone universities (English-medium instruction) and in transnational provision such as branch campuses and franchised or joint programmes, including the roles of English and other languages, content lecturers' varied expectations about language, and what this implies for the academic English that matters locally. EMI and TNE learners need academic English shaped by their actual context rather than by anglophone-campus assumptions.

Prerequisites: [English as a lingua franca in academia](#idnelf) (soft)

### Academic Pragmatics & Correspondence

Covers the sociopragmatic and pragmalinguistic resources learners need to interact with staff and peers outside the core academic genres: politeness and face, requests, apologies and accounts, emails, office hours, formal institutional statements, forum posts, channel choice and acknowledgements. Interlanguage pragmatics research shows that pragmatic competence does not keep pace with grammatical development without attention, and that staff judge messages on perceived politeness and clarity as well as accuracy. Norms are presented as variable across institutions, countries and individuals; the aim is informed, context-sensitive choice, not one correct formula.

<a id="prgpoliteness"></a>
#### Politeness and face in academic interaction

`prg.politeness` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Analyse student–staff and peer interaction in terms of face, social distance, relative power and the size of an imposition, and choose linguistic strategies (mitigation, indirectness, acknowledging the other's time and autonomy) accordingly. Politeness norms depend on context and vary across institutions and individuals, so the aim is informed choice rather than a single formula.

Prerequisites: [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft), [Academic roles and forms of address](#acuroles-address) (soft)

<a id="prgemails-to-staff"></a>
#### Emails to academic staff

`prg.emails-to-staff` · GENRE · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write emails to lecturers, supervisors and professional services staff with an informative subject line, an appropriate greeting and form of address, an early statement of purpose, sufficient context, a clear request or question, and a suitable closing and signature with identifying details. Email is often the first and most frequent written contact with staff, and its form shapes perceptions of the writer.

Prerequisites: [Features of formal academic style](#regformality-features) (soft), [Academic roles and forms of address](#acuroles-address), [Making requests](#prgrequests)

<a id="prgrequests"></a>
#### Making requests

`prg.requests` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Make academic requests (for extensions, meetings, references, clarification, feedback or resources) in writing and speech that state clearly what is wanted, give a relevant reason, and are mitigated in proportion to the imposition. Clarity of purpose and appropriate mitigation together determine whether a request is understood and granted, and some requests must go through formal procedures rather than personal appeal.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft), [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="prgoffice-hours"></a>
#### Office hours and tutorials

`prg.office-hours` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Prepare for and conduct one-to-one or small-group meetings with academic staff: booking through the expected channel, bringing specific questions and materials, framing the purpose at the start, negotiating and checking understanding, noting agreed actions and closing within the time available. These meetings are a key but under-used route to tacit expectations and individual feedback.

Prerequisites: [Asking questions and seeking clarification](#semquestions-clarification) (soft)

<a id="prgformal-statements"></a>
#### Formal statements and appeals

`prg.formal-statements` · GENRE · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write formal institutional texts (extenuating or mitigating circumstances statements, academic appeals and complaints) that follow the relevant procedure, give a factual, chronological and evidenced account, relate it to the grounds the regulations allow, state the outcome sought and keep a measured, non-accusatory tone. These texts affect progression and are judged against regulations, so procedural fit matters more than persuasion or emotional appeal.

Prerequisites: [Chronological narration](#funchronology-narration) (soft), [Making requests](#prgrequests), [Apologising and explaining](#prgapologising-explaining) (soft)

<a id="prgapologising-explaining"></a>
#### Apologising and explaining

`prg.apologising-explaining` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Apologise and give accounts for missed deadlines, absences, lateness or other lapses in a way that acknowledges responsibility proportionately, explains briefly and relevantly, proposes repair, and avoids both under-apology and excessive self-abasement or over-disclosure. Effective apologies maintain working relationships and often connect the student to the formal process they actually need.

Prerequisites: [Expectations of university learners](#acuacademic-expectations) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="prgforum-posts"></a>
#### Online discussion forum posts

`prg.forum-posts` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Write asynchronous discussion posts and replies on VLE forums that respond to the prompt, make a clear point supported by course readings or evidence, engage explicitly with others' contributions, and use a register between conversation and formal academic prose. Forum posts are often assessed and make reasoning visible to peers, so they reward both argument and collegial engagement.

Prerequisites: [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing) (soft), [Building on others' contributions](#sembuilding-on-others) (soft)

<a id="prgmessaging-channels"></a>
#### Messaging channels and norms

`prg.messaging-channels` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Choose the appropriate channel (email, VLE messages or forums, enquiry and ticketing systems, group chats, collaboration platforms) for a given purpose, recipient, level of privacy and urgency, and adjust tone, length and expectations of response time accordingly. Channel norms differ across institutions and teams and are often unstated; a misjudged channel can delay a response or blur professional boundaries.

Prerequisites: [Emails to academic staff](#prgemails-to-staff), [Virtual learning environments](#digvle) (soft)

<a id="prgacknowledgements"></a>
#### Acknowledgements

`prg.acknowledgements` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write acknowledgements for dissertations, theses and articles that thank supervisors, participants, funders, institutions and others in a conventional order and register, include required funding and contribution statements, and distinguish acknowledgement from authorship. The genre is short but socially significant, combining personal gratitude with institutional and ethical obligations.

Prerequisites: [Undergraduate and master's dissertation](#grpdissertation-overview) (soft), [Authorship and contribution](#pubauthorship) (soft)

## Digital Literacy & AI

### Digital Scholarship Tools

Academic work is mediated by digital tools (learning platforms, word processors, reference managers, shared documents, machine translation and automated writing evaluation) whose effective and ethical use is rarely taught explicitly. This domain treats these tools as part of academic literacy practices rather than as separate technical skills (Lea & Jones, 2011), emphasising critical and transparent use: tools support but do not replace learners’ judgement, their outputs must be checked, and their permitted use is set by institutional policy. Because products change quickly, topics are framed around transferable functions and decisions rather than named applications.

<a id="digvle"></a>
#### Virtual learning environments

`dig.vle` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Navigate the institution’s virtual learning environment and related systems to find module information, assessment briefs, readings, recordings, announcements and feedback; submit work correctly through submission portals; and keep track of deadlines and notifications. Missed instructions, unread feedback and failed submissions are avoidable sources of difficulty, especially in a first term.

Prerequisites: [Vocabulary of university study](#vocuniversity-terminology) (soft)

<a id="digreference-managers"></a>
#### Reference management software

`dig.reference-managers` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use reference management software to collect, organise and annotate sources, insert in-text citations and generate reference lists in a required style, and check the output for errors. Reference managers save time and reduce inconsistency, but they reproduce whatever metadata they are given, so users still need to know what a correct reference looks like.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing) (soft)

<a id="digword-processing"></a>
#### Academic word processing

`dig.word-processing` · PROCEDURAL · B1–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use the word-processing functions that support academic documents: paragraph and heading styles, automatic numbering, generated contents pages, captions and cross-references, section breaks, headers, footers and page numbers, templates, and comments. Structure-based formatting saves time in long documents, keeps formatting consistent and makes documents accessible and easier to revise.

Prerequisites: [Report conventions](#grpreport-conventions) (soft)

<a id="digmachine-translation"></a>
#### Machine translation

`dig.machine-translation` · STRATEGIC · B1–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use machine translation (MT) strategically and transparently: to support comprehension of difficult texts, to check understanding, to explore wording and, where permitted, to support drafting, while recognising MT’s characteristic errors (terminology, register, lost hedging, ambiguity), verifying its output and complying with assessment rules. MT is a legitimate plurilingual resource, but undeclared or wholesale use in assessed writing can breach integrity policies and bypass language development.

Prerequisites: [Using learner and specialist dictionaries](#vocdictionary-use) (soft), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft), [Drawing on a plurilingual repertoire](#idnplurilingual-repertoire) (soft)

<a id="digdigital-collaboration"></a>
#### Digital collaboration tools

`dig.digital-collaboration` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use shared documents, comments, suggestion and track-changes modes, version history and cloud workspaces to co-author and review academic work: setting permissions, giving and responding to comments constructively and concisely, resolving suggestions, and keeping a clear record of contributions. Digital collaboration is now the default medium for group projects and for much supervisor feedback.

Prerequisites: [Roles and planning](#grwroles-planning) (soft), [Academic word processing](#digword-processing) (soft), [File and version management](#digfile-management) (soft)

<a id="digfile-management"></a>
#### File and version management

`dig.file-management` · PROCEDURAL · B1–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Organise, name, version and back up academic files systematically: consistent folder structures and file names, clear distinction between working and submitted versions, local and off-device backups, and secure storage of sensitive material. Good file management prevents lost work and confusion between drafts and, for research students, is the basis of research data management.

<a id="digdigital-etiquette"></a>
#### Digital etiquette and online presence

`dig.digital-etiquette` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Communicate appropriately in digital academic spaces (forums, group chats, video calls, social media and professional networks), adapting tone, formality and response expectations to channel and audience, and manage an academic online presence and privacy (profiles, researcher identifiers, what to share publicly). Digital interactions persist and are searchable, so pragmatic misjudgements can have lasting effects.

Prerequisites: [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="digautomated-feedback-tools"></a>
#### Automated writing evaluation tools

`dig.automated-feedback-tools` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · EGAP

Use automated writing evaluation (AWE) tools, including grammar and style checkers, automated feedback platforms and AI-based feedback features, as one input among others: interpret their suggestions critically, accept or reject them on principled grounds, recognise what they cannot judge (argument, content, disciplinary appropriateness) and comply with rules on permitted use. Uncritical acceptance can introduce errors, flatten voice and, where rewriting features are used, breach assessment rules.

Prerequisites: [Monitoring recurrent grammatical errors](#grmerror-monitoring) (soft)

### Generative AI Literacy

Generative AI literacy is treated here as part of academic literacy: understanding how large language models generate text and why they fail; reading and applying AI policies that vary by institution, module and task; acknowledging and verifying AI use; and using AI in ways that build rather than replace learners' own language, reasoning and voice. The domain also covers the limits of AI-text detection, including documented bias against writers using English as an additional language, and privacy and intellectual-property risks. Tools and policies change quickly, so topics are framed around durable principles and verification habits rather than particular products.

<a id="gaihow-genai-works"></a>
#### How generative AI works and fails

`gai.how-genai-works` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Explain in non-technical terms how large language models generate text — by predicting likely continuations from patterns learned from very large training datasets, with further tuning to follow instructions — and why this produces fluent but unverified output, fabricated facts and references, biases inherited from training data, and gaps beyond the training data. This understanding underpins every judgement about when AI output can be trusted and how it must be checked.

<a id="gaipolicies"></a>
#### Interpreting AI policies

`gai.policies` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–B2+ · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Locate and interpret the rules on generative AI that apply to a specific task — institutional policy, programme and module guidance, the assessment brief and, for researchers, publisher and funder policies — including which kinds of use are permitted and what must be declared. Policies differ between and within institutions and change over time, so learners need a routine for checking each task and for asking when the rules are unclear.

Prerequisites: [Analysing assignment tasks](#wprtask-analysis) (soft), [Values of academic integrity](#intintegrity-values) (soft), [University assessment literacy](#fbkassessment-literacy) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works) (soft)

<a id="gaiacknowledging-use"></a>
#### Acknowledging and citing AI use

`gai.acknowledging-use` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Declare and, where required, cite generative AI use in the form the institution, assessment or publisher specifies — typically stating which tools were used, for what purpose and at which stage, and keeping prompts and outputs as records — and distinguish acknowledging assistance from citing AI output as content. Transparent records also allow learners to demonstrate their own process if their work is questioned.

Prerequisites: [Citing diverse source types](#citdiverse-sources) (soft), [Interpreting AI policies](#gaipolicies)

<a id="gaiverifying-output"></a>
#### Verifying AI output

`gai.verifying-output` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Check AI output before relying on it: verify factual claims against authoritative sources, confirm that every reference exists and says what is attributed to it, check quotations and figures against the originals, and look for omissions and bias. Fabricated and erroneous citations are a documented failure of language models, so verification is a non-negotiable step whenever AI output informs academic work.

Prerequisites: [Library catalogues, databases and discovery tools](#infdatabases) (soft), [Lateral reading](#inflateral-reading) (soft), [Compiling a reference list](#citreference-list) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works)

<a id="gailanguage-support"></a>
#### Using AI as a language-learning resource

`gai.language-support` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Use generative AI to support language development — explanations of grammar and vocabulary, feedback that identifies rather than silently fixes errors, practice dialogues and exercises, and comparison of alternative phrasings — in ways that keep the learner doing the cognitive work and stay within assessment rules. The aim is durable competence, so learners evaluate AI feedback critically and check whether their unassisted writing and speaking improve.

Prerequisites: [Using written corrective feedback](#fbkcorrective-feedback) (soft), [Prompting for learning](#gaiprompting-for-learning)

<a id="gaiauthorship-voice"></a>
#### AI, authorship and voice

`gai.authorship-voice` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Consider who is the author of a text produced with AI assistance — whose ideas, reasoning and words it contains, and who is accountable for them — and maintain one's own authorial voice and stance when using AI. AI-polished text can become generic and can misrepresent a writer's actual reasoning and competence, so learners decide where assistance must stop for a task to remain their own work.

Prerequisites: [Authorial voice](#staauthorial-voice), [Understanding plagiarism](#intplagiarism) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works) (soft)

<a id="gaidetection-limits"></a>
#### Limits of AI detection

`gai.detection-limits` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Understand that AI-text detectors estimate the likelihood that text was machine-generated, produce both false positives and false negatives, can be evaded, and have been shown to misclassify writing by non-native English writers as AI-generated at high rates. Detector output therefore does not establish misconduct; learners protect themselves by keeping evidence of their writing process and knowing how integrity processes treat such indicators.

Prerequisites: [Interpreting similarity reports](#intsimilarity-reports) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works)

<a id="gaiprompting-for-learning"></a>
#### Prompting for learning

`gai.prompting-for-learning` · STRATEGIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Write prompts that make AI act as a tutor or sparring partner — explanations at a chosen level, Socratic questions, worked examples followed by practice, counter-arguments, or feedback against criteria — rather than producing finished text, and judge whether the interaction produced learning. Effective prompting includes giving context and constraints, iterating, and stopping to work without the tool.

Prerequisites: [Self-regulated learning](#lrnself-regulation) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works), [Interpreting AI policies](#gaipolicies) (soft)

<a id="gairesearch-tools"></a>
#### AI-assisted research tools

`gai.research-tools` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Evaluate and use AI-based literature discovery, question-answering and summarisation tools, understanding their limits in coverage, ranking, reproducibility and accuracy. These tools can speed up orientation to a field, but they cannot replace documented database searching, reading the sources themselves, or the researcher's own judgement of relevance and quality.

Prerequisites: [Mapping a body of literature](#rdcliterature-mapping) (soft), [Summarising a whole text](#srcsummarising-text) (soft), [Verifying AI output](#gaiverifying-output)

<a id="gaiprivacy-ip"></a>
#### Privacy, data and intellectual property with AI

`gai.privacy-ip` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · EGAP

Judge what may and may not be entered into AI tools — personal and research-participant data, confidential or unpublished material, others' copyrighted work, and one's own unpublished work — taking account of the tool's terms on data retention and training, institutional data-protection rules and research ethics approvals. Learners also recognise that copyright in AI-generated content and in training data remains legally unsettled and varies by jurisdiction.

Prerequisites: [Open access and legal access](#infopen-access) (soft), [Research integrity](#intresearch-integrity) (soft), [How generative AI works and fails](#gaihow-genai-works)

## Disciplinary Literacies

### Science, Engineering & Computing

This domain covers the discourse of the natural sciences, engineering, mathematics and computing: how technicality, nominalisation and taxonomies build scientific knowledge, and how learners produce explanations, protocols and specifications, experimental language, proofs and software documentation. These fields are typically 'hard' in Becher and Trowler's terms, with cumulative knowledge, high consensus on method and texts in which visuals, notation and code carry much of the meaning. Teaching works best when EAP staff analyse departmental exemplars with subject lecturers, because conventions differ between, for example, a chemistry protocol, a civil engineering specification and a computing README.

<a id="dsescientific-language"></a>
#### The language of science

`dse.scientific-language` · CONCEPTUAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Recognise how scientific English builds knowledge through technical terms organised into taxonomies, chains of interlocking definitions, nominalisation (grammatical metaphor) and high lexical density, and explain what each does for the scientific reader. Understanding these features as functional, not as obscurity, lets learners unpack dense textbook and research prose and begin to produce it.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Spoken versus written academic language](#regspoken-vs-written) (soft), [Nominalisation and grammatical metaphor](#grmnominalisation), [Classifying and categorising](#funclassifying) (soft)

<a id="dsescientific-explanation"></a>
#### Scientific explanations

`dse.scientific-explanation` · GENRE · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug · ESAP

Write and present explanations of how or why phenomena occur, choosing among sequential explanations (a chain of events in time), causal and factorial explanations (multiple causes), consequential explanations (multiple effects) and theoretical explanations that apply a model or law to a case. Explanation is one of the most frequently assessed genres in undergraduate science and engineering and underlies exam answers, lab-report discussions and textbook learning.

Prerequisites: [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Describing processes, diagrams and maps](#datdescribing-diagrams) (soft), [The language of science](#dsescientific-language)

<a id="dseprotocols-specifications"></a>
#### Protocols and specifications

`dse.protocols-specifications` · GENRE · B2–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Read and write procedural and specification texts used in laboratories and engineering: experimental protocols and standard operating procedures, method statements, and requirements or design specifications. This involves numbered imperative steps with exact quantities, conditions and tolerances, safety notes placed before the hazardous step, and requirement statements that use modal verbs consistently (often shall or must for mandatory, should for recommended, may for optional) so that each statement can be verified.

Prerequisites: [Lists and bullet points](#senlists-bullets) (soft), [Describing processes and procedures](#fundescribing-process), [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure) (soft), [Units and measurement](#numunits-measurement)

<a id="dsehypothesis-language"></a>
#### Language of experimentation

`dse.hypothesis-language` · LINGUISTIC · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Use the language of experimental design and measurement precisely: testable hypotheses and predictions, independent, dependent and controlled variables, control conditions, random and systematic error, accuracy versus precision, and statements of uncertainty (for example with ± and appropriate significant figures). Many of these words have everyday meanings (error, control, significant, precise) that differ from their technical sense, so misuse signals misunderstanding of method as well as of language.

Prerequisites: [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy), [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Predicting and hypothesising](#funhypothesising), [Basic statistical vocabulary](#numstatistical-vocabulary) (soft)

<a id="dsesoftware-documentation"></a>
#### Software and technical documentation

`dse.software-documentation` · GENRE · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write the main documentation genres of computing for their distinct readers: README files that tell a newcomer what a project does and how to install, run and contribute to it; code comments and docstrings that explain intent and non-obvious decisions rather than restating the code; API reference entries that specify parameters, return values, errors and examples; and design documents that set out context, goals and non-goals, alternatives considered and trade-offs. Documentation is assessed in computing projects and is a core professional literacy.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining) (soft), [Protocols and specifications](#dseprotocols-specifications)

<a id="dsemathematical-writing"></a>
#### Mathematical writing and proofs

`dse.mathematical-writing` · GENRE · B2–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write proofs and worked solutions in which notation is embedded in grammatical, punctuated sentences and the logical steps are signalled in words: introducing objects (let, suppose, define), stating quantifiers (for all, there exists), marking inference (hence, it follows that, since), naming the proof strategy (by contradiction, by induction, by the contrapositive) and closing the argument. Markers in mathematics expect the reasoning, not only the answer, to be readable, and students used to answer-only assessment often lose marks for unexplained steps.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals), [Precise logical relations](#cohconnector-precision) (soft), [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types), [Language of mathematical expression](#nummathematical-expression)

### Health & Life Sciences

This domain covers the literacies of nursing, medicine, allied health, pharmacy and related life-science programmes, where academic writing is tied to professional practice and patient safety. Its topics are evidence-based practice as an organising concept, reflective practice writing, case presentations and care plans, communication on placement, and critical appraisal. Many of these genres are assessed against professional standards as well as academic criteria, so EAP teachers should work with clinical educators, use anonymised local exemplars and check regulator and programme requirements on confidentiality and record-keeping.

<a id="dhlevidence-based-practice"></a>
#### Evidence-based practice

`dhl.evidence-based-practice` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Understand evidence-based practice as the integration of the best available research evidence with clinical expertise and the values and circumstances of the patient, not as following research alone. In academic work this means framing focused clinical questions (for example in PICO form), recognising evidence hierarchies and their limits, and writing assignments in which recommendations for practice are justified by appraised evidence, professional judgement and patient perspective.

Prerequisites: [Focusing a topic for searching](#infresearch-question) (soft), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types), [Interpreting statistical claims](#numinterpreting-statistics) (soft)

<a id="dhlreflective-practice"></a>
#### Reflective practice writing in health

`dhl.reflective-practice` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write structured reflections on placement or practice experiences using a model the programme specifies (for example Gibbs's reflective cycle or a What? So what? Now what? model), moving from a concise, anonymised account of an event to analysis that draws on literature and professional standards, and ending with a concrete plan for future practice. Reflective writing is assessed throughout health programmes and in professional revalidation, and is judged on depth of analysis and professional judgement, not on confession.

Prerequisites: [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing), [Evidence-based practice](#dhlevidence-based-practice) (soft)

<a id="dhlcase-reports-care-plans"></a>
#### Case reports and care plans

`dhl.case-reports-care-plans` · GENRE · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Produce the case-based genres of health education: oral and written case presentations that order patient information conventionally (presenting complaint, history, examination, investigations, assessment, plan), and care plans that link assessed needs to goals, interventions with rationales, and evaluation. These genres select and order information for clinical decision-making, use standard abbreviations cautiously and must respect confidentiality.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Chronological narration](#funchronology-narration), [Case study analysis](#grpcase-study) (soft), [Evidence-based practice](#dhlevidence-based-practice) (soft)

<a id="dhlprofessional-communication"></a>
#### Professional communication in health placements

`dhl.professional-communication` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Communicate safely and appropriately with patients, families and colleagues on placement: structuring consultations, asking open and closed questions, explaining in lay terms and checking understanding, responding to emotion, seeking consent, and giving concise structured handovers or escalations to colleagues. On placement, language is part of clinical safety and is assessed against professional standards; it also involves understanding colloquial and regional ways patients describe symptoms.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences), [Understanding varied accents](#lisaccent-variation) (soft), [Checking and confirming understanding](#semchecking-understanding) (soft), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness)

<a id="dhlcritical-appraisal"></a>
#### Critical appraisal and systematic review writing

`dhl.critical-appraisal` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Appraise health research with a structured tool suited to the study design (for example a CASP checklist) and write up the appraisal as a reasoned judgement about validity, results and applicability. At more advanced levels, report systematic or structured reviews transparently, following reporting guidance such as PRISMA 2020 for search, selection, risk-of-bias assessment and synthesis. Appraisal assignments are common in health programmes and are marked on judgement, not on checklist completion.

Prerequisites: [Systematic and scoping searching](#infsystematic-searching) (soft), [Critical review of an article](#gescritique), [Evidence-based practice](#dhlevidence-based-practice)

### Business & Economics

This domain covers the literacies of business, management and economics programmes, which together attract large numbers of international students on foundation, UG and PGT courses. Business schools assess many simulated professional genres (case analyses, reports, memos, pitches) that are oriented to decision-makers, while economics builds arguments from models, assumptions and conditional reasoning. Nathan (2013) and Zhu (2004) show that these assignment genres differ from the academic essay in purpose, structure and reader, so EAP teaching should analyse the assignment briefs and exemplars of the specific school with subject staff.

<a id="dbebusiness-case-analysis"></a>
#### Business case analysis

`dbe.business-case-analysis` · GENRE · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write a business case analysis or case report: identify the central problem in an organisational case, analyse its causes with appropriate frameworks and the data in the case, generate and evaluate options against explicit criteria, and make justified, feasible recommendations for a named decision-maker. The genre is problem- and decision-oriented rather than thesis-led and is among the most common assessments in business schools.

Prerequisites: [Case study analysis](#grpcase-study), [Data commentary](#datdata-commentary) (soft), [Applying management frameworks critically](#dbecritical-frameworks)

<a id="dbeeconomic-reasoning"></a>
#### Language of economic reasoning

`dbe.economic-reasoning` · LINGUISTIC · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Control the language economists use to reason with models: stating assumptions, holding other factors constant (ceteris paribus), conditional and counterfactual predictions, comparative statics (what happens to equilibrium when one variable changes), marginal reasoning, and the distinction between positive and normative statements. It also includes describing diagrams precisely (a shift of a curve versus a movement along it) and distinguishing causal from correlational claims in empirical economics.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals), [Expressing cause and effect](#funcause-effect), [Correlation and causation](#crtcorrelation-causation) (soft), [Reading graphs and charts](#datreading-graphs) (soft)

<a id="dbeprofessional-genres"></a>
#### Professional business genres

`dbe.professional-genres` · GENRE · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write and present the simulated professional genres business schools assess: memos, briefing notes, management reports and pitches. These genres put the main point or recommendation first, are organised for a busy decision-maker, use informative headings and selective bullet points, and adjust tone and directness to the relationship between writer and reader. Their conventions differ from the academic essay and can vary between schools and industries.

Prerequisites: [Purpose and audience analysis](#prspurpose-audience) (soft), [Business report](#grpbusiness-report), [Politeness and face in academic interaction](#prgpoliteness) (soft)

<a id="dbecritical-frameworks"></a>
#### Applying management frameworks critically

`dbe.critical-frameworks` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Use management frameworks such as SWOT, PESTLE, Porter's Five Forces and the value chain as analytical tools: choose a framework that fits the question, support each entry with case evidence, prioritise factors by significance, draw out implications for the organisation, and acknowledge the framework's limitations. Markers commonly criticise frameworks presented as unprioritised lists without analysis or implications.

Prerequisites: [Evaluating theories and models](#crtevaluating-theories) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory)

### Law

This domain covers the literacies of studying law in English, with a primary focus on common-law systems such as England and Wales: the register and terms of art of legal English, reading and briefing cases, reading legislation, answering problem questions and writing legal essays. Legal knowledge is constructed through authoritative texts (judgments and statutes) whose language is itself the object of analysis, so reading precision and the handling of authority are central (Bhatia, 1993; Twining & Miers, 2010). Many international law students, especially on LLM programmes, have trained in civil-law or mixed systems, which makes making the common-law conventions explicit particularly important.

<a id="dlwlegal-language"></a>
#### Legal language and register

`dlw.legal-language` · CONCEPTUAL · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Recognise the distinctive features of legal English and why they exist: terms of art with fixed legal meanings (consideration, negligence, estoppel), everyday words with specialised legal senses (party, action, hold, find), Latin and Law French expressions, binomials such as null and void, deontic modals, long sentences with embedded qualifications, and the plain-language movement's critique of archaic drafting. Legal terms are tied to a particular legal system, so their meaning cannot be assumed from another jurisdiction or from a bilingual dictionary.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology), [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy), [Academic abbreviations and Latin expressions](#vocabbreviations-latin) (soft), [Register and situational variation](#regregister-awareness) (soft)

<a id="dlwcase-reading"></a>
#### Reading and briefing cases

`dlw.case-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Read law reports strategically and brief them: use the citation, court and headnote to orient (knowing that the headnote is an editor's summary, not authority), then identify the material facts, procedural history, legal issues, the parties' arguments, the decision, the ratio decidendi and any obiter dicta, and note concurring or dissenting judgments. Case reading is the foundation of common-law study because legal rules are extracted from, and argued about through, judgments.

Prerequisites: [Recognising text organisation](#rdstext-organisation) (soft), [Identifying arguments in texts](#rdcargument-identification), [Making notes from reading](#ntknotes-from-reading) (soft), [Legal language and register](#dlwlegal-language)

<a id="dlwproblem-questions"></a>
#### Legal problem questions

`dlw.problem-questions` · GENRE · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Answer hypothetical fact scenarios by advising a party: identify every legal issue the facts raise, state the relevant rule with authority, apply it to the specific facts (including by analogy with and distinction from decided cases), consider alternative interpretations where facts are uncertain, and reach a reasoned, appropriately hedged conclusion. IRAC (Issue, Rule, Application, Conclusion) and variants such as CLEO are widely taught scaffolds, but lecturers differ on them and critics note that applied mechanically they encourage rule-stating without application.

Prerequisites: [Calibrating claims to evidence](#staclaim-calibration) (soft), [Applying theory to cases](#crtapplying-theory), [Reading and briefing cases](#dlwcase-reading), [Reading legislation](#dlwstatutes) (soft)

<a id="dlwstatutes"></a>
#### Reading legislation

`dlw.statutes` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Navigate and interpret legislation: use its structure (long and short title, parts, sections, subsections, paragraphs, schedules, interpretation and commencement provisions) to find relevant provisions, follow cross-references and definitions (including the difference between 'means' and 'includes'), parse qualifications such as subject to, notwithstanding and unless, and recognise the main approaches courts take to interpretation (literal, golden, mischief and purposive). Statutory text is drafted for precision and comprehensiveness, so reading it requires slow, structural parsing.

Prerequisites: [Conditionals and hypothetical reasoning](#grmconditionals) (soft), [Parsing complex academic sentences](#rdpparsing-complex-sentences), [Legal language and register](#dlwlegal-language)

<a id="dlwlegal-essays"></a>
#### Legal essays

`dlw.legal-essays` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write essays that argue a position about the law or legal policy: explain the current law accurately from primary authority, analyse its coherence and development, evaluate it from doctrinal, theoretical, socio-legal or law-reform perspectives using academic commentary and official reports, and sustain a thesis in answer to the question. Legal essays rely on a hierarchy of authority, use reporting verbs with legal meanings (a court holds, a judge observes, counsel submits) and are usually referenced with footnotes in OSCOLA.

Prerequisites: [Reporting verbs](#srcreporting-verbs) (soft), [Note-based citation](#citnote-based), [Argumentative essay](#gesargumentative-essay), [Reading and briefing cases](#dlwcase-reading)

### Social Sciences & Education

This domain covers the literacies of sociology, politics, social policy, international relations, psychology's more interpretive traditions, education and related fields. These are 'soft' disciplines in Becher and Trowler's terms, where key concepts are contested, several research paradigms coexist and writers are expected to justify their conceptual and methodological choices explicitly. The topics address working with contested concepts, qualitative research writing, policy analysis and methodology justification, which recur from UG coursework to doctoral theses. Because expectations vary between fields and between supervisors, analysis of local exemplars with subject staff is essential.

<a id="dssoperationalising-concepts"></a>
#### Working with concepts

`dss.operationalising-concepts` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Handle contested social concepts (for example class, identity, power, inclusion, globalisation) explicitly: show that a concept is defined differently in different theoretical traditions, state and justify the definition adopted, and, in empirical work, operationalise it as indicators, measures or coding categories, or treat it as a sensitising concept in qualitative work. Markers in the social sciences reward conceptual precision and penalise treating contested terms as self-evident.

Prerequisites: [Sub-technical vocabulary and polysemy](#vocsubtechnical-polysemy) (soft), [Defining terms](#fundefining), [Writing relationships between sources](#synrelating-sources) (soft)

<a id="dssqualitative-writing"></a>
#### Qualitative research writing

`dss.qualitative-writing` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Produce the texts of qualitative social research: field notes that separate observational description from reflective and analytic comment; accounts of thematic or other qualitative analysis that make analytic decisions transparent; and findings sections that organise themes around interpretive claims, select and frame participant quotations, and interpret them rather than letting them speak for themselves. Qualitative writing positions the researcher reflexively and makes claims whose scope matches the data.

Prerequisites: [Reflexivity and positionality](#crtreflexivity) (soft), [Reporting qualitative findings](#resqualitative-reporting), [Justifying methodology](#dssmethodology-justification) (soft)

<a id="dsspolicy-analysis"></a>
#### Policy analysis

`dss.policy-analysis` · GENRE · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write academic analyses of public or educational policy: define and critically examine how a policy problem is framed, identify stakeholders and interests, assess the evidence base, compare policy options against explicit criteria such as effectiveness, equity, cost and feasibility, and reach a justified recommendation or critical conclusion. Policy analysis ranges from technical option appraisal to critical approaches that ask how problems are represented, and assignments may expect either.

Prerequisites: [Problem, solution and evaluation](#funproblem-solution), [Identifying assumptions and values](#rdcassumptions), [Types and strength of evidence](#crtevidence-types) (soft), [Considering multiple perspectives](#crtmultiple-perspectives) (soft)

<a id="dssmethodology-justification"></a>
#### Justifying methodology

`dss.methodology-justification` · PROCEDURAL · B2+–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Justify research design in writing: state the philosophical worldview or paradigm informing the study (for example post-positivist, constructivist, transformative or pragmatist), explain how the chosen approach and design follow from the research questions, justify sampling, data collection and analysis with reference to methodological literature, acknowledge trade-offs and ethical considerations, and distinguish methodology (the rationale) from methods (the procedures). Methodology chapters are where examiners judge coherence between questions, design and claims.

Prerequisites: [Beliefs about knowledge](#crtepistemic-beliefs) (soft), [Methods sections](#resmethods), [Research questions and hypotheses](#resresearch-questions)

### Humanities

This domain covers the literacies of literary studies, history, philosophy, religious studies, classics and related fields, where knowledge is built through interpretation and argument about texts, sources and ideas rather than through accumulating findings. Becher and Trowler characterise these 'soft-pure' fields as reiterative and interpretive, and Hyland (2004) shows that their writing typically has more explicit authorial presence and evaluative engagement with sources than the sciences. The topics cover close reading, working with primary sources, interpretive argument and philosophical writing, all of which reward detailed engagement with language and a sustained individual voice.

<a id="dhuclose-reading"></a>
#### Close reading and textual analysis

`dhu.close-reading` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Analyse how a passage creates meaning through its language and form, noticing patterns of word choice, imagery, syntax, sound, structure, point of view and genre, and moving from observation to interpretation by explaining what each feature does and why it matters. Close reading underpins literary studies and is also used in history, philosophy and cultural studies; a multilingual reader's sensitivity to language is a resource for it.

Prerequisites: [Lexical precision and near-synonyms](#vocprecision-near-synonyms) (soft), [Making inferences](#rdsinference), [Purposeful annotation](#rdsannotation) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation)

<a id="dhuprimary-sources"></a>
#### Working with primary sources

`dhu.primary-sources` · PROCEDURAL · B2–C1 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Interpret primary sources such as documents, letters, images, objects and archival records by sourcing them (who produced them, when, why and for whom), contextualising them in their period, corroborating them against other sources, reading them closely and noting their silences. Write about them with appropriate caution and cite them in the conventions of the discipline, including archival locations and translations. Whether a source counts as primary depends on the research question.

Prerequisites: [Recognising bias and perspective](#rdcbias-perspective), [Types of sources](#infsource-types), [Note-based citation](#citnote-based) (soft), [Close reading and textual analysis](#dhuclose-reading) (soft)

<a id="dhuinterpretive-argument"></a>
#### Interpretive argument

`dhu.interpretive-argument` · GENRE · B2–C2 · pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write essays that argue for an interpretation of texts, sources or events: pose an arguable interpretive thesis, build it through analysed textual or documentary evidence, position it in relation to existing critical or historiographical readings, address alternative readings and sustain an evaluative voice. Disciplinary conventions differ, for example the present tense for discussing literary works and the movement in history from recounting events to explaining and arguing about them.

Prerequisites: [Integrating quotations](#srcintegrating-quotations), [Argumentative essay](#gesargumentative-essay), [Close reading and textual analysis](#dhuclose-reading)

<a id="dhuphilosophical-writing"></a>
#### Philosophical writing

`dhu.philosophical-writing` · GENRE · B2+–C2 · in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt, pgr · ESAP

Write philosophy essays that reconstruct an argument charitably in premise–conclusion form, evaluate whether it is valid and whether its premises are true, raise objections (including counterexamples and thought experiments), consider replies, and defend a position with clear distinctions and definitions. Philosophical prose values clarity, precision and explicit signposting over rhetorical flourish, and commonly uses the first person to mark the writer's argumentative moves.

Prerequisites: [Reading theoretical and conceptual texts](#rdctheoretical-texts) (soft), [Deductive, inductive and abductive reasoning](#crtreasoning-types), [Rebuttal and concession](#argrebuttal-concession), [Argument mapping](#argargument-mapping) (soft)

### Creative Arts & Design

This domain covers the literacies of fine art, design (graphic, product, fashion, interior), architecture and related practice-based programmes, where much learning takes place in the studio and assessment centres on work produced, presented and explained. Language does specific jobs here: describing and interpreting visual work, presenting and defending work in critiques, and writing statements and rationales that make practice legible to tutors, audiences and juries. Schön's (1983) account of design as a reflective conversation with materials and Kress and van Leeuwen's (2006) visual grammar give EAP teachers ways to describe these practices, but the tacit vocabulary of a studio is local, so close collaboration with studio tutors and observation of crits is recommended.

<a id="dadstudio-critique"></a>
#### Studio critiques

`dad.studio-critique` · SOCIOPRAGMATIC · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Take part in studio critiques (crits) in their various forms, from one-to-one desk crits to group crits and formal reviews with a panel: present work concisely by explaining intentions, process and key decisions; listen to and clarify critique; respond to challenges by explaining or reconsidering choices without defensiveness; and give peers specific, constructive critique. Crits are a central, often assessed, speech event in art and design education with conventions and a vocabulary that are rarely made explicit.

Prerequisites: [Agreeing and disagreeing](#semagreeing-disagreeing) (soft), [Handling questions](#prshandling-questions), [Feedback literacy](#fbkfeedback-literacy) (soft), [Visual analysis](#dadvisual-analysis)

<a id="dadartist-statement"></a>
#### Artist and design statements

`dad.artist-statement` · GENRE · B2–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Write short statements, typically 50 to 300 words, that explain the concept, materials and process, and context of a work or body of practice for a specific purpose such as an exhibition label, degree show, portfolio or application. Effective statements are concrete and specific, usually written in the present tense and the first person, and connect the work to influences or ideas without inflated or unexplained theoretical language.

Prerequisites: [Writing and speaking for non-specialist audiences](#regnon-specialist-audiences) (soft), [Concision](#senconcision) (soft), [Self-mention and authorial roles](#staself-mention) (soft), [Visual analysis](#dadvisual-analysis)

<a id="daddesign-rationale"></a>
#### Design rationale and process documentation

`dad.design-rationale` · GENRE · B2–C1 · foundation, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Document and justify the design process in sketchbooks, process journals, annotated boards and written rationales: record research, ideation, development, testing and refinement; explain each significant decision with reference to the brief, users, constraints and precedents; show alternatives considered and why they were rejected; and evaluate the outcome against the brief. Rationales make the designer's reasoning visible to assessors and are a major component of many practice-based assessments.

Prerequisites: [Explaining and justifying](#funexplaining), [Evaluating strengths, weaknesses and significance](#funevaluating) (soft), [Reflective writing](#gesreflective-writing) (soft), [Visual analysis](#dadvisual-analysis) (soft)

<a id="dadvisual-analysis"></a>
#### Visual analysis

`dad.visual-analysis` · PROCEDURAL · B1+–C1 · foundation, pre-sessional, in-sessional-ug, in-sessional-pgt · ESAP

Describe, analyse and interpret visual works such as artworks, designs, photographs, buildings and adverts using disciplinary vocabulary: formal elements (composition, line, colour, tone, texture, scale, space, medium), and social semiotic concepts such as salience, framing, information value and gaze. Move from what is visible to what it means in context, distinguishing observation from inference and hedging interpretations appropriately. Visual analysis underpins crits, statements, contextual studies essays and design rationales.

Prerequisites: [Technical and discipline-specific terminology](#voctechnical-terminology) (soft), [Describing structures and systems](#fundescribing-structure) (soft), [Description, analysis and evaluation](#crtdescription-analysis-evaluation), [Multimodal literacy](#mmdmultimodal-literacy)
